DC circuit laws, analysis methods & network theoremsFreq: 5/50/14
Kirchhoff's Current & Voltage Laws (KCL & KVL) LAW

Kirchhoff's Current Law (KCL — Conservation of Charge): The algebraic sum of all currents entering (or leaving) any node or closed Gaussian surface at any instant is zero.
Kirchhoff's Voltage Law (KVL — Conservation of Energy): The algebraic sum of all branch voltages around any closed loop (mesh) at any instant is zero.

$$\sum_{k=1}^{N} i_k(t) = 0 \quad(\text{at any node}), \qquad \sum_{m=1}^{M} v_m(t) = 0 \quad(\text{around any closed loop})$$
Conditions: Lumped-parameter circuit (circuit dimensions $\ll$ operating wavelength $\lambda$); passive sign convention ($p = +vi$ when current enters the $+$ voltage terminal).
Exam Application: Use KCL at non-reference nodes (Nodal Analysis) and KVL around independent meshes (Mesh Analysis). Tested in 2014–15 Q.1b, 2020–21 Q.2b, 2021–22 Q.5b, 2022–23 Q.5a.
Proof & derivation steps
  1. KCL follows from conservation of charge $q$: since a node cannot store net charge, $\sum i_k = dq_{\text{node}}/dt = 0$.
  2. KVL follows from electrostatic potential energy conservation: moving a test charge $dq$ around a closed path requires net work $\oint \mathbf{E}\cdot d\mathbf{l} = 0 \implies \sum v_k = 0$.
Superposition Theorem THEOREM

Linear Circuit: A circuit composed entirely of linear elements (satisfying homogeneity $f(kx)=kf(x)$ and additivity $f(x_1+x_2)=f(x_1)+f(x_2)$) and linear sources.
Superposition Theorem: In any linear bilateral network containing multiple independent sources, the voltage across or current through any branch equals the algebraic sum of the individual responses caused by each independent source acting alone, with all other independent voltage sources replaced by short circuits ($V=0$) and all other independent current sources replaced by open circuits ($I=0$).

$$v(t) = \sum_{k=1}^{M} v_k(t),\qquad i(t) = \sum_{k=1}^{M} i_k(t);\qquad P = i^2 R = \Big(\sum_{k=1}^M i_k\Big)^2 R \neq \sum_{k=1}^{M} P_k$$
Conditions: Network must be linear; dependent (controlled) sources must NEVER be deactivated—they remain active in every sub-circuit.
Exam Application: Directly applicable to voltage and current, NOT power (since $P = i^2 R$ is non-linear), unless sources operate at different frequencies in steady-state AC.
Proof & derivation steps
  1. Select one independent source; deactivate all other independent sources (short $V$-sources, open $I$-sources). Never deactivate dependent sources.
  2. Solve for the desired branch response $v_k$ or $i_k$ in each sub-circuit.
  3. Sum algebraically: $v = \sum v_k$, $i = \sum i_k$. Compute total power only after summing total current or voltage: $P = (\sum i_k)^2 R \neq \sum i_k^2 R$.
Thévenin's & Norton's Theorems THEOREM

Thévenin's Theorem: Any linear two-terminal network can be replaced at terminals $a$–$b$ by an equivalent voltage source $V_{th} = V_{oc}$ in series with an equivalent resistance $R_{th}$ (or impedance $Z_{th}$).
Norton's Theorem: The same network can be replaced by an equivalent current source $I_N = I_{sc}$ in parallel with $R_N = R_{th}$.

$$V_{th} = V_{oc},\qquad I_N = I_{sc},\qquad R_{th} = R_N = \frac{V_{oc}}{I_{sc}} = \left.\frac{v_{\text{test}}}{i_{\text{test}}}\right|_{\text{indep. sources}=0}$$
Conditions: The external load across $a$–$b$ may be linear or non-linear, but the network being replaced must be linear and have no magnetic/controlled coupling to the external load.
Exam Application: If the circuit contains only dependent sources ($V_{oc}=0, I_{sc}=0$), apply a $1\text{ V}$ or $1\text{ A}$ test source at $a$–$b$ to find $R_{th} = v_{\text{test}}/i_{\text{test}}$ (note $R_{th}$ can be negative when dependent sources supply power, as in BUET 2021–22 Q.3a).
Proof & derivation steps
  1. By linearity of the two-terminal network, terminal voltage $V$ and terminal current $I$ (leaving terminal $a$) satisfy a linear relation $V = A - B I$.
  2. Open-circuit condition ($I = 0$) gives $A = V_{oc} = V_{th}$.
  3. Deactivating independent sources sets $V_{oc} = 0$, leaving $V = -B I$, so $B = R_{th}$. Setting $V = 0$ (short circuit) gives $I_{sc} = V_{th}/R_{th} = I_N$.
Maximum Power Transfer Theorem (DC & AC) THEOREM

DC Case: A resistive load $R_L$ connected to a DC network receives maximum power when $R_L = R_{th}$ (for $R_{th}>0$).
AC Case (Complex Load): In sinusoidal steady state, a complex load $Z_L = R_L + jX_L$ receives maximum average power when $Z_L = Z_{th}^* = R_{th} - jX_{th}$ (complex conjugate matching).
AC Case (Purely Resistive Load): If $X_L = 0$ is fixed, maximum power occurs when $R_L = |Z_{th}| = \sqrt{R_{th}^2 + X_{th}^2}$.

$$R_L = R_{th} \implies P_{\max,\text{DC}} = \frac{V_{th}^2}{4R_{th}};\qquad Z_L = Z_{th}^* \implies P_{\max,\text{AC}} = \frac{|V_{th,\text{rms}}|^2}{4R_{th}} = \frac{|V_{th,\text{peak}}|^2}{8R_{th}}$$
Conditions: Source network $(V_{th}, R_{th})$ is fixed and $R_L$ is the variable parameter ($R_{th} > 0$). At maximum power transfer, load voltage is $V_L = V_{th}/2$ and efficiency is $\eta = 50\%$.
Exam Application: In BUET exams (e.g. 2015–16 Q.2c, 2021–22 Q.8a), always check whether phasor sources are given in peak amplitude $V_m$ (use $|V_{th}|^2 / 8R_{th}$) or RMS (use $|V_{th,\text{rms}}|^2 / 4R_{th}$).
Proof & derivation steps
  1. Write load power: $P_L = I^2 R_L = \dfrac{V_{th}^2 R_L}{(R_{th} + R_L)^2}$.
  2. Differentiate with respect to $R_L$: $\dfrac{dP_L}{dR_L} = V_{th}^2 \dfrac{(R_{th}+R_L)^2 - 2R_L(R_{th}+R_L)}{(R_{th}+R_L)^4} = V_{th}^2 \dfrac{R_{th}-R_L}{(R_{th}+R_L)^3} = 0 \implies R_L = R_{th}$.
  3. Verify $d^2P_L/dR_L^2 |_{R_L=R_{th}} = -V_{th}^2 / (8R_{th}^4) < 0$, confirming a maximum.
  4. Substitute $R_L = R_{th}$: $P_{\max} = V_{th}^2 / (4R_{th})$ and efficiency $\eta = R_L/(R_{th}+R_L) = 50\%$.
Millman's, Reciprocity & Tellegen's Theorems THEOREM

Millman's Theorem: Parallel voltage branches $(V_k, R_k)$ combine into a single equivalent voltage source $V_{\text{eq}} = \frac{\sum V_k G_k}{\sum G_k}$ in series with $R_{\text{eq}} = \frac{1}{\sum G_k}$ where $G_k = 1/R_k$.
Reciprocity Theorem: In any linear, bilateral, single-source network (with no dependent sources), the ratio of excitation voltage in branch $A$ to response current in branch $B$ remains unchanged when the positions of excitation and response are interchanged.
Tellegen's Theorem: For any lumped network of $B$ branches satisfying KCL and KVL, the algebraic sum of instantaneous powers across all branches is identically zero: $\sum_{k=1}^{B} v_k i_k = 0$ ($\sum P_{\text{supplied}} = \sum P_{\text{absorbed}}$).

$$V_{\text{eq}} = \frac{\sum_{k=1}^n V_k / R_k}{\sum_{k=1}^n 1 / R_k},\quad R_{\text{eq}} = \frac{1}{\sum_{k=1}^n 1 / R_k};\qquad \left.\frac{I_B}{V_A}\right|_{\text{source in }A} = \left.\frac{I_A}{V_B}\right|_{\text{source in }B};\qquad \sum_{k=1}^{B} v_k i_k = 0$$
Conditions: Reciprocity fails if dependent sources or non-bilateral elements (diodes) are present. Tellegen's theorem depends only on KCL and KVL (topological conservation of power) and holds for both linear and non-linear elements.
Exam Application: Millman's theorem reduces parallel 3-branch battery/generator buses to a single 1-line calculation. Millman's and Reciprocity theorems were directly asked in BUET EEE 101 2024–25 Q.6c & Q.7c.
Ideal vs Practical Sources & Static vs Dynamic Resistance DEFINITION

Ideal vs Practical Voltage/Current Sources: An ideal voltage source maintains a prescribed terminal voltage $v_s(t)$ independent of the current drawn ($R_{\text{int}} = 0$); a practical (actual) voltage source has an internal series resistance $R_s$ so terminal voltage drops as $v = v_s - i R_s$. An ideal current source supplies a fixed current $i_s(t)$ regardless of terminal voltage ($R_{\text{int}} = \infty$); a practical current source has a finite parallel internal resistance $R_p$.
Static vs Dynamic Resistance: For any element with $v$–$i$ characteristic $v = f(i)$, the static (DC) resistance at operating point $(V_Q, I_Q)$ is $R_{\text{static}} = V_Q / I_Q$, whereas the dynamic (incremental / AC) resistance is the local slope $r_d = dv/di|_{Q}$.

$$v_{\text{term}} = V_s - i R_s,\quad i_{\text{term}} = I_s - \frac{v}{R_p};\qquad R_{\text{static}} = \frac{V_Q}{I_Q},\qquad r_d = \left.\frac{dv}{di}\right|_{I_Q}$$
Conditions: For a linear resistor $v = iR$, $R_{\text{static}} = r_d = R$. For a semiconductor diode $i = I_s(e^{v/V_T}-1)$, $R_{\text{static}} = V_Q/I_Q$ while $r_d \approx V_T/I_Q$.
Exam Application: Directly asked in BUET EEE 101 2018–19 Q.2(c) ("Distinguish between ideal and actual voltage and current sources") and Q.3(c) ("Define static and dynamic resistances. Give suitable examples").
Wye–Delta (Y–Δ) Transformation & Supernode / Supermesh Definitions DEFINITION

Wye–Delta ($\text{Y}$–$\Delta$) Transformation: Three resistors connected in a 3-terminal star/wye ($\text{Y}$) or tee ($\text{T}$) network ($R_1, R_2, R_3$) are externally equivalent at their three terminals to a delta ($\Delta$) or pi ($\Pi$) network ($R_a, R_b, R_c$).
Supernode: Formed in nodal analysis by enclosing a voltage source (independent or dependent) connected between two non-reference nodes and any elements in parallel with it inside a closed surface, yielding one KCL equation for the surface and one KVL constraint $v_+ - v_- = V_s$.
Supermesh: Formed in mesh analysis when two adjacent meshes share a current source (independent or dependent) by deleting the shared branch to write a single KVL equation around the combined perimeter and one KCL constraint $i_b - i_a = I_s$.

$$R_1 = \frac{R_b R_c}{R_a+R_b+R_c},\qquad R_a = \frac{R_1 R_2 + R_2 R_3 + R_3 R_1}{R_1};\qquad \text{Balanced: }R_\Delta = 3R_Y$$
Conditions: Uses of $\text{Y}$–$\Delta$ transformation: (1) simplifying unbalanced Wheatstone bridge networks where no resistors are in series or parallel, (2) converting three-phase $\Delta$-connected loads to $\text{Y}$ equivalents, and (3) reducing symmetric ladder/bridge attenuators.
Exam Application: Directly asked in BUET EEE 101 2015–16 Q.1(c), 2015–16 Q.4(c) ("Define Supermesh and Supernode"), and 2024–25 Q.8(c) ("Write some uses of wye-delta transformation").
Electric Charge, Current, Voltage, Power & Energy Interrelations DEFINITION

Electric Charge ($q$): Fundamental quantized property of matter measured in Coulombs ($\text{C}$), where $e = -1.602\times 10^{-19}\text{ C}$ ($1\text{ C} = 6.24\times 10^{18}\text{ electrons}$).
Electric Current ($i$): Time rate of charge transfer $i(t) \triangleq dq/dt$ ($1\text{ A} = 1\text{ C/s}$), so $q(t) = \int_{t_0}^t i(\tau)\,d\tau + q(t_0)$.
Voltage ($v_{ab}$): Work required to move a unit charge from $b$ to $a$: $v_{ab} \triangleq dw/dq$ ($1\text{ V} = 1\text{ J/C}$).
Power ($p$) & Energy ($w$): Rate of energy transfer $p(t) \triangleq dw/dt$ ($1\text{ W} = 1\text{ J/s}$). Multiplying by the chain rule yields $p(t) = v(t)i(t)$ and $w(t_1,t_2) = \int_{t_1}^{t_2} p(\tau)\,d\tau$.

$$i(t) \triangleq \frac{dq}{dt},\quad v_{ab} \triangleq \frac{dw}{dq},\quad p(t) \triangleq \frac{dw}{dt} = \frac{dw}{dq}\cdot\frac{dq}{dt} = v(t)i(t),\quad w(t) = \int_{t_0}^{t} v(\tau)i(\tau)\,d\tau$$
Conditions: Passive Sign Convention (PSC): When current $i$ enters the $+$ terminal, $p = +vi > 0$ means power is absorbed (load); $p < 0$ means power is extracted/supplied.
Exam Application: Power extrema require setting $dp(t)/dt = 0$. Tested in BUET EEE 101 2024–25 Q.5(a).
Worked Exam Problem (BUET 2024–25 Q.5a — Power Extrema & Net Energy)
  1. Problem: Given $v(t) = t(1 - 0.025t)\text{ V}$ and $i(t) = (4 - 0.2t)\text{ A}$ (entering the $+$ terminal of a device for $0 < t < 40\text{ s}$), find: (i) maximum power delivered to the device, (ii) maximum power extracted from the device, and (iii) net energy transferred at $t = 30\text{ s}$.
  2. Instantaneous Power $p(t)$: Under PSC, $p(t) = v(t)i(t) = (t - 0.025t^2)(4 - 0.2t) = 4t - 0.3t^2 + 0.005t^3\text{ W}$.
  3. Critical Points ($dp/dt = 0$): $\dfrac{dp}{dt} = 4 - 0.6t + 0.015t^2 = 0 \implies 3t^2 - 120t + 800 = 0 \implies t = 20 \mp \dfrac{20}{\sqrt{3}}\text{ s}$. Thus $t_1 = 20 - \dfrac{20}{\sqrt{3}} \approx 8.453\text{ s}$ and $t_2 = 20 + \dfrac{20}{\sqrt{3}} \approx 31.547\text{ s}$.
  4. (i) Max Power Delivered ($p > 0$): At $t_1 \approx 8.453\text{ s}$, $p''(t_1) = -0.6 + 0.03(8.453) = -0.346 < 0$ (local max): $p_{\max,\text{del}} = p(t_1) = \dfrac{160}{9\sqrt{3}} \approx 10.264\text{ W}$.
  5. (ii) Max Power Extracted ($p < 0$): At $t_2 \approx 31.547\text{ s}$, $p''(t_2) = +0.346 > 0$ (local min): $p(t_2) = -\dfrac{160}{9\sqrt{3}} \approx -10.264\text{ W}$, so maximum power extracted from the device is $10.264\text{ W}$.
  6. (iii) Net Energy at $t = 30\text{ s}$: $w(30) = \int_0^{30} (4t - 0.3t^2 + 0.005t^3)\,dt = \left[2t^2 - 0.1t^3 + 0.00125t^4\right]_0^{30} = 1800 - 2700 + 1012.5 = 112.5\text{ J}$ (net energy absorbed).
Ideal vs. Practical Sources & Battery Equivalent Model COMPARISON

Ideal vs. Practical Voltage Source: An ideal voltage source ($R_s = 0$) keeps $v_L = v_s$ for all $i_L$; a practical voltage source has a small series resistance $R_s$ so $v_L = v_s - i_L R_s$ ($V_{oc} = v_s$, $I_{sc} = v_s/R_s$).
Ideal vs. Practical Current Source: An ideal current source ($R_p \to \infty$) keeps $i_L = i_s$ for all $v_L$; a practical current source has a finite shunt resistance $R_p$ so $i_L = i_s - v_L/R_p$.
Four Dependent (Controlled) Sources: Diamond-symbol sources governed by another branch variable: VCVS ($\mu v_x$), CCVS ($r_m i_x$, in $\Omega$), VCCS ($g_m v_x$, in $\text{S}$), and CCCS ($\beta i_x$).

$$v_L = v_s - i_L R_s = \frac{R_L}{R_s + R_L}v_s,\qquad i_L = i_s - \frac{v_L}{R_p} = \frac{R_p}{R_p + R_L}i_s,\qquad v_s = i_s R_p,\ R_s = R_p$$
Conditions: Source equivalence holds at external terminals whenever $v_s = i_s R_p$ and $R_s = R_p$.
Exam Application: Use two load-voltage measurements $(V_{L1}, I_{L1})$ and $(V_{L2}, I_{L2})$ to determine a battery's practical voltage (Thévenin) and current (Norton) models. Tested in 2018–19 Q.2(c) and 2019–20 Q.1(b).
Worked Exam Problem (BUET 2019–20 Q.1b — Practical Battery Thévenin & Norton Models)
  1. Problem: A 12 V battery delivers $12.5\text{ V}$ across a $6.25\ \Omega$ car radio and drops to $11.7\text{ V}$ when switched to a $0.65\ \Omega$ headlight bulb. Find the Thévenin and Norton equivalents of the battery.
  2. Load Currents: For the radio ($R_{L1} = 6.25\ \Omega$), $I_{L1} = \dfrac{12.5}{6.25} = 2\text{ A}$. For the headlight ($R_{L2} = 0.65\ \Omega$), $I_{L2} = \dfrac{11.7}{0.65} = 18\text{ A}$.
  3. Terminal Equations ($V_{\text{Th}} = V_L + I_L R_{\text{Th}}$): Write $V_{\text{Th}} = 12.5 + 2 R_{\text{Th}}$ and $V_{\text{Th}} = 11.7 + 18 R_{\text{Th}}$.
  4. Solve for $R_{\text{Th}}, V_{\text{Th}}, I_N$: Subtracting gives $0.8 - 16 R_{\text{Th}} = 0 \implies R_{\text{Th}} = R_N = 0.05\ \Omega$. Back-substituting yields $V_{\text{Th}} = 12.5 + 2(0.05) = 12.6\text{ V}$ and Norton current $I_N = \dfrac{V_{\text{Th}}}{R_{\text{Th}}} = \dfrac{12.6}{0.05} = 252\text{ A}$.
Static vs. Dynamic Resistance & Ammeter Half-Deflection DEFINITION

Static vs. Dynamic Resistance Examples (2018–19 Q.3c):
1. Linear Metallic Resistor: $v = iR \implies R_{\text{static}} = V_Q/I_Q = R$ and $r_d = dv/di|_Q = R$ (identical everywhere).
2. PN-Junction Diode ($i_D = I_s(e^{v_D/(nV_T)}-1)$): At $V_D = 0.7\text{ V}, I_D = 10\text{ mA}$ ($n=1, V_T \approx 26\text{ mV}$), chord resistance is $R_{\text{static}} = 0.7/0.01 = 70\ \Omega$, whereas tangent small-signal resistance is $r_d = dv_D/di_D|_Q \approx V_T/I_D = 2.6\ \Omega$.
3. Tunnel Diode / Gas Tube: Exhibits negative dynamic resistance ($r_d = dv/di < 0$) while $R_{\text{static}} = V_Q/I_Q > 0$ remains positive.

$$R_{\text{static}} = \frac{V_Q}{I_Q},\quad r_d = \left.\frac{dv}{di}\right|_Q \approx \frac{n V_T}{I_D};\qquad R_{\text{sh}} = \frac{I_{\text{fs}}R_m}{I - I_{\text{fs}}},\quad R_{\text{mult}} = \frac{V - I_{\text{fs}}R_m}{I_{\text{fs}}},\quad R_x = R_{\text{added}} - R_m$$
Conditions: Use $R_{\text{static}}$ for DC biasing and $r_d$ for small AC signals superimposed on $Q$.
Exam Application: In current-divider half-deflection problems (2023–24 Q.5b), inserting $R_{\text{added}}$ in series with an ammeter branch of resistance $R_m$ halves the current when $R_x = R_{\text{added}} - R_m$.
Worked Exam Problem (BUET 2023–24 Q.5b — Sadiku Prob. 2.73 Half-Deflection)
  1. Problem: An unknown resistor $R_x$ is in parallel with a constant current source $I$ and an ammeter branch containing a $20\ \Omega$ resistor. When an additional resistor $R = 65\ \Omega$ is switched in series with the $20\ \Omega$ ammeter branch, the ammeter reading drops to half of its previous value. Find $R_x$.
  2. State 1 (Switch across $R$ closed, $R_m = 20\ \Omega$): By the Current Divider Rule, ammeter current is $I_1 = \dfrac{R_x}{R_x + 20}I$.
  3. State 2 (Switch open, $R$ in series with $20\ \Omega$): Total ammeter branch resistance is $20 + R = 20 + 65 = 85\ \Omega$, so $I_2 = \dfrac{R_x}{R_x + 85}I$.
  4. Half-Deflection Condition ($I_2 = \frac{1}{2}I_1$): $\dfrac{R_x}{R_x + 85}I = \dfrac{1}{2}\dfrac{R_x}{R_x + 20}I \implies 2(R_x + 20) = R_x + 85 \implies R_x = 85 - 40 = 45\ \Omega$.
Network Topology, Divider Loading & Full Y–Δ Proof THEOREM

Network Topology Theorem: Any connected graph with $b$ branches, $n$ nodes, and $l$ independent loops obeys $b = l + n - 1$.
Why Unloaded Voltage Division Fails When Loaded (2010–11 Q.2a): In a circuit where a $10\text{ V}$ source feeds $R_1$ and $R_2$ with a $0.7\text{ V}$ transistor base branch tapped at the middle node, $v_1 = \frac{R_2}{R_1+R_2}(10\text{ V})$ is invalid because the tapped branch draws $i_b \neq 0$, violating the series condition $i_{R1} = i_{R2}$.
4 Practical Uses of $\text{Y}$–$\Delta$ Conversion (2015–16 Q.1c, 2024–25 Q.8c): (1) Simplifying unbalanced Wheatstone bridges; (2) Three-phase $\Delta\to\text{Y}$ load analysis; (3) $\text{Y}$–$\Delta$ induction motor starters (cutting starting current to $1/3$); (4) T/$\Pi$ attenuator and filter design.

$$b = l + n - 1,\qquad R_1 = \frac{R_b R_c}{R_a+R_b+R_c},\qquad R_a = \frac{R_1 R_2 + R_2 R_3 + R_3 R_1}{R_1}$$
Conditions: Terminal equivalence between $\text{Y}$ ($R_1$ at node 1, $R_2$ at node 2, $R_3$ at node 3) and $\Delta$ ($R_a$ opposite $R_1$, $R_b$ opposite $R_2$, $R_c$ opposite $R_3$).
Exam Application: Full derivation of $\text{Y}\leftrightarrow\Delta$ was asked in BUET EEE 101 2018–19 Q.3(a) and 2015–16 Q.1(c).
Proof & derivation steps (BUET 2018–19 Q.3a — Complete Y ↔ Δ Derivation)
  1. Equate Open-Circuit Terminal Resistances: Between terminals 1–2 (3 open), 2–3 (1 open), and 3–1 (2 open):
    (1) $R_{12} = R_1 + R_2 = \dfrac{R_c(R_a + R_b)}{R_a + R_b + R_c}$, (2) $R_{23} = R_2 + R_3 = \dfrac{R_a(R_b + R_c)}{R_a + R_b + R_c}$, (3) $R_{31} = R_3 + R_1 = \dfrac{R_b(R_c + R_a)}{R_a + R_b + R_c}$.
  2. Derive $\Delta \to \text{Y}$: Compute $\dfrac{(1) + (3) - (2)}{2}$: $R_1 = \dfrac{R_b R_c}{R_a + R_b + R_c}$. By cyclic symmetry, $R_2 = \dfrac{R_c R_a}{R_a + R_b + R_c}$ and $R_3 = \dfrac{R_a R_b}{R_a + R_b + R_c}$.
  3. Derive $\text{Y} \to \Delta$: Multiply the $\Delta\to\text{Y}$ expressions pairwise and sum: $R_1 R_2 + R_2 R_3 + R_3 R_1 = \dfrac{R_a R_b R_c(R_a + R_b + R_c)}{(R_a + R_b + R_c)^2} = \dfrac{R_a R_b R_c}{R_a + R_b + R_c}$.
  4. Divide by $R_1 = \dfrac{R_b R_c}{R_a+R_b+R_c}$ to obtain $R_a = \dfrac{R_1 R_2 + R_2 R_3 + R_3 R_1}{R_1}$, and similarly $R_b = \dfrac{\sum R_i R_j}{R_2}$, $R_c = \dfrac{\sum R_i R_j}{R_3}$.
Sliding Load Proof, Infinite Ladder & Black-Box Synthesis DERIVATION

Sliding Load Between Two Sources (2018–19 Q.1c): Two sources $v_1$ ($x=0$) and $v_2$ ($x=L$) feed a load $R$ sliding along two parallel rails of length $L$, each having resistance $r\ \Omega/\text{m}$. The left branch resistance is $R_1(x) = 2rx$ and the right branch resistance is $R_2(x) = 2r(L-x)$.

$$v(x) = \frac{v_1 R L + R(v_2 - v_1)x}{R L + 2 r L x - 2 r x^2},\qquad x_{\min} = \frac{L}{v_2 - v_1}\!\left[-v_1 \pm \sqrt{v_1 v_2 - \frac{R}{2 r L}(v_1 - v_2)^2}\right],\qquad R_{\text{in,2-rail}} = (1+\sqrt{3})R$$
Conditions: Factor of $2$ in $2rx$ and $2r(L-x)$ accounts for both the top (supply) and bottom (return) rails.
Exam Application: Directly asked in BUET EEE 101 2018–19 Q.1(c), 2017–18 Q.5(a), Q.5(b), and Q.8(a).
Proof & Worked Exam Problems (2018–19 Q.1c, 2017–18 Q.5a, Q.8a, Q.5b)
  1. Proof of Sliding Load $v(x)$ & $x_{\min}$ (2018–19 Q.1c): Applying KCL at load node $v(x)$: $\dfrac{v - v_1}{2rx} + \dfrac{v - v_2}{2r(L-x)} + \dfrac{v}{R} = 0$. Multiplying by $2rx(L-x)R$ gives $v[RL + 2rLx - 2rx^2] = v_1 RL + R(v_2 - v_1)x$. Setting $\dfrac{dv}{dx} = 0$ yields $2r(v_2 - v_1)x^2 + 4rv_1 L x + R L(v_2 - v_1) - 2rv_1 L^2 = 0$, whose quadratic root is the exact $x_{\min}$ formula above.
  2. Infinite Ladder (2017–18 Q.5a, $R = 1706\ \Omega$): With series $R$ in both top and bottom rails, $R_{\text{in}} = 2R + (R \parallel R_{\text{in}}) = 2R + \dfrac{R R_{\text{in}}}{R + R_{\text{in}}} \implies R_{\text{in}}^2 - 2R R_{\text{in}} - 2R^2 = 0 \implies R_{\text{in}} = (1+\sqrt{3})R \approx 4660.88\ \Omega$ (for single-rail series $R$, $R_{\text{in}} = \frac{1+\sqrt{5}}{2}R \approx 2760.37\ \Omega$).
  3. 3-Terminal Black Box with Five $1\ \Omega$ Resistors (2017–18 Q.8a): Given $R_{AB} = 1.5\ \Omega, R_{BC} = 3\ \Omega, R_{AC} = 2.5\ \Omega$: solving $R_A + R_B = 1.5$, $R_B + R_C = 3$, $R_A + R_C = 2.5$ gives $R_A = 0.5\ \Omega$ (two $1\ \Omega$ in parallel), $R_B = 1.0\ \Omega$ (one $1\ \Omega$), and $R_C = 2.0\ \Omega$ (two $1\ \Omega$ in series) meeting at a central star node.
  4. Cut-Set KCL & KVL (2017–18 Q.5b): Given $V_A - V_B = 6\text{ V}$ across the top $2\ \Omega$ resistor, $I_{AB} = 6/2 = 3\text{ A}$ (left-to-right). A vertical cut-set across the three horizontal branches gives $I_{AB} + 2\text{ A} + I_{CD} = 0 \implies I_{CD} = -5\text{ A}$. Since $R_{CD} = 1\ \Omega$, $V_C - V_D = I_{CD}(1\ \Omega) = -5\text{ V}$.
Mesh Circuit Reconstruction & Two-Network Identification METHOD

Mesh Matrix Decomposition (2015–16 Q.2a): Rewrite $-R_{21}i_1 + R_{22}i_2 - R_{23}i_3 = v_{s2}$ in branch-difference form $R_{21}(i_2 - i_1) + R_{20}i_2 + R_{23}(i_2 - i_3) = v_{s2}$ to read off every shared and unshared resistor.
Coupled Two-Network Identification (2017–18 Q.6c): Replace unknown linear boxes $A$ and $B$ by their Thévenin equivalents $(V_A, R_A)$ and $(V_B, R_B)$ and write KVL for both connection polarities.

$$R_{kk}i_k - \sum_{j\neq k} R_{kj}i_j = v_{s,k};\qquad i_1 = \frac{V_A - V_B}{R_A + R_B},\quad i_2 = \frac{V_A + V_B}{R_A + R_B},\quad v = V_A - i R_A$$
Conditions: Planar network for mesh reconstruction; linear bilateral networks $A$ and $B$.
Exam Application: Directly asked in BUET EEE 101 2015–16 Q.2(a) and 2017–18 Q.6(c).
Worked Exam Problems (BUET 2015–16 Q.2a & 2017–18 Q.6c)
  1. Problem 1 (2015–16 Q.2a — Mesh Reconstruction): Given $i_2 = -10\text{ A}$, $10i_1 - 22i_2 + 10i_3 = 0$, and $i_3 = 12\text{ A}$: substituting $i_2 = -10$ and $i_3 = 12$ gives $10i_1 - 22(-10) + 10(12) = 0 \implies 10i_1 = -340 \implies i_1 = -34\text{ A}$. Rewriting the middle equation as $10(i_1 - i_2) - 12i_2 + 10i_3 = 0$ shows Mesh 2 has a $10\text{ A}$ perimeter current source ($i_2 = -10\text{ A}$), Mesh 3 has a $12\text{ A}$ perimeter current source ($i_3 = 12\text{ A}$), and the center loop shares a $10\ \Omega$ resistor with Mesh 1, a $12\ \Omega$ resistor with Mesh 2, and a $10\ \Omega$ resistor with Mesh 3.
  2. Problem 2 (2017–18 Q.6c — Coupled Networks $A$ & $B$): When $A$ and $B$ are connected $+$ to $+$, $v_1 = 1\text{ V}$ and $i_1 = 0\text{ mA}$. Since $i_1 = 0$, there is no drop across $R_A$ or $R_B$, so $V_A = 1\text{ V}$ and $V_B = 1\text{ V}$.
  3. When box $B$ is inverted ($-$ of $B$ at top), $v_2 = 0.5\text{ V}$ and $i_2 = 0.5\text{ mA}$. For Network $A$: $v_2 = V_A - i_2 R_A \implies 0.5 = 1 - (0.5\text{ mA})R_A \implies R_A = 1\text{ k}\Omega$. For inverted Network $B$: $v_2 = -V_B + i_2 R_B \implies 0.5 = -1 + (0.5\text{ mA})R_B \implies R_B = 3\text{ k}\Omega$.
Fixed-Load Internal Optimization & Millman Max-Power THEOREM

Exam Trap — Variable Internal Resistor vs. Fixed Load (2013–14 Q.4b, 2024–25 Q.7a): If the target load $R_{\text{load}}$ is fixed and an internal series resistor $R \ge 0$ is adjusted, do not set $R = R_{\text{Th}}$! Because $P_{\text{load}} = I_{\text{load}}^2 R_{\text{load}}$ has fixed $R_{\text{load}}$, maximizing $P_{\text{load}}$ requires maximizing $|I_{\text{load}}|$, which occurs at $R = 0\ \Omega$.
AC Purely Resistive Load ($X_L = 0$): When load reactance cannot be varied, choose $R_L = |Z_{\text{Th}}| = \sqrt{R_{\text{Th}}^2 + X_{\text{Th}}^2}$, yielding $P_{\max} = \dfrac{|V_{\text{Th,rms}}|^2}{2(|Z_{\text{Th}}| + R_{\text{Th}})}$.

$$R_{\text{load}}\text{ fixed, }R_{\text{internal}}\ge 0\text{ variable} \implies R_{\text{internal,opt}} = 0\ \Omega;\qquad R_L = |Z_{\text{Th}}| \implies P_{\max,\text{R-only}} = \frac{|V_{\text{Th,rms}}|^2}{2(|Z_{\text{Th}}| + R_{\text{Th}})}$$
Conditions: Non-negative passive resistor $R \ge 0$.
Exam Application: Tested in BUET EEE 101 2013–14 Q.4(b), 2019–20 Q.4(a), and 2024–25 Q.7(a).
Worked Exam Problems (BUET 2024–25 Q.7a & 2019–20 Q.4a)
  1. Problem 1 (2013–14 Q.4b / 2024–25 Q.7a — Sadiku Prob. 4.68): A $12\text{ V}$ source in series with adjustable $R$ lies on the left of a $20\ \Omega$ shunt resistor, while a fixed $10\ \Omega$ load in series with an $8\text{ V}$ source lies on the right. Find $R \ge 0$ for maximum power in the $10\ \Omega$ resistor.
  2. Solution 1: Since $10\ \Omega$ is fixed, $P_{10\Omega} = I_{10\Omega}^2(10)$ is maximized when $I_{10\Omega}$ is maximized. Setting $R = 0\ \Omega$ clamps the parallel node directly to $12\text{ V}$, giving $I_{10\Omega} = \dfrac{12 - 8}{10} = 0.4\text{ A}$ and $P_{\max,10\Omega} = (0.4)^2(10) = 1.6\text{ W}$.
  3. Problem 2 (2019–20 Q.4a — Sadiku Prob. 4.75 via Millman's Theorem): Three parallel branches ($V_1 = 1\text{ V}, V_2 = 2\text{ V}, V_3 = 3\text{ V}$, each with series resistance $R_1 = R_2 = R_3 = R$) feed an adjustable load $R_L$ that absorbs $P_{\max} = 3\text{ mW}$. Find $R$ and $R_L$.
  4. Solution 2: By Millman's theorem, $V_{\text{Th}} = \dfrac{1/R + 2/R + 3/R}{1/R + 1/R + 1/R} = \dfrac{6/R}{3/R} = 2\text{ V}$ and $R_{\text{Th}} = \dfrac{R}{3}$. For maximum power in $R_L$, $R_L = R_{\text{Th}} = \dfrac{R}{3}$ and $P_{\max} = \dfrac{V_{\text{Th}}^2}{4 R_{\text{Th}}} = \dfrac{4}{4(R/3)} = \dfrac{3}{R} = 3\times 10^{-3}\text{ W} \implies R = 1000\ \Omega = 1\text{ k}\Omega$ and $R_L = 333.33\ \Omega$.
Capacitors, inductors, transients & magnetic circuitsFreq: 5/50/12
Continuity of Capacitor Voltage & Inductor Current PRINCIPLE

In the absence of impulse (infinite) currents or voltages, the stored electric energy in a capacitor ($w_C = \frac12 C v_C^2$) and magnetic energy in an inductor ($w_L = \frac12 L i_L^2$) cannot jump instantaneously across a switching instant $t_0$:

$$v_C(t_0^+) = v_C(t_0^-),\qquad i_L(t_0^+) = i_L(t_0^-),\qquad i_C = C\frac{dv_C}{dt},\qquad v_L = L\frac{di_L}{dt}$$
Conditions: Bounded current $i_C(t)$ and bounded voltage $v_L(t)$. Note that $i_C$ and $v_L$ CAN jump instantaneously at $t_0^+$.
Exam Application: At DC steady state ($t = 0^-$ or $t \to \infty$), replace every capacitor by an OPEN circuit ($i_C = 0$) and every inductor by a SHORT circuit ($v_L = 0$). In multi-capacitor or multi-inductor black-box problems (2018–19 Q.8b, 2023–24 Q.1a, 2024–25 Q.1a), trapped energy can remain at $t=\infty$.
Complete Response of First-Order RC & RL Circuits THEOREM

Any voltage or current $x(t)$ in a first-order RC or RL circuit excited by DC sources for $t > t_0$ is the sum of its forced (steady-state) response $x(\infty)$ and its natural (transient) response $[x(t_0^+) - x(\infty)]e^{-(t-t_0)/\tau}$:

$$x(t) = x(\infty) + \big[x(t_0^+) - x(\infty)\big]e^{-(t-t_0)/\tau},\qquad \tau_{RC} = R_{th} C,\qquad \tau_{RL} = \frac{L}{R_{th}}$$
Conditions: Single effective storage element ($C$ or $L$) and constant (DC) excitations for $t > t_0$; $R_{th}$ is the Thévenin resistance seen from the storage element terminals for $t > t_0$.
Exam Application: For sequential switching (switches at $t=0$ and $t=t_1$), evaluate $x(t_1^-)$ from the first interval solution and use it as the initial condition $x(t_1^+)$ for $t \ge t_1$.
Proof & derivation steps
  1. KVL/KCL yields a first-order linear ODE: $\tau \dfrac{dx}{dt} + x = x(\infty)$ for $t > t_0$.
  2. Separate variables: $\dfrac{dx}{x - x(\infty)} = -\dfrac{dt}{\tau}$.
  3. Integrate from $t_0^+$ to $t$: $\ln\!\left|\dfrac{x(t)-x(\infty)}{x(t_0^+)-x(\infty)}\right| = -\dfrac{t-t_0}{\tau} \implies x(t) = x(\infty) + [x(t_0^+)-x(\infty)]e^{-(t-t_0)/\tau}$.
Ampère's Circuital Law & Magnetic Circuit Hopkinson's Law LAW

Ampère's Law: The line integral of magnetic field intensity $\mathbf{H}$ around any closed magnetic path equals the total enclosed magnetomotive force $\mathcal{F} = N I$.
Hopkinson's Law (Magnetic Ohm's Law): Total MMF equals magnetic flux $\Phi$ times reluctance $\mathcal{R}$:

$$\oint \mathbf{H}\cdot d\mathbf{l} = \sum_k H_k l_k = N I = \mathcal{F} = \Phi\,\mathcal{R}_{\text{eq}},\qquad \mathcal{R}_k = \frac{l_k}{\mu_0 \mu_r A_k},\qquad B_k = \frac{\Phi_k}{A_k} = \mu_0\mu_r H_k$$
Conditions: Negligible leakage flux; at an air gap of length $l_g$, $\mu_r = 1$ so $H_g = B_g / \mu_0$ (with $\mu_0 = 4\pi \times 10^{-7}\text{ H/m}$).
Exam Application: When a non-linear $B$–$H$ curve is given for cast steel/sheet steel, do NOT compute $\mathcal{R}$; instead compute $B = \Phi/A$, read $H$ from the graph, and sum $\mathcal{F} = \sum H_k l_k + (B_g/\mu_0)l_g$.
Second-Order Series & Parallel RLC Damping Regimes Theorem THEOREM

For any source-free or DC step-excited second-order RLC circuit with characteristic equation $s^2 + 2\alpha s + \omega_0^2 = 0$, the characteristic roots $s_{1,2} = -\alpha \pm \sqrt{\alpha^2 - \omega_0^2}$ classify the transient response into three damping regimes determined by the Neper frequency $\alpha$ and resonant frequency $\omega_0 = 1/\sqrt{LC}$:

$$\begin{aligned}&\text{Series RLC: }\alpha = \frac{R}{2L},\quad \text{Parallel RLC: }\alpha = \frac{1}{2RC},\quad \omega_0 = \frac{1}{\sqrt{LC}},\quad \zeta = \frac{\alpha}{\omega_0},\quad \omega_d = \sqrt{\omega_0^2 - \alpha^2}\\&(1)\ \text{Overdamped }(\alpha > \omega_0,\ \zeta > 1):\ x(t) = x(\infty) + A_1 e^{s_1 t} + A_2 e^{s_2 t}\\&(2)\ \text{Critically Damped }(\alpha = \omega_0,\ \zeta = 1):\ x(t) = x(\infty) + (A_1 + A_2 t)e^{-\alpha t}\\&(3)\ \text{Underdamped }(\alpha < \omega_0,\ \zeta < 1):\ x(t) = x(\infty) + e^{-\alpha t}\big(B_1\cos\omega_d t + B_2\sin\omega_d t\big)\end{aligned}$$
Conditions: Initial-condition equations at $t = 0^+$: (1) Overdamped ($R > 2\sqrt{L/C}$ series, $R < \frac12\sqrt{L/C}$ parallel): $A_1+A_2 = x(0^+)-x(\infty)$ and $s_1 A_1 + s_2 A_2 = x'(0^+)$; (2) Critically damped ($R = 2\sqrt{L/C}$ series, $R = \frac12\sqrt{L/C}$ parallel): $A_1 = x(0^+)-x(\infty)$ and $A_2 = x'(0^+) + \alpha A_1$; (3) Underdamped ($R < 2\sqrt{L/C}$ series, $R > \frac12\sqrt{L/C}$ parallel): $B_1 = x(0^+)-x(\infty)$ and $B_2 = \frac{x'(0^+) + \alpha B_1}{\omega_d}$.
Exam Application: To find the time $t_p > 0$ of the first peak/extremum (e.g. BUET 2024–25 Q.4b airbag igniter): set $\frac{dx(t)}{dt} = 0$. For underdamped response with $B_1=0$, $t_p = \frac{1}{\omega_d}\tan^{-1}(\omega_d/\alpha)$.
Proof & derivation steps
  1. For a series RLC loop with KVL: $R i + L\dfrac{di}{dt} + \dfrac{1}{C}\int i\,dt = V_s$; differentiating gives $\dfrac{d^2 i}{dt^2} + \dfrac{R}{L}\dfrac{di}{dt} + \dfrac{1}{LC}i = 0$ (so $2\alpha = R/L$).
  2. For a parallel RLC node with KCL: $\dfrac{v}{R} + \dfrac{1}{L}\int v\,dt + C\dfrac{dv}{dt} = I_s$; differentiating gives $\dfrac{d^2 v}{dt^2} + \dfrac{1}{RC}\dfrac{dv}{dt} + \dfrac{1}{LC}v = 0$ (so $2\alpha = 1/(RC)$).
  3. Substitute trial solution $x_n(t) = A e^{st}$ into the homogeneous ODE to obtain $s^2 + 2\alpha s + \omega_0^2 = 0 \implies s_{1,2} = -\alpha \pm \sqrt{\alpha^2 - \omega_0^2}$.
General Second-Order Dynamic Circuits — Complete 6-Step Solution Algorithm THEOREM

When a circuit contains two independent energy-storage elements ($L_1, L_2$, or $C_1, C_2$, or $L$ and $C$) that are neither in simple series nor simple parallel, any branch voltage or current $x(t)$ for $t > 0$ satisfies a linear second-order ODE $\frac{d^2 x}{dt^2} + 2\alpha\frac{dx}{dt} + \omega_0^2 x = K$ and is solved systematically in six steps:

$$\frac{d^2 x}{dt^2} + a_1\frac{dx}{dt} + a_0 x = K \implies s^2 + a_1 s + a_0 = 0,\qquad \alpha = \frac{a_1}{2},\quad \omega_0 = \sqrt{a_0},\quad x(\infty) = \frac{K}{a_0}$$
Conditions: Requires four boundary values for the target variable $x(t)$: $x(0^+)$, $\frac{dx(0^+)}{dt}$, $x(\infty)$, and the characteristic roots $s_1, s_2$.
Exam Application: Tested in BUET EEE 101 2020–21 Q.5b, 2021–22 Q.2b, 2022–23 Q.1a, 2023–24 Q.2b, and 2024–25 Q.4a.
Proof & derivation steps
  1. Step 1 ($t = 0^-$ steady state): Draw the circuit before switching ($t < 0$) with capacitors open and inductors shorted; compute $v_C(0^-)$ and $i_L(0^-)$.
  2. Step 2 ($t = 0^+$ continuity): Set $v_C(0^+) = v_C(0^-)$ and $i_L(0^+) = i_L(0^-)$. Draw the $t = 0^+$ circuit replacing $C$ by a voltage source $v_C(0^+)$ and $L$ by a current source $i_L(0^+)$ to find $i_C(0^+)$, $v_L(0^+)$, and $x(0^+)$.
  3. Step 3 (Initial derivative $x'(0^+)$): Compute $\dfrac{dv_C(0^+)}{dt} = \dfrac{i_C(0^+)}{C}$ and $\dfrac{di_L(0^+)}{dt} = \dfrac{v_L(0^+)}{L}$. Differentiate the KCL/KVL expression for $x(t)$ at $t = 0^+$ to obtain $\dfrac{dx(0^+)}{dt}$.
  4. Step 4 ($t > 0$ differential equation & roots): Write KCL/KVL for the $t > 0$ network using $i_C = C\,dv_C/dt$ and $v_L = L\,di_L/dt$ (or operator $s = d/dt$), eliminate the second state variable to get $(s^2 + 2\alpha s + \omega_0^2)x = K$, and solve $s^2 + 2\alpha s + \omega_0^2 = 0$ for $s_1, s_2$.
  5. Step 5 ($t \to \infty$ forced response): Open capacitors and short inductors in the $t > 0$ circuit to find the DC steady-state value $x(\infty)$.
  6. Step 6 (Substitute initial conditions): Write $x(t) = x(\infty) + x_n(t)$ according to whether $\alpha > \omega_0$, $\alpha = \omega_0$, or $\alpha < \omega_0$, and solve the two linear equations for $A_1, A_2$ (or $B_1, B_2$) from $x(0^+)$ and $x'(0^+)$.
Equivalent Inductance $L_{\text{eq}}$ with Dependent Source METHOD

Finding $L_{\text{eq}}$ in Networks with Dependent Sources: Do not integrate! Instead, apply a test current $i(t)$ at terminals $a$–$b$, differentiate KCL ($\frac{di}{dt} = \frac{di_1}{dt} + \frac{di_2}{dt}$), and solve the KVL equations directly for the ratio $L_{\text{eq}} = \dfrac{v_{ab}}{di/dt}$.

$$v_{ab} = L_{\text{eq}}\frac{di}{dt} \implies L_{\text{eq}} = \frac{v_{ab}}{di/dt} = \frac{53}{8}\text{ H} = 6.625\text{ H}$$
Conditions: Linear inductor network with dependent voltage or current source proportional to $di/dt$ or $i$.
Exam Application: Directly asked in BUET EEE 101 2013–14 Q.6(b), 2023–24 Q.2(a), and 2024–25 Q.2(c) (Sadiku Prob. 6.57).
Worked Exam Problem (BUET 2013–14 Q.6b, 2023–24 Q.2a, 2024–25 Q.2c — Sadiku Prob. 6.57)
  1. Problem: Terminal $a$ feeds a $4\text{ H}$ series inductor Carrying current $i$ into a node that splits into a parallel $3\text{ H}$ inductor (current $i_1$) and a right branch (current $i_2$) containing a dependent voltage source $2\frac{di}{dt}$ ($+$ on top) in series with a $5\text{ H}$ inductor. Find $L_{\text{eq}}$ at terminals $a$–$b$.
  2. Differentiated KCL at the Node: $i = i_1 + i_2 \implies \dfrac{di}{dt} = \dfrac{di_1}{dt} + \dfrac{di_2}{dt} \implies \dfrac{di_2}{dt} = \dfrac{di}{dt} - \dfrac{di_1}{dt}$.
  3. KVL Around the Parallel Loop: Voltage across the $3\text{ H}$ inductor equals the right-branch voltage: $3\dfrac{di_1}{dt} = 2\dfrac{di}{dt} + 5\dfrac{di_2}{dt}$.
  4. Substitute $\frac{di_2}{dt}$: $3\dfrac{di_1}{dt} = 2\dfrac{di}{dt} + 5\!\left(\dfrac{di}{dt} - \dfrac{di_1}{dt}\right) = 7\dfrac{di}{dt} - 5\dfrac{di_1}{dt} \implies 8\dfrac{di_1}{dt} = 7\dfrac{di}{dt} \implies \dfrac{di_1}{dt} = \dfrac{7}{8}\dfrac{di}{dt}$.
  5. Input KVL at Terminals $a$–$b$: $v_{ab} = 4\dfrac{di}{dt} + 3\dfrac{di_1}{dt} = 4\dfrac{di}{dt} + 3\!\left(\dfrac{7}{8}\dfrac{di}{dt}\right) = \dfrac{53}{8}\dfrac{di}{dt} = L_{\text{eq}}\dfrac{di}{dt} \implies L_{\text{eq}} = \dfrac{53}{8}\text{ H} = 6.625\text{ H}$.
Singularity Functions ($u(t), \delta(t), r(t)$) & Waveform Synthesis DEFINITION

Unit Step, Impulse & Ramp: $\delta(t) = \dfrac{du(t)}{dt}$ and $r(t) = \int_{-\infty}^t u(\tau)\,d\tau = t\,u(t)$.
Time-Reversed Step & Gate: $u(-t) = 1 - u(t)$ ($1$ for $t < 0$, $0$ for $t > 0$); a rectangular pulse of height $A$ on $[t_1, t_2]$ is $A[u(t-t_1) - u(t-t_2)]$.
Piecewise Synthesis Rule: At each breakpoint $t_k$, add $(\text{jump})\cdot u(t-t_k)$ plus $(\Delta\text{slope})\cdot r(t-t_k)$ where $\Delta\text{slope} = m_{\text{new}} - m_{\text{old}}$.

$$u(t-t_0) = \begin{cases}0,&t<t_0\\1,&t>t_0\end{cases},\quad \delta(t-t_0) = \frac{du(t-t_0)}{dt},\quad r(t-t_0) = (t-t_0)u(t-t_0),\quad \int_a^b f(t)\delta(t-t_0)\,dt = f(t_0)$$
Conditions: Sifting integral $\int_a^b f(t)\delta(t-t_0)\,dt = f(t_0)$ holds when $a < t_0 < b$ (and equals $0$ if $t_0 \notin [a,b]$).
Exam Application: Directly asked in BUET EEE 101 2014–15 Q.8(a) and 2024–25 Q.2(a)(i, ii).
Worked Exam Problems (BUET 2014–15 Q.8a & 2024–25 Q.2a-i, ii)
  1. Problem 1 (2014–15 Q.8a — Step + Ramp Waveform): A current $i(t)$ jumps to $+2\text{ A}$ at $t=0$, stays flat until $t=1$, ramps linearly from $+2\text{ A}$ at $t=1$ to $-2\text{ A}$ at $t=3$ (slope $-2\text{ A/s}$), stays at $-2\text{ A}$ on $(3,4)$, and jumps back to $0$ at $t=4$.
    Solution: Jump $+2$ at $t=0 \implies +2u(t)$; slope change $0 \to -2$ at $t=1 \implies -2r(t-1)$; slope change $-2 \to 0$ at $t=3 \implies +2r(t-3)$; jump $-2 \to 0$ at $t=4 \implies +2u(t-4)$. Thus $i(t) = 2u(t) - 2r(t-1) + 2r(t-3) + 2u(t-4)\text{ A}$.
  2. Problem 2 (2024–25 Q.2a-i — Sadiku Prob. 7.27 Staircase): $v(t) = 10\text{ V}$ on $(-1,0)$, $30\text{ V}$ on $(0,1)$, $20\text{ V}$ on $(1,2)$, $-10\text{ V}$ on $(2,3)$, and $0\text{ V}$ for $t > 3$. Summing the step jumps at $t = -1, 0, 1, 2, 3$: $v(t) = 10u(t+1) + 20u(t) - 10u(t-1) - 30u(t-2) + 10u(t-3)\text{ V}$.
  3. Problem 3 (2024–25 Q.2a-ii — Sketching $i(t) = u(t+4) - u(-t) - u(t-2) + u(t-4)$): Evaluate interval by interval using $u(-t) = 1$ for $t < 0$ and $0$ for $t > 0$:
    • $t < -4$: $0 - 1 - 0 + 0 = -1\text{ A}$
    • $-4 < t < 0$: $1 - 1 - 0 + 0 = 0\text{ A}$
    • $0 < t < 2$: $1 - 0 - 0 + 0 = +1\text{ A}$
    • $2 < t < 4$: $1 - 0 - 1 + 0 = 0\text{ A}$
    • $t > 4$: $1 - 0 - 1 + 1 = +1\text{ A}$.
Series $RL$ Step-Response Proof & $95\%$ Energy Delivery Law DERIVATION

Series $RL$ Step Response (2016–17 Q.2c): For $t > 0$, KVL gives $L\dfrac{di}{dt} + Ri = V_s$ with $i(0^+) = i(0^-) = I_0$ and $\tau = L/R$.
Fraction $\eta$ of Stored Energy Delivered in Source-Free $RL$ / $RC$ (2022–23 Q.4b-iii): Since $p(t) = i^2(t)R = I_0^2 R e^{-2t/\tau}$, energy delivered by time $t$ is $W(t) = W(\infty)(1 - e^{-2t/\tau})$. For $\eta = 95\%$ ($0.95$), $\dfrac{t}{\tau} = -\dfrac{1}{2}\ln(0.05) = \dfrac{\ln 20}{2} \approx 1.498 \approx 1.5\text{ time constants}$.

$$i(t) = \frac{V_s}{R} + \left(I_0 - \frac{V_s}{R}\right)e^{-t/\tau},\quad \tau = \frac{L}{R};\qquad \frac{W(t)}{W(\infty)} = 1 - e^{-2t/\tau} = \eta \implies \frac{t}{\tau} = -\frac{1}{2}\ln(1 - \eta)$$
Conditions: First-order source-free $RL$ ($\tau = L/R$, $W(\infty) = \frac{1}{2}LI_0^2$) or $RC$ ($\tau = RC$, $W(\infty) = \frac{1}{2}CV_0^2$) discharge.
Exam Application: Directly asked in BUET EEE 101 2016–17 Q.2(c) and 2022–23 Q.4(b)(iii).
Proof & derivation steps (2016–17 Q.2c & 2022–23 Q.4b-iii)
  1. Series $RL$ ODE Separation of Variables (2016–17 Q.2c): Divide $L\dfrac{di}{dt} + Ri = V_s$ by $R$: $\dfrac{L}{R}\dfrac{di}{dt} = \dfrac{V_s}{R} - i \implies \dfrac{di}{i - V_s/R} = -\dfrac{R}{L}\,dt = -\dfrac{dt}{\tau}$. Integrating from $0^+$ to $t$ gives $\ln\!\left(\dfrac{i(t) - V_s/R}{I_0 - V_s/R}\right) = -\dfrac{t}{\tau} \implies i(t) = \dfrac{V_s}{R} + \left(I_0 - \dfrac{V_s}{R}\right)e^{-t/\tau}$.
  2. $95\%$ Energy Delivery Proof (2022–23 Q.4b-iii): In a source-free $RL$ circuit, $i(t) = I_0 e^{-t/\tau}$ and $p_R(t) = i^2(t)R = I_0^2 R e^{-2t/\tau}$.
  3. Integrate power from $0$ to $t$: $W(t) = \int_0^t I_0^2 R e^{-2x/\tau}\,dx = I_0^2 R\left[-\dfrac{\tau}{2}e^{-2x/\tau}\right]_0^t = \dfrac{1}{2}I_0^2 R\tau(1 - e^{-2t/\tau}) = \dfrac{1}{2}LI_0^2(1 - e^{-2t/\tau}) = W(\infty)(1 - e^{-2t/\tau})$.
  4. Set $\dfrac{W(t)}{W(\infty)} = 0.95 \implies 1 - e^{-2t/\tau} = 0.95 \implies e^{-2t/\tau} = 0.05 \implies \dfrac{t}{\tau} = -\dfrac{\ln(0.05)}{2} \approx 1.4979 \approx 1.5\text{ time constants}$.
Second-Order $RLC$ Peak Time ($t_{\text{peak}}$) & Airbag Igniter METHOD

Exact Peak-Time Formulas Across All 3 Damping Regimes ($\frac{dx}{dt} = 0$):
• Overdamped (with $x(0^+)=0 \implies A_2 = -A_1$): $x(t) = A_1(e^{s_1 t} - e^{s_2 t})$ peaks at $t_{\text{peak}} = \dfrac{\ln(s_2/s_1)}{s_1 - s_2}$.
• Critically Damped: $x(t) = (A_1 + A_2 t)e^{-\alpha t}$ peaks at $t_{\text{peak}} = \dfrac{1}{\alpha} - \dfrac{A_1}{A_2}$ ($= \dfrac{1}{\alpha}$ when $A_1 = 0$).
• Underdamped: $x(t) = e^{-\alpha t}(A_1\cos\omega_d t + A_2\sin\omega_d t)$ peaks at $t_{\text{peak}} = \dfrac{1}{\omega_d}\tan^{-1}\!\left(\dfrac{\omega_d A_2 - \alpha A_1}{\omega_d A_1 + \alpha A_2}\right)$.

$$t_{\text{peak,over}} = \frac{\ln(s_2/s_1)}{s_1 - s_2}\ (A_2=-A_1),\qquad t_{\text{peak,crit}} = \frac{1}{\alpha} - \frac{A_1}{A_2},\qquad t_{\text{peak,under}} = \frac{1}{\omega_d}\tan^{-1}\!\left(\frac{\omega_d A_2 - \alpha A_1}{\omega_d A_1 + \alpha A_2}\right)$$
Conditions: Characteristic roots $s_1, s_2 < 0$ in overdamped regime ($s_2 / s_1 > 0$).
Exam Application: Directly asked in BUET EEE 101 2024–25 Q.4(b) (Sadiku Prob. 8.78 — Automobile Airbag Igniter).
Worked Exam Problem (BUET 2024–25 Q.4b — Sadiku Prob. 8.78 Airbag Igniter Peak Time)
  1. Problem: In an automobile airbag igniter, a capacitor $C = \frac{1}{30}\text{ F}$ charged to $12\text{ V}$ is switched at $t = 0$ into a series path with $L = 60\text{ mH} = 0.06\text{ H}$ and igniter resistor $R = 3\ \Omega$. Find the time $t_p$ at which the voltage across the resistor reaches its maximum value.
  2. Damping Parameters & Roots: For the series $RLC$ loop, $\alpha = \dfrac{R}{2L} = \dfrac{3}{2(0.06)} = 25\text{ Np/s}$ and $\omega_0 = \dfrac{1}{\sqrt{LC}} = \dfrac{1}{\sqrt{0.06 \times (1/30)}} = \sqrt{500} \approx 22.3607\text{ rad/s}$.
  3. Since $\alpha = 25 > \omega_0 = 22.3607$, the circuit is overdamped with roots $s_{1,2} = -25 \pm \sqrt{25^2 - 500} = -25 \pm 5\sqrt{5}$, giving $s_1 \approx -13.8197\text{ s}^{-1}$ and $s_2 \approx -36.1803\text{ s}^{-1}$.
  4. Initial Conditions & Peak Time: Since $i(0^+) = i(0^-) = 0$, $v_R(0^+) = R i(0^+) = 0 \implies A_1 + A_2 = 0 \implies A_2 = -A_1$, so $v_R(t) = 3A_1(e^{s_1 t} - e^{s_2 t})$. Setting $\dfrac{dv_R}{dt} = 0$ gives $t_p = \dfrac{\ln(s_2/s_1)}{s_1 - s_2} = \dfrac{\ln(-36.1803 / -13.8197)}{-13.8197 - (-36.1803)} = \dfrac{0.96242}{22.3607} \approx 0.04304\text{ s} = 43.0\text{ ms}$.
Principle of Duality & Dual Circuit Construction PRINCIPLE

Principle of Duality (Sadiku Sec. 8.10): Two planar networks are duals if the mesh (KVL) equations of one are identical in mathematical form to the nodal (KCL) equations of the other under the exact substitutions:
• $v(t) \leftrightarrow i(t)$ and $q(t) \leftrightarrow \lambda(t)$
• $R\ (\Omega) \leftrightarrow G\ (\text{S})$ ($R_{\text{dual}} = 1/R\ \Omega$)
• $L\ (\text{H}) \leftrightarrow C\ (\text{F})$ ($L\text{ H} \to C = L\text{ F}$ and $C\text{ F} \to L = C\text{ H}$)
• Series branch $\leftrightarrow$ Parallel branches; Mesh $\leftrightarrow$ Non-reference node; Short circuit $\leftrightarrow$ Open circuit; Thévenin $\leftrightarrow$ Norton.

$$R \leftrightarrow G,\quad L \leftrightarrow C,\quad v(t) \leftrightarrow i(t),\quad q(t) \leftrightarrow \lambda(t),\quad \text{Mesh (KVL)} \leftrightarrow \text{Node (KCL)},\quad \text{Series} \leftrightarrow \text{Parallel}$$
Conditions: Original network must be planar (no crossing branches).
Exam Application: Directly asked in BUET EEE 101 2024–25 Q.3(c) ("Obtain the dual of the circuit").
Worked Exam Problem (BUET 2024–25 Q.3c — Step-by-Step Dual Circuit Construction)
  1. Problem: Obtain the dual of a 2-mesh planar circuit where Mesh 1 has an outer $5\text{ A}$ source (upward) and $1\text{ F}$ capacitor, Mesh 1 and Mesh 2 share a vertical branch with $2\ \Omega$ and $0.25\text{ H}$ in series, and Mesh 2 has a top $3\ \Omega$ resistor and an outer branch with a $12\text{ V}$ source in series with $1\ \Omega$.
  2. Step 1 (Assign Dual Nodes): Place Node 1 inside Mesh 1, Node 2 inside Mesh 2, and Reference Node 0 ($GND$) outside the circuit.
  3. Step 2 (Convert Outer Branches of Mesh 1 — Node 1 to Node 0): The $5\text{ A}$ current source assisting clockwise $i_1$ becomes a $5\text{ V}$ voltage source with $+$ terminal at Node 1; the $1\text{ F}$ capacitor in series in Mesh 1 becomes a $1\text{ H}$ inductor connected in parallel from Node 1 to Node 0.
  4. Step 3 (Convert Shared Branch — Node 1 to Node 2): Because $2\ \Omega$ and $0.25\text{ H}$ are in series in the shared branch between Mesh 1 and Mesh 2, their duals are connected in parallel between Node 1 and Node 2: a conductance $G = 2\text{ S}$ ($R_{\text{dual}} = 0.5\ \Omega$) in parallel with a capacitor $C = 0.25\text{ F}$.
  5. Step 4 (Convert Outer Branches of Mesh 2 — Node 2 to Node 0): The top $3\ \Omega$ resistor becomes $G = 3\text{ S}$ ($\frac{1}{3}\ \Omega$) from Node 2 to Node 0. The series combination of $12\text{ V}$ and $1\ \Omega$ becomes a parallel combination of a $12\text{ A}$ current source and $G = 1\text{ S}$ ($1\ \Omega$) connected in series along a branch from Node 2 to Node 0.
Magnetic Circuit Definitions, Air-Gap Force & Analogy Table DEFINITION

Fundamental Magnetic Quantities (2011–12 Q.4a,b, 2024–25 Q.1b):
• Flux ($\Phi$, $\text{Wb}$): Total magnetic lines ($\Phi = BA$). Flux Density ($B$, $\text{T} = \text{Wb/m}^2$): $B = \Phi/A = \mu H$.
• Permeability ($\mu = \mu_0\mu_r$, $\text{H/m}$): $\mu_0 = 4\pi\times 10^{-7}\text{ H/m}$; $\mu_r = \mu/\mu_0$ is relative permeability ($\mu_r = 1$ in air).
• Magnetizing Force ($H$, $\text{At/m}$): MMF gradient $H = NI/l = B/\mu$; in an air gap, $H_g = B_g/\mu_0 \approx 7.958\times 10^5 B_g\text{ At/m}$.
• MMF ($\mathcal{F} = NI$, $\text{At}$) & Reluctance ($\mathcal{R} = \frac{l}{\mu A}$, $\text{At/Wb}$): Hopkinson's law $\mathcal{F} = \Phi\mathcal{R}$; Permeance $\mathcal{P} = 1/\mathcal{R}$.
• Air-Gap Energy Density & Clamping Force: $w_v = \dfrac{B_g^2}{2\mu_0}\ (\text{J/m}^3) \implies F_{\text{gap}} = \dfrac{B_g^2 A_g}{2\mu_0}\ (\text{N})$.

$$\Phi = B A,\quad B = \mu_0\mu_r H,\quad H = \frac{NI}{l},\quad \mathcal{F} = NI = \Phi\mathcal{R},\quad \mathcal{R} = \frac{l}{\mu A},\quad F_{\text{gap}} = \frac{B_g^2 A_g}{2\mu_0}$$
Conditions: If core cross-section increases by $+x\%$ at constant $\Phi$, $B_{\text{new}} = \Phi/[(1 + x/100)A]$ decreases.
Exam Application: Directly asked in BUET EEE 101 2011–12 Q.4(a,b) and 2024–25 Q.1(b).
Complete Electric vs. Magnetic Circuit Analogy Table
  1. Driving Force: Electromotive Force (EMF) $V$ or $E$ ($\text{V}$) $\longleftrightarrow$ Magnetomotive Force (MMF) $\mathcal{F} = NI$ ($\text{At}$).
  2. Flow Quantity: Electric Current $I$ ($\text{A}$) $\longleftrightarrow$ Magnetic Flux $\Phi$ ($\text{Wb}$).
  3. Flow Density: Current Density $J = I/A$ ($\text{A/m}^2$) $\longleftrightarrow$ Flux Density $B = \Phi/A$ ($\text{T} = \text{Wb/m}^2$).
  4. Opposition: Resistance $R = \dfrac{l}{\sigma A}$ ($\Omega$) $\longleftrightarrow$ Reluctance $\mathcal{R} = \dfrac{l}{\mu A}$ ($\text{At/Wb}$); Conductance $G = 1/R$ $\longleftrightarrow$ Permeance $\mathcal{P} = 1/\mathcal{R}$.
  5. Material Property & Field Gradient: Conductivity $\sigma$ ($\text{S/m}$) and Electric Field $E = V/l = J/\sigma$ ($\text{V/m}$) $\longleftrightarrow$ Permeability $\mu = \mu_0\mu_r$ ($\text{H/m}$) and Magnetizing Force $H = NI/l = B/\mu$ ($\text{At/m}$).
  6. Governing Laws: Ohm's Law $V = IR$, KCL $\sum I = 0$, KVL $\sum V = \sum IR$ $\longleftrightarrow$ Hopkinson's Law $\mathcal{F} = \Phi\mathcal{R}$, Gauss's Flux Law $\sum \Phi = 0$, Ampère's Law $NI = \sum H_k l_k = \sum \Phi_k\mathcal{R}_k$.
AC Phasor Diagram Construction, RMS Shortcuts & PF Correction METHOD

Piecewise RMS Squared-Area Shortcuts over $\Delta t = t_2 - t_1$ (2016–17 Q.4b, 2017–18 Q.4a,b):
• Constant segment $V_0$: $\int v^2\,dt = V_0^2\Delta t$
• Ramp through origin ($0 \leftrightarrow V_m$): $\int v^2\,dt = \dfrac{1}{3}V_m^2\Delta t$
• General linear segment ($V_1 \to V_2$): $\int v^2\,dt = \dfrac{\Delta t}{3}(V_1^2 + V_1 V_2 + V_2^2)$
• Sinusoidal half-cycle arch ($V_m\sin\omega t$): $\int v^2\,dt = \dfrac{1}{2}V_m^2\Delta t$.

$$V_{\text{rms}} = \sqrt{\frac{1}{T}\int_0^T v^2(t)\,dt},\quad \int_{t_1}^{t_2} v_{\text{ramp}}^2\,dt = \frac{\Delta t}{3}(V_1^2 + V_1 V_2 + V_2^2),\quad C = \frac{P_T(\tan\theta_1 - \tan\theta_2)}{2\pi f V_{\text{rms}}^2}$$
Conditions: Form Factor $= V_{\text{rms}}/V_{\text{avg}}$ ($1.11$ for sine); Crest/Peak Factor $= V_{\max}/V_{\text{rms}}$ ($\sqrt{2}$ for sine).
Exam Application: Directly asked in BUET EEE 101 2016–17 Q.2(b), Q.4(b), 2017–18 Q.4(a,b), and 2019–20 Q.8(a,b).
Worked Exam Problem (BUET 2016–17 Q.2b — 5-Step Series-Parallel RLC Phasor Diagram)
  1. Problem: Source $\mathbf{V}_s$ in series with $R_1$ feeds two parallel branches: Branch 1 ($R_C$ in series with $X_{C1}$) and Branch 2 ($R_L$ in series with $X_{L1}$). Construct the complete voltage and current phasor diagram.
  2. Step 1 (Reference Phasor): Choose the voltage across the parallel branches $\mathbf{V}_p = V_p\angle 0^\circ$ along the positive horizontal axis.
  3. Step 2 (Capacitive Branch 1): Since $Z_1 = R_C - jX_{C1}$, current $\mathbf{I}_1$ leads $\mathbf{V}_p$ by $\theta_1 = \tan^{-1}(X_{C1}/R_C)$. Along $\mathbf{I}_1$ lies $\mathbf{V}_{R_C} = \mathbf{I}_1 R_C$, and lagging $\mathbf{I}_1$ by $90^\circ$ lies $\mathbf{V}_{C1} = -j\mathbf{I}_1 X_{C1}$ such that $\mathbf{V}_{R_C} + \mathbf{V}_{C1} = \mathbf{V}_p$.
  4. Step 3 (Inductive Branch 2): Since $Z_2 = R_L + jX_{L1}$, current $\mathbf{I}_2$ lags $\mathbf{V}_p$ by $\theta_2 = \tan^{-1}(X_{L1}/R_L)$. Along $\mathbf{I}_2$ lies $\mathbf{V}_{R_L} = \mathbf{I}_2 R_L$, and leading $\mathbf{I}_2$ by $90^\circ$ lies $\mathbf{V}_{L1} = +j\mathbf{I}_2 X_{L1}$ such that $\mathbf{V}_{R_L} + \mathbf{V}_{L1} = \mathbf{V}_p$.
  5. Step 4 & 5 (Total Current $\mathbf{I}_T$ & Source Voltage $\mathbf{V}_s$): Construct the parallelogram sum $\mathbf{I}_T = \mathbf{I}_1 + \mathbf{I}_2$. From the tip of $\mathbf{V}_p$, draw $\mathbf{V}_{R1} = \mathbf{I}_T R_1$ parallel to $\mathbf{I}_T$; the vector from the origin to its tip is $\mathbf{V}_s = \mathbf{V}_p + \mathbf{I}_T R_1$.
Limits, continuity, differentiation & mean value theoremsFreq: 5/50/11
Rolle's Theorem THEOREM

If a real-valued function $f(x)$ defined on $[a, b]$ satisfies:
(1) $f(x)$ is continuous on the closed interval $[a, b]$,
(2) $f(x)$ is differentiable on the open interval $(a, b)$, and
(3) $f(a) = f(b)$,
then there exists at least one point $c \in (a, b)$ such that $f'(c) = 0$.
Increment ($\theta$) Form: If $f(a+h) = f(a)$, then $\exists\,\theta \in (0,1)$ such that $f'(a+\theta h) = 0$.

$$f \in C[a,b],\quad f \text{ differentiable on }(a,b),\quad f(a) = f(b) \implies \exists\,c \in (a,b):\ f'(c) = 0$$
Conditions: All three hypotheses must hold strictly; a single corner point (e.g. $f(x) = |x|$ on $[-1, 1]$) or interior discontinuity invalidates the guarantee.
Exam Application: Geometric Meaning: At least one point $(c, f(c))$ on the curve between $x=a$ and $x=b$ has a horizontal tangent parallel to the $x$-axis. Used to prove that $f'(x) = 0$ has at least one real root between any two consecutive roots of $f(x) = 0$ (BUET 2023–24 Q.2b, 2024–25 Q.2b).
Proof & derivation steps
  1. By the Extreme Value Theorem, continuous $f$ on $[a,b]$ attains its absolute maximum $M$ and minimum $m$.
  2. If $M = m$, $f$ is constant on $[a,b]$, so $f'(c) = 0$ for every $c \in (a,b)$.
  3. If $M > m$, since $f(a) = f(b)$, at least one extremum occurs at an interior point $c \in (a,b)$; by Fermat's theorem on interior extrema, $f'(c) = 0$.
Lagrange's & Cauchy's Mean Value Theorems THEOREM

Lagrange's Mean Value Theorem (First MVT): If $f(x)$ is continuous on the closed interval $[a,b]$ and differentiable on the open interval $(a,b)$, then there exists at least one point $c \in (a,b)$ where the tangent is parallel to the secant chord joining $(a, f(a))$ and $(b, f(b))$: $f'(c) = \frac{f(b)-f(a)}{b-a}$. In increment form ($b = a+h$), $\exists\,\theta \in (0,1)$ such that $f(a+h) = f(a) + h f'(a+\theta h)$.
Cauchy's Generalized Mean Value Theorem: If $f(x)$ and $g(x)$ are both continuous on $[a,b]$ and differentiable on $(a,b)$, and $g'(x) \neq 0$ for all $x \in (a,b)$, then there exists at least one point $c \in (a,b)$ such that $\frac{f(b)-f(a)}{g(b)-g(a)} = \frac{f'(c)}{g'(c)}$ (or $\frac{f(a+h)-f(a)}{g(a+h)-g(a)} = \frac{f'(a+\theta h)}{g'(a+\theta h)}$ for $0 < \theta < 1$).

$$f'(c) = \frac{f(b)-f(a)}{b-a};\qquad f(a+h) = f(a) + h f'(a+\theta h);\qquad \frac{f(b)-f(a)}{g(b)-g(a)} = \frac{f'(c)}{g'(c)}\quad (a < c < b,\ 0 < \theta < 1)$$
Conditions: $f, g \in C[a,b]$, differentiable on $(a,b)$, and $g'(x) \neq 0$ throughout $(a,b)$.
Exam Application: Use Lagrange's MVT to prove two-sided inequalities: bound $f'(c)$ between its extreme values on $[a,b]$ (e.g. $\frac{b-a}{1+b^2} < \tan^{-1}b - \tan^{-1}a < \frac{b-a}{1+a^2}$ for $0 < a < b$, tested in 2010–11 Q.2a, 2011–12 Q.2c, 2014–15 Q.2a, 2018–19 Q.1c, 2021–22 Q.3b, 2023–24 Q.2c).
Proof & derivation steps
  1. Define the auxiliary function $\phi(x) = f(x) - f(a) - \dfrac{f(b)-f(a)}{g(b)-g(a)}[g(x) - g(a)]$ (with $g(x)=x$ for Lagrange).
  2. Verify $\phi \in C[a,b]$, $\phi$ is differentiable on $(a,b)$, and $\phi(a) = \phi(b) = 0$.
  3. Apply Rolle's theorem to $\phi$: $\exists\,c \in (a,b)$ with $\phi'(c) = 0 \implies f'(c) - \dfrac{f(b)-f(a)}{g(b)-g(a)}g'(c) = 0$.
Leibnitz's Theorem for n-th Derivative of a Product THEOREM

Statement: If $u(x)$ and $v(x)$ are two functions possessing derivatives up to the $n$-th order on an interval, then the $n$-th derivative of their product $y = uv$ is given by the binomial-like expansion:

$$(uv)_n = D^n(uv) = \sum_{r=0}^{n} \binom{n}{r} u_{n-r} v_r = u_n v + n u_{n-1} v_1 + \frac{n(n-1)}{2!} u_{n-2} v_2 + \frac{n(n-1)(n-2)}{3!} u_{n-3} v_3 + \cdots + u v_n$$
Conditions: Choose $v$ as the polynomial factor (like $1\pm x^2$ or $x^2$) so that $v_3 = v_4 = \cdots = 0$ and the sum terminates after 3 terms: $D^n[(1\pm x^2)y_2] = (1\pm x^2)y_{n+2} \pm 2nx y_{n+1} \pm n(n-1)y_n$ and $D^n[xy_1] = xy_{n+1} + ny_n$.
Exam Application: Standard 2-part BUET exam problem (2014–15 Q.1b, 2016–17 Q.2a, 2021–22 Q.1c, 2023–24 Q.2a): (1) form a 2nd-order ODE $(1-x^2)y_2 - xy_1 + m^2 y = 0$, (2) differentiate $n$ times by Leibnitz to get $(1-x^2)y_{n+2} - (2n+1)xy_{n+1} + (m^2-n^2)y_n = 0$, then put $x = 0$ to find $y_n(0)$.
Proof & derivation steps
  1. Base case ($n=1$): $(uv)_1 = u_1 v + u v_1 = \binom{1}{0}u_1 v_0 + \binom{1}{1}u_0 v_1$, which holds by the product rule.
  2. Inductive step: Assume the formula holds for order $m$. Differentiate once more and use Pascal's identity $\binom{m}{r-1} + \binom{m}{r} = \binom{m+1}{r}$ to obtain $(uv)_{m+1} = \sum_{r=0}^{m+1}\binom{m+1}{r}u_{m+1-r}v_r$.
Taylor's Theorem with Lagrange & Cauchy Forms of Remainder THEOREM

Taylor's Theorem with Remainder: If $f(x)$ has continuous derivatives up to order $n$ on the closed interval $[a, a+h]$ and $f^{(n+1)}(x)$ exists on the open interval $(a, a+h)$, then there exists $\theta \in (0, 1)$ such that $f(a+h) = f(a) + h f'(a) + \frac{h^2}{2!}f''(a) + \cdots + \frac{h^n}{n!}f^{(n)}(a) + R_n$.
Maclaurin's Theorem: Setting $a = 0$ and replacing $h$ by $x$ gives $f(x) = \sum_{k=0}^{n} \frac{x^k}{k!}f^{(k)}(0) + R_n(x)$.

$$R_n = \frac{h^{n+1}}{(n+1)!}f^{(n+1)}(a+\theta h)\ (\text{Lagrange}),\qquad R_n = \frac{h^{n+1}(1-\theta)^n}{n!}f^{(n+1)}(a+\theta h)\ (\text{Cauchy}),\quad 0<\theta<1$$
Conditions: Note: If the expansion is written up to the $(n-1)$-th derivative term $\frac{h^{n-1}}{(n-1)!}f^{(n-1)}(a)$, then the $n$-th remainder is $R_n = \frac{h^n}{n!}f^{(n)}(a+\theta h)$ (Lagrange) and $R_n = \frac{h^n(1-\theta)^{n-1}}{(n-1)!}f^{(n)}(a+\theta h)$ (Cauchy).
Exam Application: Infinite Taylor series converges to $f(x)$ iff $\lim_{n\to\infty} R_n(x) = 0$. Classic BUET problem (2011–12 Q.2b, 2019–20 Q.2b): for $f(x) = \frac{1}{1-x}$, $R_n = \frac{x^n}{(1-\theta x)^{n+1}} \implies \theta = \frac{1-(1-x)^{1/(n+1)}}{x}$.
Formal Definitions of Continuity, Discontinuity & Differentiability at a Point DEFINITION

Continuity at $x = a$: A function $f(x)$ is continuous at an interior point $x = a$ if (i) $f(a)$ is defined (finite), (ii) $\lim_{x\to a} f(x)$ exists ($\lim_{x\to a^-} f(x) = \lim_{x\to a^+} f(x)$), and (iii) $\lim_{x\to a} f(x) = f(a)$. In $\varepsilon$–$\delta$ form: $\forall\,\varepsilon > 0,\ \exists\,\delta > 0$ such that $|x-a|<\delta \implies |f(x)-f(a)|<\varepsilon$.
Differentiability at $x = a$: $f(x)$ is differentiable at $x = a$ if the left-hand derivative $Lf'(a)$ and right-hand derivative $Rf'(a)$ both exist finitely and are equal.

$$Lf'(a) = \lim_{h\to 0^-}\frac{f(a+h)-f(a)}{h},\qquad Rf'(a) = \lim_{h\to 0^+}\frac{f(a+h)-f(a)}{h},\qquad f'(a)\text{ exists} \iff Lf'(a)=Rf'(a)\text{ (finite)}$$
Conditions: Theorem: Differentiability at $x = a$ implies continuity at $x = a$, but the converse is false (e.g. $f(x)=|x-a|$ or $f(x)=x\sin(1/x)$ at $x=0$). For $f(x) = x^p\sin(1/x)$ with $f(0)=0$: continuous at $0 \iff p > 0$; differentiable at $0 \iff p > 1$.
Exam Application: Tested in Question 1(a) of EVERY BUET Math 157 exam (2010–2025): always write the explicit difference quotient $\lim_{h\to 0^\pm} \frac{f(a+h)-f(a)}{h}$, never just differentiate the formulas piece-by-piece!
L'Hôpital's Rule for Indeterminate Forms THEOREM

Statement: Suppose $f(x)$ and $g(x)$ are differentiable on an open interval $I$ containing $a$ (except possibly at $a$ itself) with $g'(x) \neq 0$ on $I\setminus\{a\}$. If $\lim_{x\to a} f(x) = \lim_{x\to a} g(x) = 0$ (form $\frac{0}{0}$) or $\pm\infty$ (form $\frac{\infty}{\infty}$), and if $\lim_{x\to a} \frac{f'(x)}{g'(x)} = L$ (finite or $\pm\infty$), then $\lim_{x\to a} \frac{f(x)}{g(x)} = L$.

$$\lim_{x\to a}\frac{f(x)}{g(x)} = \lim_{x\to a}\frac{f'(x)}{g'(x)};\qquad \text{For }1^\infty, 0^0, \infty^0:\ \lim_{x\to a} u(x)^{v(x)} = \exp\!\left[\lim_{x\to a} v(x)\ln u(x)\right]$$
Conditions: For indeterminate difference $\infty - \infty$ (e.g. $\frac{x}{x-1} - \frac{1}{\ln x}$), combine over a common denominator first to get $0/0$ before differentiating.
Exam Application: Standard Maclaurin shortcuts for L'Hôpital limits: $\sin x = x - \frac{x^3}{6} + \frac{x^5}{120} - \cdots$, $\tan x = x + \frac{x^3}{3} + \frac{2x^5}{15} + \cdots$, $\ln(1+x) = x - \frac{x^2}{2} + \frac{x^3}{3} - \cdots$.
Classic Oscillating & Cusp Continuity/Differentiability Proofs at x = 0 (2012–2025) THEOREM

Fundamental Theorem (Differentiability $\implies$ Continuity, 2023–24 Q1a, 2024–25 Q1a): Every function differentiable at $x=a$ is necessarily continuous at $x=a$, since $\lim_{x\to a}[f(x)-f(a)] = \lim_{x\to a}\frac{f(x)-f(a)}{x-a}(x-a) = f'(a)\cdot 0 = 0$. The converse is false: a continuous function can fail to be differentiable at a rapid oscillation ($x\sin(1/x)$), a vertical tangent / cusp ($\pm\sqrt{|x|}$), or a sharp corner ($|x-2|+|x-3|$).

$$f_A(x)=x\sin\frac{1}{x}\;(\text{cont.,}\;f_A'(0)\text{ DNE}),\quad f_B(x)=x^2\cos\frac{1}{x}\;(f_B'(0)=0),\quad f_C(x)=\operatorname{sgn}(x)\sqrt{|x|}\;(Rf_C'(0)=Lf_C'(0)=+\infty)$$
Conditions: Use the Squeeze Theorem ($0\le |x^k\sin(1/x)|\le |x|^k$) for continuity at $x=0$, and the limit definition $f'(0)=\lim_{h\to 0}\frac{f(h)-f(0)}{h}$ for differentiability.
Exam Application: Tested in 2012–13 Q1a, 2013–14 Q1a, 2018–19 Q1a, 2019–20 Q1a, 2023–24 Q1a, 2024–25 Q1a.
Complete Exam Proofs for Cases A, B & C
  1. Case A ($f(x)=x\sin(1/x)$ for $x\neq 0, f(0)=0$, 2012–13 Q1a, 2019–20 Q1a): (1) Continuity: $0\le |x\sin(1/x)|\le |x|\to 0 \implies \lim_{x\to 0}f(x)=0=f(0)$ by Squeeze Theorem. (2) Differentiability: $f'(0)=\lim_{h\to 0}\frac{h\sin(1/h)-0}{h}=\lim_{h\to 0}\sin(1/h)$, which oscillates infinitely between $-1$ and $+1$ without approaching a finite limit $\implies$ not differentiable at $x=0$.
  2. Case B ($f(x)=x^2\cos(1/x)$ for $x\neq 0, f(0)=0$, 2013–14 Q1a): (1) Continuity: $0\le |x^2\cos(1/x)|\le x^2\to 0 \implies$ continuous at $x=0$. (2) Differentiability: $f'(0)=\lim_{h\to 0}\frac{h^2\cos(1/h)-0}{h}=\lim_{h\to 0}h\cos(1/h)=0$ by Squeeze Theorem ($-|h|\le h\cos(1/h)\le |h|$) $\implies$ both continuous and differentiable with $f'(0)=0$.
  3. Case C ($f(x)=\sqrt{|x|}$ for $x\ge 0$ and $-\sqrt{|x|}$ for $x<0$, 2018–19 Q1a): (1) Continuity: $\lim_{x\to 0^-}(-\sqrt{-x})=0=\lim_{x\to 0^+}\sqrt{x}=f(0)$. (2) Differentiability: $Rf'(0)=\lim_{h\to 0^+}\frac{\sqrt{h}-0}{h}=\lim_{h\to 0^+}\frac{1}{\sqrt{h}}=+\infty$ and $Lf'(0)=\lim_{h\to 0^-}\frac{-\sqrt{-h}-0}{h}=\lim_{h\to 0^-}\frac{1}{\sqrt{-h}}=+\infty$ (infinite one-sided derivatives / vertical tangent $\implies$ not differentiable).
Solved Past-Paper Indeterminate Limits ($1^\infty$, $\infty - \infty$) & RL Circuit R → 0⁺ Limit THEOREM

1. Type $1^\infty$ Exponential Limits: Write $\ln L = \lim_{x\to a}g(x)\ln f(x)$ to convert $1^\infty$ into $0/0$: (i) $\lim_{x\to 0}(\frac{\sin x}{x})^{1/x^2}=e^{-1/6}$ (2024–25 Q1c); (ii) $\lim_{x\to 0}(\frac{\tan x}{x})^{1/x^2}=e^{1/3}$ (2013–14 Q2b-ii); (iii) $\lim_{x\to e}(\ln x)^{1/(1-\ln x)}=1/e$ (2010–11 Q1b); (iv) $\lim_{x\to 1^-}(1-x^2)^{1/\ln(1-x)}=e$ (2023–24 Q1b).
2. Type $\infty-\infty$ Logarithmic Limits: $\lim_{x\to 1}(\frac{x}{x-1}-\frac{1}{\ln x})=\frac{1}{2}$ (2013–14 Q2b-i, 2016–17 Q2c) and $\lim_{x\to 2}(\frac{1}{x-2}-\frac{1}{\ln(x-1)})=-\frac{1}{2}$ (2021–22 Q2a).
3. RL Circuit Zero-Resistance Limit (2018–19 Q2c): As $R\to 0^+$, $I(t)=\frac{V}{R}(1-e^{-Rt/L}) \to \frac{Vt}{L}$.

$$\lim_{x\to a}[f(x)]^{g(x)}=\exp\!\left[\lim_{x\to a}g(x)\ln f(x)\right],\qquad \lim_{R\to 0^+}I(t)=\lim_{R\to 0^+}\frac{V(1-e^{-Rt/L})}{R}=\frac{Vt}{L}$$
Conditions: In the RL circuit limit, differentiate numerator and denominator with respect to $R$ while treating $t, V, L$ as constants.
Exam Application: Directly asked in 2010–11 Q1b, 2013–14 Q2b, 2016–17 Q2c, 2018–19 Q2c, 2021–22 Q2a, 2023–24 Q1b, 2024–25 Q1b,c.
Complete Derivations of All 6 Past-Paper Limit Problems
  1. Exponential Limits (2010–2025): (1) $\ln L = \lim_{x\to 0}\frac{\ln(\sin x/x)}{x^2} = -\frac{1}{6} \implies L=e^{-1/6}$; (2) $\ln L = \lim_{x\to 0}\frac{\ln(\tan x/x)}{x^2} = +\frac{1}{3} \implies L=e^{1/3}$; (3) $\ln L = \lim_{x\to e}\frac{\ln(\ln x)}{1-\ln x} \stackrel{\text{L'H}}{=} \lim_{x\to e}\frac{1/(x\ln x)}{-1/x} = -1 \implies L=1/e$; (4) $\ln L = \lim_{x\to 1^-}\frac{\ln(1-x)+\ln(1+x)}{\ln(1-x)} = 1 \implies L=e$.
  2. Logarithmic Limits (2013–2022): $\lim_{x\to 1}\frac{x\ln x-x+1}{(x-1)\ln x} \stackrel{\text{L'H}}{=} \lim_{x\to 1}\frac{\ln x}{\ln x+1-1/x} \stackrel{\text{L'H}}{=} \lim_{x\to 1}\frac{1/x}{1/x+1/x^2} = \frac{1}{2}$. Substituting $u=x-1\to 1$ as $x\to 2$ gives $\lim_{x\to 2}[\frac{1}{x-2}-\frac{1}{\ln(x-1)}] = \lim_{u\to 1}[\frac{1}{u-1}-\frac{1}{\ln u}] = -\frac{1}{2}$.
  3. RL Circuit Limit as $R\to 0^+$ (2018–19 Q2c): $\lim_{R\to 0^+}\frac{V(1-e^{-Rt/L})}{R} \stackrel{\text{L'H}}{=} \lim_{R\to 0^+}\frac{V(\frac{t}{L}e^{-Rt/L})}{1} = \frac{Vt}{L}$. Physical meaning: resistive damping vanishes and $I(t)$ rises linearly without bound at slope $dI/dt = V/L$.
Master Derivations of Leibnitz Differential Equations & $(y_n)_0$ Recurrences (2010–2025) THEOREM

Standard $n$-th Derivative of $\frac{1}{x^2+a^2}$: Setting $x=a\cot\theta$ ($\theta=\cot^{-1}(x/a)$) yields $\frac{d^n}{dx^n}\left(\frac{1}{x^2+a^2}\right) = \frac{(-1)^n n!}{a^{n+1}}\sin^{n+1}\theta\sin[(n+1)\theta]$.
Four Classic Leibnitz ODEs:
1. $y=\tan^{-1}x \implies (1+x^2)y_{n+2}+2(n+1)xy_{n+1}+n(n+1)y_n=0$ (2024–25 Q2a).
2. $y=a\cos(\ln x)+b\sin(\ln x) \implies x^2 y_{n+2}+(2n+1)xy_{n+1}+(n^2+1)y_n=0$ (2011–12 Q2a, 2020–21 Q1c).
3. $y^{1/m}+y^{-1/m}=2x \implies (x^2-1)y_{n+2}+(2n+1)xy_{n+1}+(n^2-m^2)y_n=0$ (2014–15 Q1b, 2019–20 Q2a).
4. $y=(x+\sqrt{1+x^2})^m$ or $\sinh(m\sinh^{-1}x) \implies (1+x^2)y_{n+2}+(2n+1)xy_{n+1}+(n^2-m^2)y_n=0$ (2012–13 Q2a, 2023–24 Q2a).

$$(1+x^2)y_{n+2}+(2n+1)xy_{n+1}+(n^2-m^2)y_n=0,\qquad (y_n)_0=\begin{cases}m^2(m^2-2^2)\cdots[m^2-(n-2)^2],&n\text{ even}\\m(m^2-1^2)\cdots[m^2-(n-2)^2],&n\text{ odd}\end{cases}$$
Conditions: Eliminate radicals and denominators before differentiating a second time, then apply Leibnitz's theorem $n$ times term-by-term.
Exam Application: Tested in every BUET MATH 157 paper from 2010–11 through 2024–25.
Complete Proofs for All 4 Leibnitz Problems
  1. Problem 1 ($y=\tan^{-1}x$, 2024–25 Q2a): $(1+x^2)y_1=1 \implies (1+x^2)y_2+2xy_1=0$. Differentiating $n$ times gives $[(1+x^2)y_{n+2}+2nxy_{n+1}+n(n-1)y_n]+[2xy_{n+1}+2ny_n]=0 \implies (1+x^2)y_{n+2}+2(n+1)xy_{n+1}+n(n+1)y_n=0$.
  2. Problem 2 ($y=a\cos(\ln x)+b\sin(\ln x)$, 2011–12 Q2a, 2020–21 Q1c): $xy_1=-a\sin(\ln x)+b\cos(\ln x) \implies x^2y_2+xy_1+y=0 \implies x^2y_{n+2}+(2n+1)xy_{n+1}+(n^2+1)y_n=0$.
  3. Problem 3 ($y^{1/m}+y^{-1/m}=2x$, 2014–15 Q1b, 2019–20 Q2a): Solving $z+1/z=2x$ gives $y=(x\pm\sqrt{x^2-1})^m \implies (x^2-1)y_1^2=m^2y^2 \implies (x^2-1)y_2+xy_1-m^2y=0 \implies (x^2-1)y_{n+2}+(2n+1)xy_{n+1}+(n^2-m^2)y_n=0$, with $(y_{n+2})_0=(n^2-m^2)(y_n)_0$.
  4. Problem 4 ($y=(x+\sqrt{1+x^2})^m$, 2012–13 Q2a, 2023–24 Q2a): $(1+x^2)y_1^2=m^2y^2 \implies (1+x^2)y_2+xy_1-m^2y=0 \implies (1+x^2)y_{n+2}+(2n+1)xy_{n+1}+(n^2-m^2)y_n=0$. Setting $x=0$ gives $(y_{n+2})_0=(m^2-n^2)(y_n)_0$ with $(y)_0=1, (y_1)_0=m$.
Rolle's Geometric Mean Proof, Highway Speeding Ticket Problem & MVT Inequality Proofs THEOREM

1. Rolle's Geometric Mean Problem (2023–24 Q2b): For $f(x)=\ln\left[\frac{x^2+ab}{x(a+b)}\right]$ on $[a,b]$ ($0<a<b$), $f(a)=f(b)=\ln 1=0$ and $f'(c)=\frac{c^2-ab}{c(c^2+ab)}=0 \implies c=\sqrt{ab}\in(a,b)$.
2. Highway Police Speeding Ticket Problem (2018–19 Q1c): Clocked at $50\text{ mph}$ at 8:05 a.m. and $55\text{ mph}$ at 8:10 a.m. over $\Delta s=5\text{ miles}$ ($\Delta t=1/12\text{ hr}$), MVT guarantees $v(c)=s'(c)=\frac{5}{1/12}=60\text{ mph}>55\text{ mph}$ at some instant $t=c$.
3. Classic MVT & Cauchy MVT Inequality Proofs (2021–22 Q3b & 2011–12 Q2c): Tight two-sided bounds via Lagrange and Cauchy MVT.

$$c=\sqrt{ab}\in(a,b);\quad v(c)=60\text{ mph}>55\text{ mph};\quad \frac{\pi}{4}+\frac{3}{25}<\tan^{-1}\!\left(\frac{4}{3}\right)<\frac{\pi}{4}+\frac{1}{6};\quad \sqrt{\frac{1-x}{1+x}}<\frac{\ln(1+x)}{\sin^{-1}x}<1$$
Conditions: Verify continuity on the closed interval $[a,b]$ and differentiability on the open interval $(a,b)$ before invoking Rolle's or Lagrange's/Cauchy's MVT.
Exam Application: Directly asked in 2011–12 Q2c, 2018–19 Q1c, 2021–22 Q3b, 2023–24 Q2b, 2024–25 Q2b.
Complete Proofs for All 4 MVT Exam Problems
  1. Rolle's Theorem on $f(x)=\ln\left[\frac{x^2+ab}{x(a+b)}\right]$ (2023–24 Q2b): Continuous on $[a,b]$, differentiable on $(a,b)$, and $f(a)=f(b)=\ln 1=0$. Solving $f'(c)=\frac{2c}{c^2+ab}-\frac{1}{c}=0$ gives $c=\sqrt{ab}\in(a,b)$.
  2. Speeding Ticket Problem (2018–19 Q1c): Position $s(t)$ is continuous and differentiable on $[t_1,t_2]$ ($\Delta t=5/60=1/12\text{ hr}, \Delta s=5\text{ mi}$). By MVT, $\exists c\in(t_1,t_2)$ with $v(c)=s'(c)=5/(1/12)=60\text{ mph}>55\text{ mph}$.
  3. Arctangent Inequality (2021–22 Q3b): On $[u,v]$ ($0<u<v$), $\frac{\tan^{-1}v-\tan^{-1}u}{v-u}=\frac{1}{1+c^2}\in\left(\frac{1}{1+v^2},\frac{1}{1+u^2}\right)$. Setting $u=1, v=4/3$ gives $\frac{\pi}{4}+\frac{3}{25}<\tan^{-1}(4/3)<\frac{\pi}{4}+\frac{1}{6}$.
  4. Log/Arcsin Inequality (2011–12 Q2c): By Cauchy's MVT on $f(t)=\ln(1+t), g(t)=\sin^{-1}t$ over $[0,x]$ ($0<x<1$), $\frac{\ln(1+x)}{\sin^{-1}x}=\sqrt{\frac{1-c}{1+c}}\in\left(\sqrt{\frac{1-x}{1+x}},1\right)$.
Taylor/Maclaurin Lagrange Remainder Proofs: Exact θ for (1−x)⁻¹ & ln(1.2) Error Bound THEOREM

1. Exact $\theta$ in Lagrange's Remainder for $f(x)=\frac{1}{1-x}$ (2011–12 Q2b, 2019–20 Q2b): Since $f^{(k)}(x)=k!(1-x)^{-(k+1)}$, Maclaurin's remainder after $n$ terms is $R_n=\frac{x^n}{(1-\theta x)^{n+1}}$. Equating to the exact geometric progression remainder $\frac{x^n}{1-x}$ gives $\theta=\frac{1-(1-x)^{1/(n+1)}}{x}$.
2. Maclaurin Expansion of $\ln(1+x)$ with Lagrange Remainder & $\ln(1.2)$ (2024–25 Q2c): Summing the first $n=7$ terms at $x=0.2$ yields $\ln(1.2)\approx 0.1823218$ with remainder $|R_7|=\frac{(0.2)^8}{8(1+0.2\theta)^8}<3.2\times 10^{-7}$.

$$\theta=\frac{1-(1-x)^{\frac{1}{n+1}}}{x},\qquad \ln(1.2)\approx\sum_{k=1}^7(-1)^{k-1}\frac{(0.2)^k}{k}=0.1823218,\qquad |R_7|<\frac{(0.2)^8}{8}=3.2\times 10^{-7}$$
Conditions: $f(x)$ must have continuous derivatives up to order $(n-1)$ on $[a,x]$ and $f^{(n)}(x)$ existing on $(a,x)$, with $0<\theta<1$.
Exam Application: Directly asked in 2011–12 Q2b, 2019–20 Q2b, 2021–22 Q2c, 2024–25 Q2c.
Step-by-step Remainder Derivations
  1. Finding $\theta$ for $(1-x)^{-1}$ (2011–12 Q2b, 2019–20 Q2b): $\frac{1}{1-x}=1+x+\cdots+x^{n-1}+R_n$ where $R_n=\frac{x^n}{n!}f^{(n)}(\theta x)=\frac{x^n}{(1-\theta x)^{n+1}}$. From $\frac{1}{1-x}-\frac{1-x^n}{1-x}=\frac{x^n}{1-x}$, equate $\frac{x^n}{(1-\theta x)^{n+1}}=\frac{x^n}{1-x} \implies 1-\theta x=(1-x)^{1/(n+1)} \implies \theta=\frac{1-(1-x)^{1/(n+1)}}{x}$.
  2. Approximating $\ln(1.2)$ with Error Bound (2024–25 Q2c): For $f(x)=\ln(1+x)$, $f^{(k)}(x)=\frac{(-1)^{k-1}(k-1)!}{(1+x)^k}$. At $x=0.2$ with $n=7$: $\ln(1.2)\approx 0.2-0.02+0.0026667-0.0004+0.000064-0.00001067+0.00000183=0.1823218$, and since $\theta>0$, $|R_7|=\frac{(0.2)^8}{8(1+0.2\theta)^8}<\frac{(0.2)^8}{8}=3.2\times 10^{-7}$.
Partial derivatives, curves & geometryFreq: 5/50/9
Euler's Theorem on Homogeneous Functions THEOREM

Definition: A function $u(x, y, z)$ is homogeneous of degree $n$ if $u(tx, ty, tz) = t^n u(x, y, z)$ for all $t > 0$ (equivalently $u = x^n \phi(y/x, z/x)$).
Euler's Theorem (1st & 2nd Order): If $u(x, y)$ is a homogeneous function of degree $n$ in $x$ and $y$ with continuous partial derivatives, then:

$$\begin{aligned}&x\frac{\partial u}{\partial x}+y\frac{\partial u}{\partial y}=nu,\qquad x^2\frac{\partial^2u}{\partial x^2}+2xy\frac{\partial^2u}{\partial x\partial y}+y^2\frac{\partial^2u}{\partial y^2}=n(n-1)u\\&\text{Composite }f(u)=v(x,y)\text{ (deg }n\text{): }xu_x+yu_y = n\frac{f(u)}{f'(u)} = g(u),\quad x^2u_{xx}+2xyu_{xy}+y^2u_{yy} = g(u)\big[g'(u)-1\big]\end{aligned}$$
Conditions: If $f(u) = v(x,y)$ where $v$ is homogeneous of degree $n$ (e.g. $u = \sin^{-1}\!\left(\frac{x^2+y^2}{x+y}\right) \implies v = \sin u$ has degree $n=1$), then $x u_x + y u_y = n \dfrac{f(u)}{f'(u)}$ and $x^2 u_{xx} + 2xy u_{xy} + y^2 u_{yy} = g(u)[g'(u)-1]$ where $g(u) = n f(u)/f'(u)$.
Exam Application: Always check whether $u$ itself is homogeneous OR a function $f(u)$ is homogeneous before applying Euler's theorem! Tested in EVERY BUET Math 157 exam (2010–2025).
Proof & derivation steps
  1. Write $u(x,y) = x^n \phi(y/x)$ and differentiate partially with respect to $x$ and $y$.
  2. $x u_x = n x^n \phi(y/x) + x^{n+1}\phi'(y/x)(-y/x^2) = n u - x^{n-1}y\,\phi'(y/x)$.
  3. $y u_y = y x^n \phi'(y/x)(1/x) = x^{n-1}y\,\phi'(y/x)$. Adding gives $x u_x + y u_y = n u$.
Second-Derivative Test (Hessian) & Lagrange Multipliers THEOREM

1D Second-Derivative Test & Concavity: At a critical point $f'(x_0)=0$: $f''(x_0)<0 \Rightarrow$ local maximum; $f''(x_0)>0 \Rightarrow$ local minimum; $f''(x_0)=0$ with sign change of $f''$ $\Rightarrow$ point of inflection.
2D Second-Derivative (Hessian) Test: At a stationary point $(a,b)$ where $f_x = f_y = 0$, let $r = f_{xx}$, $s = f_{xy}$, $t = f_{yy}$, and $D = rt - s^2$. Then $D > 0, r < 0 \Rightarrow$ local maximum; $D > 0, r > 0 \Rightarrow$ local minimum; $D < 0 \Rightarrow$ saddle point; $D = 0 \Rightarrow$ test inconclusive.
Lagrange's Method of Undetermined Multipliers: To find the extremum of $f(x,y,z)$ subject to the constraint $g(x,y,z)=0$, form the auxiliary function $\mathcal{L}(x,y,z,\lambda) = f(x,y,z) - \lambda g(x,y,z)$ and solve $\nabla f = \lambda \nabla g$ together with $g(x,y,z)=0$.

$$D=f_{xx}f_{yy}-f_{xy}^2,\qquad \mathcal{L}(x,y,z,\lambda)=f(x,y,z)-\lambda g(x,y,z),\qquad \nabla f=\lambda\nabla g \iff \frac{f_x}{g_x}=\frac{f_y}{g_y}=\frac{f_z}{g_z}=\lambda$$
Conditions: $f, g$ have continuous second partial derivatives and $\nabla g \neq \mathbf{0}$ on the constraint surface.
Exam Application: For geometric optimization (maximum volume box inscribed in ellipsoid $x^2/a^2 + y^2/b^2 + z^2/c^2 = 1$), symmetry + Lagrange multipliers gives $x = a/\sqrt{3}, y = b/\sqrt{3}, z = c/\sqrt{3}$ in 4 lines.
Laplace's Equation in 2D Polar Coordinates (r, θ) & 3D Radial Functions V(r) THEOREM

2D Polar Laplacian: Under the polar coordinate transformation $x = r\cos\theta, y = r\sin\theta$ ($r^2 = x^2+y^2, \theta = \tan^{-1}(y/x)$), the two-dimensional Laplacian transforms as:
3D Radial Laplacian: If $V = f(r)$ where $r^2 = (x-a)^2 + (y-b)^2 + (z-c)^2$, the three-dimensional Laplacian depends only on $r$:

$$\frac{\partial^2 V}{\partial x^2} + \frac{\partial^2 V}{\partial y^2} = \frac{\partial^2 V}{\partial r^2} + \frac{1}{r}\frac{\partial V}{\partial r} + \frac{1}{r^2}\frac{\partial^2 V}{\partial \theta^2};\qquad \frac{\partial^2 V}{\partial x^2} + \frac{\partial^2 V}{\partial y^2} + \frac{\partial^2 V}{\partial z^2} = \frac{d^2 V}{dr^2} + \frac{2}{r}\frac{dV}{dr}$$
Conditions: For 3D radial functions: $\frac{\partial r}{\partial x} = \frac{x-a}{r} \implies \frac{\partial V}{\partial x} = f'(r)\frac{x-a}{r}$ and $\frac{\partial^2 V}{\partial x^2} = f''(r)\frac{(x-a)^2}{r^2} + f'(r)\frac{r^2-(x-a)^2}{r^3}$. Summing over $x, y, z$ gives $f''(r) + \frac{2}{r}f'(r)$.
Exam Application: Tested repeatedly: 2011–12 Q.3c ($u=\ln r \implies \nabla^2 u = 1/r^2$), 2012–13 Q.3a, 2013–14 Q.2a ($u=r^m$ harmonic in 3D for $m=0,-1$), 2014–15 Q.2b, 2016–17 Q.3a, 2018–19 Q.2a, 2020–21 Q.3a.
Curvature (κ), Radius & Circle of Curvature, Pedal Equation (p–r) & Focal Chord Theorem THEOREM

Curvature & Radius of Curvature: The curvature $\kappa = \left|\frac{d\psi}{ds}\right|$ of a plane curve at any point measures the rate at which the tangent angle $\psi$ turns with respect to arc length $s$. The radius of curvature is $\rho = \frac{1}{\kappa} = \left|\frac{ds}{d\psi}\right|$.
Pedal Equation: The relation between the radius vector $r$ from the pole (origin) and the perpendicular distance $p = r\sin\phi$ from the pole to the tangent line.

$$\rho = \frac{(1+y_1^2)^{3/2}}{|y_2|} = \frac{(r^2+r_1^2)^{3/2}}{|r^2+2r_1^2-rr_2|} = r\frac{dr}{dp};\qquad \frac{1}{p^2} = \frac{1}{r^2} + \frac{1}{r^4}\left(\frac{dr}{d\theta}\right)^2$$
Conditions: Centre of Curvature $(\bar{x}, \bar{y})$ & Circle of Curvature: $\bar{x} = x - \frac{y_1(1+y_1^2)}{y_2}$, $\bar{y} = y + \frac{1+y_1^2}{y_2}$, and $(X-\bar{x})^2 + (Y-\bar{y})^2 = \rho^2$.
Exam Application: Classic BUET Theorems: (1) For ellipse $\frac{x^2}{a^2}+\frac{y^2}{b^2}=1$, at $(a,0)$, $\rho = \frac{b^2}{a} =$ semi-latus rectum (2011–12 Q.4a, 2018–19 Q.4b, 2024–25 Q.4b); (2) If $\rho_1, \rho_2$ are radii of curvature at the ends of a focal chord of parabola $y^2=4ax$, then $\rho_1^{-2/3} + \rho_2^{-2/3} = (2a)^{-2/3}$ (2023–24 Q.4b).
Asymptotes of Algebraic Curves & Cubic Intersection Theorem THEOREM

Definition: A straight line is an asymptote of an infinite branch of a curve if the perpendicular distance from a point $P$ on the curve to the line approaches zero as $P$ moves to infinity along the branch.
Method of $\phi_n(m)$: Arrange the degree-$n$ algebraic curve in homogeneous degree groups $\phi_n(x,y) + \phi_{n-1}(x,y) + \phi_{n-2}(x,y) + \cdots = 0$ and put $x=1, y=m$:

$$\text{Slopes: }\phi_n(m) = 0;\quad \text{Non-repeated }m:\ c = -\frac{\phi_{n-1}(m)}{\phi_n'(m)};\quad \text{Double root }m:\ \frac{c^2}{2!}\phi_n''(m) + c\phi_{n-1}'(m) + \phi_{n-2}(m) = 0$$
Conditions: Parallel asymptotes: equate to zero the real linear factors of the coefficient of the highest power of $x$ (horizontal) and of $y$ (vertical).
Exam Application: Cubic Intersection Theorem (2011–12 Q.4c): The 3 asymptotes $L_1 L_2 L_3 = 0$ of a cubic curve $F_3 + F_1 + F_0 = 0$ cut the curve again in $3(3-2) = 3$ points lying on the line $F_1 + F_0 = 0$ (after matching $F_3 + F_2$ with $L_1 L_2 L_3$).
Composite Euler's Theorem Proofs & Cyclic Chain Rule Identity (2011–2025) THEOREM

Composite Euler's Theorem Corollary: If $\phi(u)=f(x,y,z)$ is homogeneous of degree $n$, applying Euler's theorem to $\phi(u)$ yields $x\phi'(u)u_x+y\phi'(u)u_y+z\phi'(u)u_z=n\phi(u)$, so $\sum x u_x = n\frac{\phi(u)}{\phi'(u)} = g(u)$ and $x^2 u_{xx}+2xy u_{xy}+y^2 u_{yy}=g(u)[g'(u)-1]$.
Cyclic Chain Rule Identity (2023–24 Q3a): For $u=f(v,w)$ where $v=x^2+2yz$ and $w=y^2+2zx$, $(y^2-zx)u_x+(x^2-yz)u_y+(z^2-xy)u_z=0$.

$$x u_x+y u_y+z u_z=n\frac{\phi(u)}{\phi'(u)}=g(u),\quad x^2 u_{xx}+2xy u_{xy}+y^2 u_{yy}=g(u)[g'(u)-1];\quad (y^2-zx)u_x+(x^2-yz)u_y+(z^2-xy)u_z=0$$
Conditions: First rewrite the given inverse-trig or logarithmic equation as $\phi(u)=f(x,y,z)$ and compute the homogeneity degree $n$ of $f$ (numerator degree minus denominator degree).
Exam Application: Tested in 2011–12 Q3b, 2019–20 Q3a, 2020–21 Q2c, 2021–22 Q2b, 2023–24 Q3a, 2024–25 Q3a.
All 5 Solved Past-Paper Problems
  1. Ex 1 (2019–20 Q3a): $u=\tan^{-1}\left(\frac{x^2+y^2}{x-y}\right) \implies \phi(u)=\tan u$ has degree $n=2-1=1 \implies xu_x+yu_y=1\cdot\frac{\tan u}{\sec^2 u}=\frac{1}{2}\sin 2u$.
  2. Ex 2 (2020–21 Q2c) & Ex 3 (2021–22 Q2b): For $\tan u=\frac{5x^4-3xy^3+7y^2z^2}{x^2-2y^2+3yz}$ ($n=4-2=2$), $xu_x+yu_y+zu_z=2\frac{\tan u}{\sec^2 u}=\sin 2u$. For $u=\ln\left(\frac{x^4+y^4}{x+y}\right) \implies e^u$ ($n=4-1=3$), $xu_x+yu_y=3\frac{e^u}{e^u}=3$.
  3. Ex 4 (2011–12 Q3b): $u=\csc^{-1}\sqrt{\frac{x^{1/2}+y^{1/2}}{x^{1/3}+y^{1/3}}} \implies \csc u$ has degree $n=\frac{1/2-1/3}{2}=\frac{1}{12} \implies g(u)=-\frac{1}{12}\tan u$ and $x^2u_{xx}+2xyu_{xy}+y^2u_{yy}=g(u)[g'(u)-1]=\frac{1}{12}\tan u\left(\frac{13}{12}+\frac{1}{12}\tan^2 u\right)$.
  4. Ex 5 (2023–24 Q3a): For $v=x^2+2yz, w=y^2+2zx$: $\sum(y^2-zx)u_x = [(y^2-zx)(2x)+(x^2-yz)(2z)+(z^2-xy)(2y)]f_v + [(y^2-zx)(2z)+(x^2-yz)(2y)+(z^2-xy)(2x)]f_w = 0\cdot f_v + 0\cdot f_w = 0$.
Straight-Line Tangent Proof & Master Pedal Equation Derivations (2010–2025) THEOREM

1. Straight-Line Tangent Proof (2024–25 Q3c): On $y=mx+c$, at any point $P(x_1,y_1)$ (where $y_1=mx_1+c$), the slope is $\left.\frac{dy}{dx}\right|_{(x_1,y_1)}=m$, so the tangent line is $Y-y_1=m(X-x_1) \implies Y=mX+(y_1-mx_1)=mX+c$ (the line itself).
2. Definition of Pedal Equation (2018–19 Q3b): The relation between the radius vector $r$ of any point $P$ from the pole $O$ and the perpendicular length $p$ from $O$ onto the tangent at $P$ is the pedal ($p$-$r$) equation.
3. Five Master Pedal Equations: (i) Rose $r^n=a^n\cos(n\theta) \implies r^{n+1}=a^np$; (ii) Cardioid $r=a(1\pm\cos\theta) \implies r^3=2ap^2$; (iii) Astroid $x^{2/3}+y^{2/3}=a^{2/3} \implies r^2+3p^2=a^2$; (iv) Ellipse focus $\frac{b^2}{p^2}=\frac{2a}{r}-1$; (v) Parabola vertex $r^2(a^2-p^2)^2=a^2p^2(4a^2-3p^2)$.

$$p=r\sin\phi\;\left(\tan\phi=r\frac{d\theta}{dr}\right),\qquad \frac{1}{p^2}=\frac{1}{r^2}+\frac{1}{r^4}\left(\frac{dr}{d\theta}\right)^2,\qquad p=\frac{|xy_1-y|}{\sqrt{1+y_1^2}}$$
Conditions: Always check whether the pole $O$ is at the vertex $(0,0)$ or the focus $(a,0)$ of a parabola/conic.
Exam Application: Directly asked in 2010–11 Q4b, 2011–12 Q4b, 2012–13 Q4a, 2014–15 Q4b, 2018–19 Q3b, 2020–21 Q4b, 2021–22 Q3c, 2023–24 Q4a, 2024–25 Q3c, Q4a.
Complete Pedal Equation Derivations (1–5)
