  1. Rose Curve $r^n=a^n\cos(n\theta)$ (2021–22 Q3c, 2024–25 Q4a): Log-differentiate: $\cot\phi=\frac{1}{r}\frac{dr}{d\theta}=-\tan(n\theta)=\cot(\frac{\pi}{2}+n\theta) \implies p=r\sin(\frac{\pi}{2}+n\theta)=r\cos(n\theta)=r(r^n/a^n) \implies r^{n+1}=a^np$.
  2. Cardioid $r=a(1\pm\cos\theta)$ (2020–21 Q4b, 2023–24 Q4a): $p=r\cos(\theta/2)=r\sqrt{r/(2a)}$ (or $r\sin(\theta/2)=r\sqrt{r/(2a)}$) $\implies r^3=2ap^2$.
  3. Astroid $x=a\cos^3\theta, y=a\sin^3\theta$ (2010–11 Q4b, 2011–12 Q4b): Tangent $\frac{X}{a\cos\theta}+\frac{Y}{a\sin\theta}=1 \implies p=a\sin\theta\cos\theta$ and $r^2=a^2(1-3\sin^2\theta\cos^2\theta)=a^2-3p^2 \implies r^2+3p^2=a^2$.
  4. Ellipse Focus & Parabola Vertex (2012–13 Q4a, 2014–15 Q4b, 2018–19 Q3b): For $\frac{l}{r}=1+e\cos\theta$, $\frac{1}{p^2}=\frac{1}{r^2}+\frac{e^2\sin^2\theta}{l^2}=\frac{2a}{b^2r}-\frac{1}{b^2} \implies \frac{b^2}{p^2}=\frac{2a}{r}-1$. For $y^2=4ax$ at $(0,0)$, $x=at^2, y=2at \implies t^2=\frac{p^2}{a^2-p^2}$ in $r^2=a^2t^2(t^2+4) \implies r^2(a^2-p^2)^2=a^2p^2(4a^2-3p^2)$.
Curvature Definitions, Ellipse Semi-Latus Rectum Proof & Parabola Focal Chord Proof THEOREM

Definitions (2018–19 Q4b, 2023–24 Q4b, 2024–25 Q4b): Curvature $\kappa = |d\psi/ds|$ is the rate of change of the tangent inclination angle $\psi$ w.r.t. arc length $s$. Radius of Curvature $\rho = 1/\kappa$.
Proof 1 (Ellipse $\frac{x^2}{a^2}+\frac{y^2}{b^2}=1$ at Axes): At major axis $(\pm a,0)$, $\rho_{\text{major}}=\frac{b^2}{a}$ (equals the semi-latus rectum, maximum curvature $\kappa_{\max}=\frac{a}{b^2}$); at minor axis $(0,\pm b)$, $\rho_{\text{minor}}=\frac{a^2}{b}$ (minimum curvature $\kappa_{\min}=\frac{b}{a^2}$).
Proof 2 (Parabola $y^2=4ax$ Focal Chord, 2023–24 Q4b): If $\rho_1, \rho_2$ are radii of curvature at the ends of a focal chord, $\rho_1^{-2/3}+\rho_2^{-2/3}=(2a)^{-2/3}$.

$$\kappa=\left|\frac{d\psi}{ds}\right|,\quad \rho(t)_{\text{ellipse}}=\frac{(a^2\sin^2 t+b^2\cos^2 t)^{3/2}}{ab},\quad \rho_{\text{major}}=\frac{b^2}{a},\quad \rho_{\text{minor}}=\frac{a^2}{b},\quad \rho_1^{-2/3}+\rho_2^{-2/3}=(2a)^{-2/3}$$
Conditions: For a focal chord of $y^2=4ax$ through $(a,0)$ joining $(at_1^2,2at_1)$ and $(at_2^2,2at_2)$, the parameters satisfy $t_1t_2=-1$.
Exam Application: Directly asked in 2011–12 Q4a, 2018–19 Q4b, 2021–22 Q4b, 2023–24 Q4b, 2024–25 Q4b.
Complete Proofs for Ellipse Axes & Parabola Focal Chord
  1. Ellipse Proof (2011–12 Q4a, 2018–19 Q4b, 2021–22 Q4b, 2024–25 Q4b): With $x=a\cos t, y=b\sin t$, $x'y''-y'x''=ab(\sin^2 t+\cos^2 t)=ab$, so $\rho(t)=\frac{(a^2\sin^2 t+b^2\cos^2 t)^{3/2}}{ab}$. At $t=0,\pi$ ($\pm a,0$), $\rho_{\text{major}}=\frac{(b^2)^{3/2}}{ab}=\frac{b^2}{a}$ (semi-latus rectum). At $t=\frac{\pi}{2},\frac{3\pi}{2}$ ($0,\pm b$), $\rho_{\text{minor}}=\frac{(a^2)^{3/2}}{ab}=\frac{a^2}{b}$.
  2. Parabola Focal Chord Proof (2023–24 Q4b): For $(at^2,2at)$ on $y^2=4ax$, $x'=2at, y'=2a, x''=2a, y''=0 \implies \rho(t)=\frac{(4a^2t^2+4a^2)^{3/2}}{4a^2}=2a(1+t^2)^{3/2} \implies \rho^{-2/3}=(2a)^{-2/3}\frac{1}{1+t^2}$. Since $t_1t_2=-1 \implies t_2^2=1/t_1^2$, summing gives $\rho_1^{-2/3}+\rho_2^{-2/3}=(2a)^{-2/3}\left(\frac{1}{1+t_1^2}+\frac{t_1^2}{t_1^2+1}\right)=(2a)^{-2/3}$.
Complete Catalogue of 2010–2025 BUET Applied Optimization Problems THEOREM

Applied Optimization Protocol: Express the target quantity $Q$ in terms of a single variable $x$ using the geometric/cost constraint, solve $\frac{dQ}{dx}=0$ for stationary points, and verify $\frac{d^2Q}{dx^2}<0$ (for maximum) or $\frac{d^2Q}{dx^2}>0$ (for minimum).

$$h_{\text{cone in sph}}=\frac{4}{3}a,\quad y_{\text{cyl in cone}}=\frac{1}{3}h,\quad h_{\text{Norman}}=r=\frac{P}{\pi+4},\quad x_{\text{sq in wire }l}=\frac{\pi l}{\pi+4}$$
Conditions: Check boundary endpoints when minimizing total area (e.g. wire cut into square and circle in 2023–24 Q3c).
Exam Application: Covers every Q3a/Q3b/Q3c optimization problem from 2010–11 to 2024–25.
All 12 Past-Paper Optimization Solutions (2010–2025)
  1. 2010–11 Q3a & 2011–12 Q3a: Fenced plot $6x+4y=6000$, max $A=xy \implies 6x=4y=3000 \implies x=500\text{ ft}, y=750\text{ ft}$. Closed box $V=x^2h=200\text{ m}^3$, min $C=6x^2+6xh \implies x=2\sqrt[3]{25}\text{ m}, h=2x, C_{\min}=180\sqrt[3]{5}\text{ Tk}$.
  2. 2012–13 Q3b & 2013–14 Q3a: Norman window $P=2h+2r+\pi r=25\text{ ft} \implies h=r=\frac{25}{\pi+4}\text{ ft}$ (width $2r=\frac{50}{\pi+4}\text{ ft}$). Max cylinder in cone $(h,\alpha)$: $y=\frac{1}{3}h, r=\frac{2}{3}h\tan\alpha, V_{\max}=\frac{4}{27}\pi h^3\tan^2\alpha$.
  3. 2014–15 Q3a, 2016–17 Q3b & 2018–19 Q3a: Closed can $V=\pi r^2h=1000\text{ cm}^3$, min $S \implies h=2r, r=\sqrt[3]{500/\pi}\text{ cm}$. Max rectangle in circle $R$: square of side $\sqrt{2}R$. Sector wire $2r+r\theta=20\text{ m} \implies \theta=2\text{ rad}, r=5\text{ m}, A_{\max}=25\text{ m}^2$.
  4. 2019–20 Q3b, 2020–21 Q3b & 2021–22 Q3a: Max cone in sphere $a$: $h=\frac{4}{3}a, V_{\max}=\frac{32}{81}\pi a^3=\frac{8}{27}V_{\text{sph}}$. Open cylinder $V=24\pi\text{ in}^3$ (bottom $3\times$ side): $h=3r \implies r=2\text{ in}, h=6\text{ in}$. Box $4x+h=108\text{ in}$: $x=18\text{ in}, h=36\text{ in}, V_{\max}=11,664\text{ in}^3$.
  5. 2023–24 Q3c & 2024–25 Q3b: Wire $l$ into square ($x$) and circle ($l-x$), min area $A=(x/4)^2+\pi(\frac{l-x}{2\pi})^2 \implies x_{\text{sq}}=\frac{\pi l}{\pi+4}, l-x_{\text{cir}}=\frac{4l}{\pi+4}$. Page with print area $xy=42\text{ in}^2$, min $(x+3)(y+2) \implies x=3\sqrt{7}\text{ in}, y=2\sqrt{7}\text{ in}$, page $(3\sqrt{7}+3)\times(2\sqrt{7}+2)$.
Integral calculus, Beta–Gamma functions & improper integralsFreq: 5/50/9
Fundamental Theorem of Calculus & Leibnitz Rule (Differentiation Under Integral Sign) THEOREM

Fundamental Theorem of Calculus (Parts I & II): (Part I) If $f(x)$ is continuous on $[a,b]$, then the area function $F(x) = \int_a^x f(t)\,dt$ is differentiable on $(a,b)$ and $F'(x) = \frac{d}{dx}\int_a^x f(t)\,dt = f(x)$. (Part II — Newton–Leibnitz Formula) If $G(x)$ is any antiderivative (primitive) of $f(x)$ on $[a,b]$ ($G'(x) = f(x)$), then $\int_a^b f(x)\,dx = G(b) - G(a)$.
Leibnitz's Rule (Differentiation Under the Integral Sign): If $F(\alpha) = \int_{a(\alpha)}^{b(\alpha)} f(x, \alpha)\,dx$ where $f(x,\alpha)$ and $\partial f/\partial \alpha$ are continuous and $a(\alpha), b(\alpha)$ are differentiable, then:

$$\frac{d}{dx}\int_a^x f(t)\,dt = f(x),\quad \int_a^b f(x)\,dx = G(b)-G(a);\qquad \frac{d}{d\alpha}\int_{a(\alpha)}^{b(\alpha)}f(x,\alpha)\,dx = \int_{a(\alpha)}^{b(\alpha)}\frac{\partial f}{\partial \alpha}\,dx + f(b(\alpha),\alpha)b'(\alpha) - f(a(\alpha),\alpha)a'(\alpha)$$
Conditions: If the limits $a, b$ are constants, the boundary terms vanish and $F'(\alpha) = \int_a^b (\partial f/\partial\alpha)\,dx$. Also if $G'(x)=g(x)$, Integration by Parts $\int u\,dv = uv - \int v\,du$ follows directly from differentiating $u(x)G(x)$ (asked in BUET 2024–25 Q.5c).
Exam Application: Used to evaluate integrals like $\int_0^1 \dfrac{x^\alpha - 1}{\ln x}\,dx = \ln(1+\alpha)$ and $\int_0^\infty e^{-x^2}\cos(\alpha x)\,dx = \dfrac{\sqrt{\pi}}{2}e^{-\alpha^2/4}$.
Beta–Gamma Relation, Legendre Duplication & Euler Reflection Formula THEOREM

Definitions: Euler's Gamma function is $\Gamma(n) = \int_0^\infty x^{n-1}e^{-x}\,dx$ ($n>0$, satisfying $\Gamma(n+1)=n\Gamma(n)$ and $\Gamma(1/2)=\sqrt{\pi}$) and Beta function is $B(m,n) = \int_0^1 x^{m-1}(1-x)^{n-1}\,dx$ ($m>0, n>0$).
Beta–Gamma Relation: $B(m, n) = \dfrac{\Gamma(m)\Gamma(n)}{\Gamma(m+n)}$.
Euler's Reflection Formula: For $0 < p < 1$, $\Gamma(p)\Gamma(1-p) = \dfrac{\pi}{\sin(p\pi)}$.
Legendre's Duplication Formula: For $m > 0$, $\Gamma(m)\Gamma\!\left(m+\frac12\right) = \dfrac{\sqrt{\pi}}{2^{2m-1}}\Gamma(2m)$.

$$B(m,n)=\frac{\Gamma(m)\Gamma(n)}{\Gamma(m+n)}=2\int_0^{\pi/2}\sin^{2m-1}\theta\cos^{2n-1}\theta\,d\theta,\qquad \Gamma(p)\Gamma(1-p)=\frac{\pi}{\sin(p\pi)},\qquad \Gamma(2m)=\frac{2^{2m-1}}{\sqrt{\pi}}\Gamma(m)\Gamma\!\left(m+\frac12\right)$$
Conditions: $m, n > 0$; reflection formula requires $0 < p < 1$. For negative non-integers, use $\Gamma(n) = \frac{\Gamma(n+1)}{n}$ or $\Gamma(p)\Gamma(-p) = \frac{-\pi}{p\sin(p\pi)}$ (tested in 2019–20 Q.7b).
Exam Application: Converts any trigonometric integral $\int_0^{\pi/2} \sin^p\theta \cos^q\theta\,d\theta = \dfrac{\Gamma(\frac{p+1}{2})\Gamma(\frac{q+1}{2})}{2\Gamma(\frac{p+q+2}{2})}$ or algebraic integral $\int_0^\infty \dfrac{x^{m-1}}{(1+x)^{m+n}}\,dx = B(m,n)$ immediately. Legendre duplication proof was asked in 2024–25 Q.7b.
Proof & derivation steps
  1. Start with $\Gamma(m)\Gamma(n) = \int_0^\infty u^{m-1}e^{-u}du \int_0^\infty v^{n-1}e^{-v}dv$.
  2. Put $u = x^2, v = y^2$ to get $4\int_0^\infty\!\int_0^\infty x^{2m-1}y^{2n-1}e^{-(x^2+y^2)}dx\,dy$.
  3. Switch to polar coordinates $x = r\cos\theta, y = r\sin\theta, dx\,dy = r\,dr\,d\theta$: factors into $\left(2\int_0^\infty r^{2(m+n)-1}e^{-r^2}dr\right)\left(2\int_0^{\pi/2}\sin^{2n-1}\theta\cos^{2m-1}\theta\,d\theta\right) = \Gamma(m+n)B(m,n)$.
  4. For Legendre Duplication: equate $B(m,m) = \dfrac{[\Gamma(m)]^2}{\Gamma(2m)} = 2\int_0^{\pi/2}\sin^{2m-1}\theta\cos^{2m-1}\theta\,d\theta = 2^{2-2m}\int_0^{\pi/2}\sin^{2m-1}(2\theta)\,d\theta = 2^{1-2m}B(m, 1/2)$.
Definite Integral as the Limit of a Sum & Infinite Product Limits THEOREM

Riemann Limit of a Sum: For any continuous function $f(x)$ on $[a, b]$ partitioned into $n$ equal subintervals of width $h = \frac{b-a}{n}$:
Infinite Products via Definite Integrals: For a product $P = \lim_{n\to\infty} \prod_{r=1}^n a_{n,r}$, take the natural logarithm $\ln P = \lim_{n\to\infty} \sum_{r=1}^n \ln a_{n,r}$, factor out $\frac{1}{n}$, and replace $\frac{r}{n}$ by $x \in [0, 1]$.

$$\int_a^b f(x)\,dx = \lim_{n\to\infty} h\sum_{r=0}^{n-1} f(a+rh),\quad h=\frac{b-a}{n};\qquad \ln P = \lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{r=1}^{n}\phi\!\left(\frac{r}{n}\right) = \int_0^1 \phi(x)\,dx$$
Conditions: For $\lim_{n\to\infty} \prod_{r=1}^n \left(2 + \frac{r^2}{n^2}\right)^{r/n^2}$ (tested in 2013–14 Q.6b, 2021–22 Q.6b): $\ln P = \lim_{n\to\infty} \frac{1}{n}\sum_{r=1}^n \frac{r}{n}\ln\left(2 + \frac{r^2}{n^2}\right) = \int_0^1 x\ln(2+x^2)\,dx$.
Exam Application: For $\int_a^b \sin x\,dx$ or $\int_a^b \sec^2(2x)\,dx$ by summation (2010–11 Q.6b, 2023–24 Q.7b): use the trigonometric identity $\sum_{r=0}^{n-1}\sin(a+rh) = \frac{\sin(nh/2)}{\sin(h/2)}\sin\left(a+\frac{(n-1)h}{2}\right)$.
King's Property of Definite Integrals & Integral Triangle Inequality THEOREM

King's Property: $\int_a^b f(x)\,dx = \int_a^b f(a+b-x)\,dx$, and in particular $\int_0^a f(x)\,dx = \int_0^a f(a-x)\,dx$.
Integral Triangle Inequality: For any integrable function $f(x)$ on $[a,b]$ ($a<b$), the absolute value of the integral is at most the integral of the absolute value: $\left|\int_a^b f(x)\,dx\right| \le \int_a^b |f(x)|\,dx$.

$$\int_a^b f(x)\,dx = \int_a^b f(a+b-x)\,dx,\qquad \int_0^a f(x)\,dx = \int_0^a f(a-x)\,dx,\qquad \left|\int_a^b f(x)\,dx\right| \le \int_a^b |f(x)|\,dx$$
Conditions: To prove $\int_0^a f(x)\,dx = \int_0^a f(a-x)\,dx$ (asked in 2024–25 Q.6b): substitute $x = a - t \implies dx = -dt$, flip limits $t: a \to 0$ to $0 \to a$, and rename dummy variable $t \to x$.
Exam Application: Immediate exam applications: $\int_0^{\pi/2}\ln(\tan x + \cot x)\,dx = -\int_0^{\pi/2}\ln(\sin x\cos x)\,dx = \pi\ln 2$ (2011–12 Q.6b, 2024–25 Q.6b) and $\int_0^\pi \frac{x\tan x}{\sec x + \tan x}\,dx = \frac{\pi}{2}(\pi - 2)$ (2012–13 Q.6c, 2014–15 Q.7a).
Improper Integrals: Convergence Definitions, p-Tests & μ-Comparison Tests THEOREM

First Kind (Infinite Interval): $\int_a^\infty f(x)\,dx = \lim_{R\to\infty}\int_a^R f(x)\,dx$.
Second Kind (Unbounded Integrand at $x = c \in [a,b]$): $\int_a^b f(x)\,dx = \lim_{\varepsilon\to 0^+}\int_a^{c-\varepsilon}f(x)\,dx + \lim_{\delta\to 0^+}\int_{c+\delta}^b f(x)\,dx$ (both limits must exist finitely and independently!).

$$\int_1^\infty \frac{dx}{x^p}\text{ converges} \iff p > 1;\qquad \int_a^b \frac{dx}{(x-a)^q}\text{ converges} \iff q < 1$$
Conditions: $\mu$-Test at $\infty$: If $\lim_{x\to\infty} x^\mu f(x) = L$ (finite $\neq 0$), then $\int_a^\infty f(x)\,dx$ converges if $\mu > 1$ and diverges if $\mu \le 1$. $\mu$-Test at singularity $x=a$: If $\lim_{x\to a^+} (x-a)^\mu f(x) = L \neq 0$, converges if $\mu < 1$ and diverges if $\mu \ge 1$.
Exam Application: Watch out for interior singularities like $\int_0^4 \frac{dx}{(x-1)^2}$ (tested in 2019–20 Q.7c): split at $x = 1$; since $q = 2 \ge 1$, the integral diverges (applying $F(4)-F(0)$ blindly gives a wrong negative answer!).
Antiderivative Definition, Constant of Integration (MVT Proof) & Integration by Parts Proof (2024–25 Q5c) DEFINITION

1. Definition of Antiderivative (2024–25 Q5c): A function $F(x)$ is an antiderivative of $f(x)$ on an interval $I$ if $F'(x)=f(x)$ for all $x\in I$. Example: $F(x)=\frac{1}{3}x^3$ (or $\frac{1}{3}x^3+5$) is an antiderivative of $f(x)=x^2$ on $(-\infty,\infty)$.
2. Constant of Integration ($C$) & Why It Is Necessary: Since $\frac{d}{dx}[C]=0$, $F(x)+C$ has derivative $f(x)$ for any constant $C$. Conversely, by the Zero-Derivative Corollary of MVT, if $G'(x)=F'(x)=f(x)$ on $I$, then $[G(x)-F(x)]'=0 \implies G(x)=F(x)+C$ (geometrically, a family of vertical-translation parallel curves).
3. Proof of Integration by Parts: Follows directly from integrating the product rule $\frac{d}{dx}[f(x)G(x)]=f(x)G'(x)+G(x)f'(x)$.

$$F'(x)=\frac{d}{dx}[F(x)]=f(x)\;\forall x\in I,\qquad \int f(x)\,dx=F(x)+C,\qquad \int u\,dv=uv-\int v\,du$$
Conditions: $u=f(x)$ and $v=G(x)$ must be differentiable functions on $I$.
Exam Application: All three parts were asked together for 15 marks in BUET MATH 157 2024–25 Q5c.
Complete Proof of Integration by Parts (2024–25 Q5c)
  1. Let $u=f(x)$ and $v=G(x)$ be differentiable functions of $x$, where $G'(x)=g(x)$, so $du=f'(x)\,dx$ and $dv=G'(x)\,dx=g(x)\,dx$.
  2. By the product rule of differentiation: $\frac{d}{dx}[f(x)G(x)] = f(x)G'(x) + G(x)f'(x) \implies f(x)G'(x) = \frac{d}{dx}[f(x)G(x)] - G(x)f'(x)$.
  3. Integrate both sides w.r.t. $x$: $\int f(x)G'(x)\,dx = f(x)G(x) - \int G(x)f'(x)\,dx \implies \int u\,dv = uv - \int v\,du$.
Master Reduction Formulas & Wallis's Sine/Cosine Formula Proofs (2010–2025) THEOREM

Complete Catalogue of Tested Reduction Formulas (2010–2025):
1. $\int\sin^n x\,dx$ & Wallis's Formula (2021–22 Q7a, 2024–25 Q6a), e.g. $\int_0^{\pi/2}\sin^5 x\,dx = \frac{4}{5}\cdot\frac{2}{3}\cdot 1 = \frac{8}{15}$.
2. $\int\sin^m x\cos^n x\,dx = \frac{\sin^{m+1}x\cos^{n-1}x}{m+n}+\frac{n-1}{m+n}\int\sin^m x\cos^{n-2}x\,dx$ (2023–24 Q6a).
3. $I_n=\int x\sin^n x\,dx = -\frac{x\sin^{n-1}x\cos x}{n}+\frac{\sin^n x}{n^2}+\frac{n-1}{n}I_{n-2}$ and $J_n=\int x\cos^n x\,dx$ (2012–13 Q6a, 2016–17 Q6a, 2020–21 Q6a).
4. $I_n=\int x^n\cos(mx)\,dx = \frac{x^n\sin(mx)}{m}+\frac{nx^{n-1}\cos(mx)}{m^2}-\frac{n(n-1)}{m^2}I_{n-2}$ (2011–12 Q6a, 2013–14 Q6a).
5. $I_n=\int e^{ax}\cos^n x\,dx = \frac{e^{ax}\cos^{n-1}x(a\cos x+n\sin x)}{a^2+n^2}+\frac{n(n-1)}{a^2+n^2}I_{n-2}$ (2014–15 Q6a).
6. $I_n=\int\frac{x^n\,dx}{\sqrt{ax^2+bx+c}} \implies naI_n+\frac{2n-1}{2}bI_{n-1}+(n-1)cI_{n-2}=x^{n-1}\sqrt{ax^2+bx+c}$ (2010–11 Q6a).
7. $I_n=\int x^n e^x\,dx = x^n e^x - nI_{n-1} \implies I_3 = e^x(x^3-3x^2+6x-6)+C$ (2019–20 Q5b).

$$I_n=\int\sin^n x\,dx=-\frac{1}{n}\sin^{n-1}x\cos x+\frac{n-1}{n}I_{n-2};\qquad W_n=\int_0^{\pi/2}\sin^n x\,dx=\begin{cases}\frac{n-1}{n}\cdot\frac{n-3}{n-2}\cdots\frac{2}{3}\cdot 1,&n\text{ odd}\\\frac{n-1}{n}\cdot\frac{n-3}{n-2}\cdots\frac{1}{2}\cdot\frac{\pi}{2},&n\text{ even}\end{cases}$$
Conditions: In Wallis's formula, the integrated boundary term $\left[-\frac{1}{n}\sin^{n-1}x\cos x\right]_0^{\pi/2}=0$ vanishes for all $n>1$.
Exam Application: Question 6a in almost every BUET MATH 157 exam from 2010–11 to 2024–25.
Derivation of sin^n x Reduction & Wallis Formula
  1. Write $I_n = \int \sin^{n-1}x\sin x\,dx$. Integrate by parts with $u=\sin^{n-1}x$ and $dv=\sin x\,dx$ ($v=-\cos x$): $I_n = -\sin^{n-1}x\cos x + (n-1)\int \sin^{n-2}x(1-\sin^2 x)\,dx = -\sin^{n-1}x\cos x + (n-1)I_{n-2} - (n-1)I_n$.
  2. Add $(n-1)I_n$ to both sides and divide by $n$: $I_n = -\frac{1}{n}\sin^{n-1}x\cos x + \frac{n-1}{n}I_{n-2}$.
  3. Apply limits $[0,\pi/2]$: $W_n = \frac{n-1}{n}W_{n-2}$ with base cases $W_1 = \int_0^{\pi/2}\sin x\,dx = 1$ (odd $n$) and $W_0 = \int_0^{\pi/2}1\,dx = \frac{\pi}{2}$ (even $n$).
Complete Proofs of Γ(1/2) = √π, Legendre Duplication & Classic Beta-Gamma Identities (2011–2025) THEOREM

1. Negative Half-Integer & Reflection Values: $\Gamma(-1/2)=-2\sqrt{\pi}$, $\Gamma(-3/2)=\frac{4}{3}\sqrt{\pi}$, $\Gamma(-11/2)=-\frac{64}{10,395}\sqrt{\pi}$ (2021–22 Q7b, 2023–24 Q6c); $\Gamma(5/3)\Gamma(-5/3)=\frac{2\pi\sqrt{3}}{5}$ (2019–20 Q7b); $\Gamma(5/4)\Gamma(-5/4)=\frac{4\pi\sqrt{2}}{5}$ (2021–22 Q7b-v).
2. Three Classic Beta-Gamma Integral Identities: (i) $\text{B}(p+1,q)+\text{B}(p,q+1)=\text{B}(p,q)$ (2013–14 Q7c-i); (ii) $\left(\int_0^1\frac{x^2\,dx}{\sqrt{1-x^4}}\right)\left(\int_0^1\frac{dx}{\sqrt{1+x^4}}\right)=\frac{\pi}{4\sqrt{2}}$ (2011–12 Q7a); (iii) $\int_0^{\pi/2}\sqrt{\tan x}\,dx = \frac{\pi}{\sqrt{2}}$ (2016–17 Q7c-ii).

$$\Gamma\!\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)=\sqrt{\pi},\quad \Gamma(m)\Gamma\!\left(m+\frac{1}{2}\right)=\frac{\sqrt{\pi}}{2^{2m-1}}\Gamma(2m),\quad \text{B}(p+1,q)+\text{B}(p,q+1)=\text{B}(p,q),\quad \int_0^{\pi/2}\sqrt{\tan x}\,dx=\frac{\pi}{\sqrt{2}}$$
Conditions: $\text{B}(m,n)=\frac{\Gamma(m)\Gamma(n)}{\Gamma(m+n)}$ holds for $m>0, n>0$; reflection $\Gamma(z)\Gamma(1-z)=\frac{\pi}{\sin(\pi z)}$ holds for non-integer $z$.
Exam Application: Directly asked in 2011–12 Q7a, 2013–14 Q7c, 2016–17 Q7c, 2019–20 Q7a,b, 2020–21 Q7a,b, 2021–22 Q7b, 2023–24 Q6c, Q7c, 2024–25 Q7b,c.
Complete Proofs of Duplication Formula & Identities 1–3
  1. Legendre's Duplication Proof (2024–25 Q7b): In $\int_0^{\pi/2}\sin^p\theta\cos^q\theta\,d\theta = \frac{\Gamma((p+1)/2)\Gamma((q+1)/2)}{2\Gamma((p+q+2)/2)}$, set $p=q=2m-1$ to get $\int_0^{\pi/2}\sin^{2m-1}\theta\cos^{2m-1}\theta\,d\theta = \frac{[\Gamma(m)]^2}{2\Gamma(2m)}$. On the other hand, using $\sin\theta\cos\theta=\frac{1}{2}\sin 2\theta$ and $\phi=2\theta$ gives $\frac{1}{2^{2m-1}}\int_0^{\pi/2}\sin^{2m-1}\phi\cos^0\phi\,d\phi = \frac{1}{2^{2m-1}}\frac{\Gamma(m)\Gamma(1/2)}{2\Gamma(m+1/2)}$. Equating both sides gives $\Gamma(m)\Gamma(m+1/2)=\frac{\sqrt{\pi}}{2^{2m-1}}\Gamma(2m)$.
  2. Identity 1 (2013–14 Q7c-i): $\text{B}(p+1,q)+\text{B}(p,q+1)=\frac{\Gamma(p+1)\Gamma(q)+\Gamma(p)\Gamma(q+1)}{\Gamma(p+q+1)}=\frac{\Gamma(p)\Gamma(q)(p+q)}{(p+q)\Gamma(p+q)}=\text{B}(p,q)$.
  3. Identity 2 (2011–12 Q7a): In $I_1=\int_0^1\frac{x^2\,dx}{\sqrt{1-x^4}}$, put $x=\sqrt{\sin\theta} \implies I_1=\frac{\Gamma(3/4)\Gamma(1/4)}{4\Gamma(1)}$. In $I_2=\int_0^1\frac{dx}{\sqrt{1+x^4}}$, put $x=\sqrt{\tan\phi} \implies I_2=\frac{\Gamma(1/4)\Gamma(1/2)}{4\sqrt{2}\Gamma(3/4)}$. Multiplying and using $\Gamma(1/4)\Gamma(3/4)=\frac{\pi}{\sin(\pi/4)}=\pi\sqrt{2}$ gives $I_1 I_2 = \frac{\pi}{4\sqrt{2}}$.
  4. Identity 3 (2016–17 Q7c-ii): $\int_0^{\pi/2}\sqrt{\tan x}\,dx = \int_0^{\pi/2}\sin^{1/2}x\cos^{-1/2}x\,dx = \frac{\Gamma(3/4)\Gamma(1/4)}{2\Gamma(1)} = \frac{1}{2}\frac{\pi}{\sin(\pi/4)} = \frac{\pi}{\sqrt{2}}$.
Quadrature, Rectification, Volume & Surface of Revolution Master Atlas (2010–2025) THEOREM

Master Results for All 9 Recurring BUET Exam Curves (2010–2025):
1. Astroid $x^{2/3}+y^{2/3}=a^{2/3}$: Area $=\frac{3}{8}\pi a^2$ (or $\frac{3}{8}\pi ab$), $V_x=\frac{32}{105}\pi ab^2$, $S_x=\frac{12}{5}\pi a^2$.
2. Cardioid $r=a(1+\cos\theta)$: Area $=\frac{3}{2}\pi a^2$, Perimeter $=8a$, $V_{\text{init}}=\frac{8}{3}\pi a^3$, $S_{\text{init}}=\frac{32}{5}\pi a^2$.
3. Lemniscate $r^2=a^2\cos 2\theta$: Area (2 loops) $=a^2$, $S_x=2\pi a^2(2-\sqrt{2})$, $V_x=\frac{\pi a^3}{6}[\frac{3}{\sqrt{2}}\ln(\sqrt{2}+1)-1]$.
4. Cycloid Arch $x=a(\theta-\sin\theta), y=a(1-\cos\theta)$: Area $=3\pi a^2$, Arc $=8a$, $V_x=5\pi^2 a^3$, $V_y=6\pi^3 a^3$.
5. Trisectrix Loop $y^2(a+x)=x^2(3a-x)$ ($0\le x\le 3a$): Area $=3\sqrt{3}\,a^2$, $V_x=\pi a^3(8\ln 2-3)$.
6. Right Strophoid Loop $y^2(a-x)=x^2(a+x)$: Area $=(2-\frac{\pi}{2})a^2$.
7. Witch of Agnesi $y(a^2+x^2)=a^3$: Area $=\pi a^2$, $V_x=\frac{\pi^2 a^3}{2}$.
8. Circle $r=2a\cos\theta$ about Initial Line: Sphere of radius $a$, $S=4\pi a^2$.
9. Common Area of $y^2=ax$ & $x^2+y^2=4ax$: $A=a^2(3\sqrt{3}+\frac{4\pi}{3})$.

$$A=\int_a^b y\,dx=\frac{1}{2}\int_\alpha^\beta r^2\,d\theta,\quad s=\int\sqrt{1+y_1^2}\,dx,\quad V_x=\pi\int_a^b y^2\,dx=\frac{2\pi}{3}\int_\alpha^\beta r^3\sin\theta\,d\theta,\quad S_x=2\pi\int y\,ds$$
Conditions: Use symmetry carefully: multiply single-quadrant area/arc by $4$ for astroid/lemniscate, and multiply upper-half surface/volume by $2$ only when revolving about an axis that does not cross the interior of the loop.
Exam Application: Question 8 (and Q7c) of every BUET MATH 157 exam from 2010–11 to 2024–25.
Complex numbers, analytic functions & conformal mappingFreq: 5/50/11
Cauchy–Riemann (C–R) Equations & Sufficient Condition for Analyticity THEOREM

Analyticity: A complex function $f(z) = u(x,y) + i v(x,y)$ is analytic (holomorphic) at $z_0$ if it is differentiable in an open neighbourhood of $z_0$; it is entire if analytic throughout $\mathbb{C}$.
Necessary & Sufficient Condition: $f(z) = u(x,y) + i v(x,y)$ is differentiable at $z_0 = x_0 + i y_0$ if and only if the first partial derivatives $u_x, u_y, v_x, v_y$ exist in a neighbourhood of $z_0$, are continuous at $z_0$ (making $u, v$ real-differentiable), and satisfy the Cauchy–Riemann equations at $z_0$:

$$\text{Cartesian: }u_x=v_y,\quad u_y=-v_x,\quad f'(z)=u_x+iv_x;\qquad \text{Polar: }u_r=\frac1r v_\theta,\quad v_r=-\frac1r u_\theta,\quad f'(z)=e^{-i\theta}(u_r+iv_r)$$
Conditions: Satisfaction of C–R equations alone is necessary but NOT sufficient unless the partial derivatives are also continuous! Classic counterexample: $f(z) = \bar{z}^2/z$ or $\sqrt{|xy|}$ at $z=0$ satisfies C–R at $(0,0)$ yet $f'(0)$ does not exist because $\lim_{z\to 0}\frac{f(z)-f(0)}{z}$ along $y=mx$ depends on slope $m$.
Exam Application: Also proves that if $f(z) = u + iv$ is analytic, then $u$ and $v$ are harmonic ($\nabla^2 u = \nabla^2 v = 0$) and their level curves $u(x,y)=c_1, v(x,y)=c_2$ intersect orthogonally ($\nabla u \cdot \nabla v = 0$) wherever $f'(z) \neq 0$.
Proof & derivation steps
  1. Take $\Delta z = \Delta x \to 0$ along the real axis: $f'(z) = u_x + i v_x$.
  2. Take $\Delta z = i\Delta y \to 0$ along the imaginary axis: $f'(z) = \frac{1}{i}(u_y + i v_y) = v_y - i u_y$.
  3. Equating real and imaginary parts of the two limits yields $u_x = v_y$ and $u_y = -v_x$.
Milne–Thomson Method & Conformal Mapping Theorem THEOREM

Milne–Thomson Method: If a harmonic function $u(x,y)$ (or $v(x,y)$) is given as the real (or imaginary) part of an analytic function $f(z) = u(x,y) + i v(x,y)$, then since $f'(z) = u_x(x,y) + i v_x(x,y) = u_x(x,y) - i u_y(x,y)$, setting $x = z$ and $y = 0$ directly reconstructs $f(z)$ by a single integration without finding the harmonic conjugate first.
Conformal Mapping Theorem: An analytic mapping $w = f(z)$ is conformal (preserves both the magnitude and sense/orientation of the angle between any two smooth intersecting curves) at every point $z_0$ where $f'(z_0) \neq 0$, with local magnification factor $|f'(z_0)|$ and rotation angle $\arg f'(z_0)$.

$$f(z)=\int\big[u_x(z,0)-i\,u_y(z,0)\big]dz+iC\quad(\text{given }u),\qquad f(z)=\int\big[v_y(z,0)+i\,v_x(z,0)\big]dz+C\quad(\text{given }v)$$
Conditions: $u(x,y)$ or $v(x,y)$ must first be verified to be harmonic ($\nabla^2 u = u_{xx} + u_{yy} = 0$).
Exam Application: Critical points of a mapping occur where $f'(z) = 0$; at a zero of $f'(z)$ of order $k-1$, angles between intersecting curves are multiplied by $k$. To find orthogonal trajectories of $u(x,y)=c_1$, find its harmonic conjugate $v(x,y)=c_2$!
Proof & derivation steps
  1. Let a smooth curve $C$ pass through $z_0$ with tangent angle $\alpha = \arg dz$. Under $w = f(z)$, $dw = f'(z_0)\,dz$, so $\arg dw = \arg f'(z_0) + \arg dz$.
  2. Thus every tangent vector at $z_0$ is rotated by the same fixed angle $\phi_0 = \arg f'(z_0)$ and scaled by $|f'(z_0)| \neq 0$, preserving the angle $\theta_2 - \theta_1$ between any two curves $C_1, C_2$.
Triangle Inequalities, De Moivre's Theorem & Roots of Complex Numbers THEOREM

Triangle Inequalities: For any complex numbers $z_1, z_2 \in \mathbb{C}$, the modulus of their sum is at most the sum of their moduli, and the modulus of their difference is at least the absolute difference of their moduli.
De Moivre's Theorem & $n$-th Roots: For any integer $n$, $(\cos\theta + i\sin\theta)^n = \cos(n\theta) + i\sin(n\theta)$. If $z = r(\cos\theta + i\sin\theta) = r e^{i\theta}$ ($r > 0$), then $z^{1/n}$ has $n$ distinct values spaced by $\frac{2\pi}{n}$ radians on the circle of radius $r^{1/n}$.

$$\big||z_1| - |z_2|\big| \le |z_1 \pm z_2| \le |z_1| + |z_2|;\qquad z_k = r^{1/n}\left[\cos\!\left(\frac{\theta + 2k\pi}{n}\right) + i\sin\!\left(\frac{\theta + 2k\pi}{n}\right)\right],\quad k = 0,1,\dots,n-1$$
Conditions: Proof of $|z_1+z_2| \le |z_1|+|z_2|$ (asked in 2017–18 Q.1a, 2023–24 Q.1a): $|z_1+z_2|^2 = (z_1+z_2)(\bar{z}_1+\bar{z}_2) = |z_1|^2 + |z_2|^2 + 2\operatorname{Re}(z_1\bar{z}_2) \le |z_1|^2 + |z_2|^2 + 2|z_1||z_2| = (|z_1|+|z_2|)^2$.
Exam Application: For $k=0$ with $\theta = \operatorname{Arg} z \in (-\pi, \pi]$, $z_0$ is called the principal root.
Complex Logarithm (ln z vs Ln z), Branch Points & Riemann Surface DEFINITION

Complex Logarithm: For $z = r e^{i(\theta + 2k\pi)} \neq 0$, the multi-valued logarithm is $\ln z = \ln r + i(\theta + 2k\pi)\ (k \in \mathbb{Z})$, and the principal value is $\operatorname{Ln} z = \ln |z| + i\operatorname{Arg} z$ where $-\pi < \operatorname{Arg} z \le \pi$.
Branch Point & Riemann Surface: A point $z_0$ is a branch point of a multi-valued function $f(z)$ if encircling $z_0$ once along a small closed loop does not return $f(z)$ to its starting value. For $\ln z$, encircling $z=0$ increases $\theta$ by $2\pi$, shifting $\ln z$ by $+2\pi i$; the Riemann surface for $\ln z$ is an infinite helicoidal stack of sheets cut along the negative real axis $(-\infty, 0]$.

$$\ln z = \ln|z| + i(\operatorname{Arg} z + 2k\pi),\qquad \operatorname{Ln} z = \ln|z| + i\operatorname{Arg} z\quad (-\pi < \operatorname{Arg} z \le \pi)$$
Conditions: Because $\operatorname{Arg}(z^2)$ must lie in $(-\pi, \pi]$, $\operatorname{Ln}(z^2) \neq 2\operatorname{Ln} z$ whenever $2\operatorname{Arg} z \notin (-\pi, \pi]$ (e.g. for $z = -1+i$, $\operatorname{Arg} z = 3\pi/4$, so $2\operatorname{Ln}(-1+i)$ has imaginary part $3\pi/2$ while $\operatorname{Ln}(-1+i)^2 = \operatorname{Ln}(-2i)$ has imaginary part $-\pi/2$).
Exam Application: Tested in BUET Math 159 2014–15 Q.1c, 2016–17 Q.2b, 2018–19 Q.1a, 2020–21 Q.2a, and 2022–23 Q.1a.
Bilinear (Möbius) Transformation & Cross-Ratio Invariance Theorem THEOREM

Bilinear (Möbius) Transformation: A transformation of the form $w = \frac{az+b}{cz+d}$ with $ad - bc \neq 0$ is a one-to-one conformal mapping of the extended complex plane $\mathbb{C}\cup\{\infty\}$ onto itself that maps circles and straight lines onto circles and straight lines, and preserves the cross-ratio of any four distinct points:

$$w = \frac{az+b}{cz+d}\ (ad-bc\neq 0) \iff z = \frac{-dw+b}{cw-a};\qquad \frac{(w-w_1)(w_2-w_3)}{(w-w_3)(w_2-w_1)} = \frac{(z-z_1)(z_2-z_3)}{(z-z_3)(z_2-z_1)}$$
Conditions: Fixed points of $w = f(z)$ are found by solving $z = \frac{az+b}{cz+d} \iff cz^2 + (d-a)z - b = 0$.
Exam Application: To find the image of a disk $|z| < r$ or half-plane $\operatorname{Re} z \ge 0$ (e.g. 2010–11 Q.3b, 2012–13 Q.1d, 2023–24 Q.1c): solve for $z$ in terms of $w$, substitute into the boundary equation, and test one interior point (like $z=0$).
Modulus, Conjugate & Argument Short Proofs (|z+ia|=|z+ib|, |z²−1|=|z²|+1, z̄₁+iz̄₂=0) THEOREM

Standard Modulus & Argument Short Proofs:
(1) Bisector Identity (2010–11 Q.1a): If $a,b\in\mathbb{R}$ ($a\ne b$) and $|z+ia|=|z+ib|$, squaring via $|w|^2=w\bar{w}$ gives $(z+ia)(\bar{z}-ia)=(z+ib)(\bar{z}-ib)\implies z-\bar{z}=-i(a+b)$ (so $\operatorname{Im}z=-\frac{a+b}{2}$).
(2) Purely Imaginary Locus (2021–22 Q.1b): $|z^2-1|=|z^2|+1\iff (z^2-1)(\bar{z}^2-1)=(|z|^2+1)^2\iff z^2+\bar{z}^2=-2z\bar{z}\iff (z+\bar{z})^2=0\iff z+\bar{z}=0$ ($z=iy$ lies on the imaginary axis).
(3) Modulus Bound on Unit Disk (2016–17 Q.1b): For $|z|\le 1$, $|\operatorname{Re}(2+\bar{z}+z^3)|\le |2+\bar{z}+z^3|\le 2+|\bar{z}|+|z|^3\le 2+1+1=4$.
(4) Argument System (2022–23 Q.2c): $\bar{z}_1+i\bar{z}_2=0\implies z_1=iz_2\implies \arg z_1-\arg z_2=\frac{\pi}{2}$; combined with $\arg(z_1z_2)=\arg z_1+\arg z_2=\pi$, adding yields $2\arg z_1=\frac{3\pi}{2}\implies \arg z_1=\frac{3\pi}{4}$.

$$|z+ia|=|z+ib|\ (a\ne b)\implies z-\bar{z}=-i(a+b);\qquad |z^2-1|=|z^2|+1\implies z+\bar{z}=0;\qquad \bar{z}_1+i\bar{z}_2=0,\ \arg(z_1z_2)=\pi\implies\arg z_1=\frac{3\pi}{4}$$
Conditions: Always expand $|w|^2 = w\bar{w}$ rather than substituting $z=x+iy$ when proving algebraic identities in $z$ and $\bar{z}$.
Exam Application: Tested in BUET Math 159 2010–11 Q.1a, 2016–17 Q.1b, 2021–22 Q.1b, and 2022–23 Q.2c.
Proof & derivation steps
  1. For $|z+ia|^2=|z+ib|^2$: $z\bar{z}-iaz+ia\bar{z}+a^2 = z\bar{z}-ibz+ib\bar{z}+b^2 \implies i(b-a)(z-\bar{z})=(b-a)(b+a)$. Divide by $i(b-a)\ne 0$ to get $z-\bar{z}=-i(a+b)$.
  2. For $|z^2-1|^2=(|z|^2+1)^2$: $|z|^4-(z^2+\bar{z}^2)+1 = |z|^4+2|z|^2+1 \implies z^2+2z\bar{z}+\bar{z}^2=0 \implies (z+\bar{z})^2=0 \implies z+\bar{z}=0$.
  3. For $\bar{z}_1+i\bar{z}_2=0$: take conjugates to get $z_1-iz_2=0 \implies \frac{z_1}{z_2}=i=e^{i\pi/2} \implies \arg z_1-\arg z_2=\frac{\pi}{2}$. Add to $\arg z_1+\arg z_2=\pi$ to get $\arg z_1=\frac{3\pi}{4}$.
Principal Logarithm Failure (Log z² ≠ 2 Log z) & Inverse Sine Identity sin⁻¹(csc θ) THEOREM

Principal Logarithm Discrepancy (2014–15 Q.1c, 2016–17 Q.2b): Because the principal argument must satisfy $-\pi < \operatorname{Arg}z \le \pi$, the identity $\operatorname{Log}(z^2) = 2\operatorname{Log}z$ FAILS whenever $2\operatorname{Arg}z \notin (-\pi,\pi]$. For $z=-1+i$ ($|z|=\sqrt{2}, \operatorname{Arg}z=\frac{3\pi}{4}$), $z^2=-2i$ has $\operatorname{Arg}(-2i)=-\frac{\pi}{2}$, so $\operatorname{Log}(-1+i)^2 = \ln 2 - i\frac{\pi}{2} \ne 2\operatorname{Log}(-1+i) = \ln 2 + i\frac{3\pi}{2}$. Similarly, for $z=-1+\sqrt{3}i$ ($|z|=2, \operatorname{Arg}z=\frac{2\pi}{3}$), $\operatorname{Log}(-1+\sqrt{3}i)^2 = \ln 4 - i\frac{2\pi}{3} \ne 2\operatorname{Log}(-1+\sqrt{3}i) = \ln 4 + i\frac{4\pi}{3}$.
Inverse Sine of $\csc\theta$ (2010–11 Q.1c): For $0 < \theta < \frac{\pi}{2}$, $\sin^{-1}(\csc\theta) = \frac{\pi}{2} + i\ln\!\left(\cot\frac{\theta}{2}\right)$ (or $(2n+\frac12)\pi \pm i\ln(\cot\frac{\theta}{2})$ in general).

$$\operatorname{Log}(-1+i)^2=\ln 2-i\frac{\pi}{2}\ne 2\operatorname{Log}(-1+i)=\ln 2+i\frac{3\pi}{2};\qquad \sin^{-1}(\csc\theta)=\frac{\pi}{2}+i\ln\!\left(\cot\frac{\theta}{2}\right)\quad\left(0<\theta<\frac{\pi}{2}\right)$$
Conditions: Also covers 2022–23 Q.1a: $\operatorname{Ln}(i) = \ln|i| + i\operatorname{Arg}(i) = i\frac{\pi}{2}$ and $|\sin z|^2 = |\sin x\cosh y + i\cos x\sinh y|^2 = \sin^2 x + \sinh^2 y$.
Exam Application: Tested in BUET Math 159 2010–11 Q.1c, 2014–15 Q.1c, 2016–17 Q.2b, and 2022–23 Q.1a.
Proof & derivation steps
  1. Let $\sin^{-1}(\csc\theta) = x+iy \implies \sin(x+iy) = \sin x\cosh y + i\cos x\sinh y = \csc\theta + i0$.
  2. Equate real and imaginary parts: $\sin x\cosh y = \csc\theta > 1$ and $\cos x\sinh y = 0$. Since $\cosh y \ge 1$ and $\sin x \le 1$, $\sinh y \ne 0$, so $\cos x = 0 \implies x = \frac{\pi}{2}$ (principal) and $\sin x = 1$.
  3. Then $\cosh y = \csc\theta \implies \sinh y = \pm\sqrt{\csc^2\theta-1} = \pm\cot\theta \implies e^y = \cosh y + \sinh y = \csc\theta + \cot\theta = \frac{1+\cos\theta}{\sin\theta} = \cot\frac{\theta}{2} \implies y = \ln\!\left(\cot\frac{\theta}{2}\right)$.
Neighbourhoods, Deleted Neighbourhoods, Complex Conic Loci & Elementary Mappings DEFINITION

Neighbourhood & Deleted Neighbourhood: A $\delta$-neighbourhood of $z_0$ is the open disk $|z - z_0| < \delta$; a deleted (punctured) neighbourhood $0 < |z - z_0| < \delta$ excludes $z_0$ itself (used to define limits and isolated singularities).
Standard Complex Loci in BUET Exams:
(1) Ellipse ($|z-c_1|+|z-c_2| = 2a > |c_1-c_2|=2c$): Foci at $c_1, c_2$, semi-axes $a$ and $b=\sqrt{a^2-c^2}$. E.g., $|z+2i|+|z-2i|\le 6 \implies \frac{x^2}{5}+\frac{y^2}{9}\le 1$ (2010–11 Q.1b, 2013–14 Q.1b, 2021–22 Q.1c); $|z+1|+|z-1|\le 4 \implies \frac{x^2}{4}+\frac{y^2}{3}\le 1$ (2018–19 Q.1b); $|z-4i|+|z+4i|=10 \implies \frac{x^2}{9}+\frac{y^2}{25}=1$ (2023–24 Q.1b); $|z-3|+|z+3|=10 \implies \frac{x^2}{25}+\frac{y^2}{16}=1$ (2024–25 Q.1a).
(2) Annulus & Hyperbola: $2 < |z-4-5i| < 3$ is an open ring centred at $(4,5)$ (2011–12 Q.1b); $\operatorname{Im}(z^2) \le 4 \iff xy \le 2$ is a hyperbolic region (2015–16 Q.1b).

$$0 < |z - z_0| < \delta\ (\text{deleted }\delta\text{-neighbourhood});\qquad |z-c_1|+|z-c_2|=2a \iff \text{ellipse with }b^2=a^2-c^2;\qquad w=z^2=(x^2-y^2)+i(2xy)$$
Conditions: Elementary Mappings: Translation $w=z+(a+ib)$, CCW rotation by $90^\circ$ $w=iz=-y+ix$, reflection in real axis $w=\bar{z}$, and squaring map $w=z^2 \implies u=x^2-y^2,\ v=2xy$ (maps hyperbolas $x^2-y^2=c_1,\ xy=c_2$ to straight lines $u=c_1,\ v=2c_2$).
Exam Application: For $z(\bar{z}+2)=3$ (2017–18 Q.1b): $x^2+y^2+2x+i(2y)=3 \implies y=0,\ x=1,-3$ (whereas $z\bar{z}+2\operatorname{Re}z=3$ is the circle $(x+1)^2+y^2=4$).
Branch, Branch Cut, Branch Point Proof & Riemann Surface Construction for ln z DEFINITION

Branch of a Multiple-Valued Function: Any single-valued function $F(z)$ that is analytic in a domain $D$ and takes one of the values of a multiple-valued function $f(z)$ at every point of $D$ (e.g., the Principal Branch $\operatorname{Ln}z = \ln r + i\Theta$ on $r>0,\ -\pi < \Theta < \pi$).
Branch Cut: A curve or ray (typically the negative real axis $\theta=\pi$ together with the origin) removed from the complex plane to keep a chosen branch single-valued and prevent $z$ from encircling a branch point.
Branch Point: A point $z_0$ such that $f(z)$ fails to return to its starting value after $z$ traverses a small simple closed circuit once around $z_0$.
Riemann Surface for $f(z)=\ln z$: An infinite stack of complex sheets $S_n$ ($n\in\mathbb{Z}$), each cut along the negative real axis $\theta=\pi$, where the upper edge of sheet $S_n$ ($\theta\to(2n+1)\pi^-$) is joined smoothly to the lower edge of sheet $S_{n+1}$ ($\theta\to(2n+1)\pi^+$), forming a continuous infinite helicoid on which $\ln z$ is single-valued and analytic everywhere except $z=0$.

$$f(re^{i(\theta_0+2\pi)}) = \ln r + i(\theta_0 + 2\pi) = f(re^{i\theta_0}) + 2\pi i \ne f(re^{i\theta_0});\qquad S_n:\ (2n-1)\pi < \theta < (2n+1)\pi\quad (n\in\mathbb{Z})$$
Conditions: Both $z=0$ and $z=\infty$ are branch points of $\ln z$ and $z^\alpha$ ($\alpha\notin\mathbb{Z}$).
Exam Application: To prove $f(z)=\ln z$ has a branch point at $z=0$ (2018–19 Q.1a, 2020–21 Q.2a): let $z=re^{i\theta}$ traverse $|z|=r>0$ from $\theta_0$ to $\theta_0+2\pi$. Then $f(z)$ changes from $\ln r + i\theta_0$ to $\ln r + i(\theta_0+2\pi) = f_{\text{start}} + 2\pi i \ne f_{\text{start}}$.
Analytic, Entire & Harmonic Functions, Harmonic Conjugates & PDE Connections DEFINITION

Analytic (Holomorphic / Regular) Function: $f(z)$ is analytic at $z_0$ if its derivative $f'(z)$ exists not just at $z_0$, but at every point in an open neighbourhood $|z-z_0|<\varepsilon$.
Entire Function: A function that is analytic at every point in the finite complex plane $\mathbb{C}$ (e.g., polynomials, $e^z, e^{z^2}, \sin z, \cos z, \sinh z, \cosh z$).
Harmonic Function & Conjugate: A real function $u(x,y)\in C^2(D)$ is harmonic in $D$ if it satisfies Laplace's 2D equation $\nabla^2 u = u_{xx}+u_{yy} = 0$ (the steady-state limit of the 2D Heat equation $u_t = \nabla^2 u$ and Wave equation $u_{tt} = \nabla^2 u$). If $f(z) = u(x,y) + i v(x,y)$ is analytic in $D$, both $u$ and $v$ are harmonic ($u_{xx} = (v_y)_x = (v_x)_y = -u_{yy}$) and $v$ is called the harmonic conjugate of $u$.

$$\nabla^2 u = u_{xx} + u_{yy} = 0,\qquad \nabla^2 v = v_{xx} + v_{yy} = 0,\qquad f(z) = u(z,0) + i\,v(z,0) + iC,\qquad f'(z) = u_x(z,0) - i\,u_y(z,0)$$
Conditions: Note that if $v$ is a harmonic conjugate of $u$, then $-u$ (not $u$) is the harmonic conjugate of $v$ because $i f(z) = -v + iu$ is analytic!
Exam Application: Once $v(x,y)$ is found from $dv = -u_y\,dx + u_x\,dy$, substitute $x=z,\ y=0$ directly into $u(x,y)+iv(x,y)$ to write $f(z) = u(z,0)+iv(z,0)$ in terms of $z$ alone (tested in 14 of 15 BUET exams 2010–2025).
Classic C–R Counterexamples (z̄²/z), Isolated Differentiability & Entire vs Non-Entire Functions THEOREM

Counterexample 1 — C–R Satisfied at $(0,0)$ Yet Not Differentiable (2011–12 Q.2a, 2015–16 Q.1c, 2021–22 Q.2a): For $f(z) = \frac{(\bar{z})^2}{z} = \frac{x^3-3xy^2}{x^2+y^2} + i\frac{y^3-3x^2y}{x^2+y^2}$ ($z\ne 0$) and $f(0)=0$, the partial derivatives at $(0,0)$ are $u_x(0,0)=v_y(0,0)=1$ and $u_y(0,0)=-v_x(0,0)=0$, so C–R holds at $(0,0)$. However, along $y=mx$ ($z=re^{i\theta}$), $\frac{f(z)-f(0)}{z} = \frac{(\bar{z})^2}{z^2} = \left(\frac{1-im}{1+im}\right)^2 = e^{-i4\theta}$ depends on the slope $m$, so $f'(0)$ does not exist!
Counterexample 2 — Differentiable at a Single Point Only, Nowhere Analytic (2010–11 Q.2a, 2018–19 Q.2a, 2023–24 Q.2a, 2024–25 Q.1c): Using C–R or Wirtinger derivative $\frac{\partial f}{\partial\bar{z}} = 0$: (i) $f(z)=i\bar{z}^2=2xy+i(x^2-y^2)$ is differentiable only at $z=0$; (ii) $f(z)=z\bar{z}+\bar{z}$ has $\frac{\partial f}{\partial\bar{z}}=z+1=0$, differentiable only at $z=-1$; (iii) $f(z)=z^2\bar{z}$ has $\frac{\partial f}{\partial\bar{z}}=z^2=0$, differentiable only at $z=0$. None is analytic anywhere.

$$f(z)=\frac{(\bar{z})^2}{z}\ (z\ne 0),\ f(0)=0:\ \ u_x=v_y=1,\ u_y=-v_x=0\text{ at }(0,0),\ \text{yet }f'(0)=\lim_{z\to 0}e^{-i4\theta}\text{ DNE};\qquad \frac{\partial f}{\partial\bar{z}}=0$$
Conditions: Why does C–R fail to guarantee $f'(0)$ for $\bar{z}^2/z$? Because $u_x, u_y, v_x, v_y$ are discontinuous at $(0,0)$, violating the sufficient condition for differentiability!
Exam Application: Entire vs. Non-Entire Functions (2013–14 Q.1c, 2021–22 Q.2c, 2023–24 Q.2b): $f(z) = -ie^{iz} = e^{-y}\sin x - ie^{-y}\cos x$ and $f(z) = e^{z^2} = e^{x^2-y^2}(\cos 2xy + i\sin 2xy)$ satisfy C–R on all of $\mathbb{C}$ (entire, with $|e^{z^2}|=e^{x^2-y^2}\le e^{|z|^2}$), whereas $g(z)=e^{|z|^2}=e^{x^2+y^2}$ satisfies C–R only at $(0,0)$ and is nowhere analytic.
Complex integration, series & residuesFreq: 5/50/10
Cauchy's Integral Theorem & Cauchy's Integral Formula (with Derivatives) THEOREM

Cauchy–Goursat Integral Theorem: If $f(z)$ is analytic at all points interior to and on a simple closed contour $C$, then $\oint_C f(z)\,dz = 0$. In a multiply-connected domain bounded by outer contour $C$ and inner contours $C_1, \dots, C_k$, $\oint_C f(z)\,dz = \sum_{j=1}^k \oint_{C_j} f(z)\,dz$.
Cauchy's Integral Formula: If $f(z)$ is analytic inside and on a positively oriented simple closed contour $C$ and $z_0$ is any point interior to $C$, then $f(z_0) = \frac{1}{2\pi i}\oint_C \frac{f(z)}{z-z_0}\,dz$.
Generalized Cauchy Integral Formula for Derivatives: An analytic function has derivatives of all orders inside $C$, given by $f^{(n)}(z_0) = \frac{n!}{2\pi i}\oint_C \frac{f(z)}{(z-z_0)^{n+1}}\,dz$.

$$\oint_C f(z)\,dz = 0,\qquad f(z_0)=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\oint_C\frac{f(z)}{z-z_0}\,dz,\qquad f^{(n)}(z_0)=\frac{n!}{2\pi i}\oint_C\frac{f(z)}{(z-z_0)^{n+1}}\,dz,\qquad |f^{(n)}(z_0)|\le\frac{n!\,M}{R^n}$$
Conditions: $C$ is traversed counterclockwise; $M = \max_{|z-z_0|=R}|f(z)|$ gives Cauchy's inequality, which immediately proves Liouville's Theorem: every bounded entire function is constant!
Exam Application: Rewrite $\oint_C \dfrac{g(z)}{(z-z_0)^{n+1}}\,dz = \dfrac{2\pi i}{n!}g^{(n)}(z_0)$ where $g(z)$ includes all factors analytic inside $C$.
Proof & derivation steps
  1. Green's Theorem Proof of Cauchy's Theorem (when $f'$ is continuous, BUET 2018–19 Q.3a): $\oint_C (u+iv)(dx+i\,dy) = \oint_C (u\,dx - v\,dy) + i\oint_C (v\,dx + u\,dy) = \iint_R (-v_x - u_y)\,dx\,dy + i\iint_R (u_x - v_y)\,dx\,dy = 0$ by the C–R equations.
  2. Proof of Cauchy's Integral Formula: Deform $C$ into a small circle $C_\rho: |z-z_0|=\rho$. Write $\oint_C \frac{f(z)}{z-z_0}dz = f(z_0)\oint_{C_\rho}\frac{dz}{z-z_0} + \oint_{C_\rho}\frac{f(z)-f(z_0)}{z-z_0}dz = 2\pi i\,f(z_0) + I_\rho$. By continuity of $f$ at $z_0$, $|I_\rho| \le \frac{\varepsilon}{\rho}(2\pi\rho) = 2\pi\varepsilon \to 0$.
Laurent's Theorem & Cauchy's Residue Theorem THEOREM

Laurent's Theorem: If $f(z)$ is analytic throughout an annular domain $R_1 < |z - z_0| < R_2$, it has a unique two-sided Laurent series representation $f(z) = \sum_{n=0}^\infty a_n(z-z_0)^n + \sum_{n=1}^\infty b_n(z-z_0)^{-n}$, where $a_n = \frac{1}{2\pi i}\oint_C \frac{f(z)}{(z-z_0)^{n+1}}\,dz$ and $b_n = a_{-n} = \frac{1}{2\pi i}\oint_C \frac{f(z)}{(z-z_0)^{-n+1}}\,dz$.
Classification of Isolated Singularities: (1) Removable: all $b_n = 0$; (2) Pole of order $m$: $b_m \neq 0$ and $b_n = 0$ for $n > m$; (3) Essential: infinitely many $b_n \neq 0$.
Cauchy's Residue Theorem: If $f(z)$ is analytic inside and on a positively oriented simple closed curve $C$ except for isolated singularities $z_1, \dots, z_k$ strictly inside $C$, then $\oint_C f(z)\,dz = 2\pi i \sum_{j=1}^k \operatorname{Res}(f, z_j)$, where $\operatorname{Res}(f, z_0) = b_1$.

$$\oint_C f(z)\,dz = 2\pi i\sum_{j=1}^{k}\operatorname{Res}(f, z_j),\qquad \operatorname{Res}_{z=z_0}f(z)=b_1=\frac{1}{(m-1)!}\lim_{z\to z_0}\frac{d^{m-1}}{dz^{m-1}}\big[(z-z_0)^m f(z)\big]$$
Conditions: Only include singularities that lie strictly INSIDE the contour $C$! Furthermore, the sum of all residues in the extended plane (including $\operatorname{Res}_{z=\infty} f(z) = -\operatorname{Res}_{w=0}[w^{-2}f(1/w)]$) is zero.
Exam Application: For a simple pole $f(z) = P(z)/Q(z)$ with $P(z_0) \neq 0, Q(z_0)=0, Q'(z_0) \neq 0$, use $\operatorname{Res}(f, z_0) = P(z_0)/Q'(z_0)$. For Laurent expansions in exams, decompose into partial fractions and use geometric series $\frac{1}{1-w} = \sum_{n=0}^\infty w^n$ for $|w|<1$.
Proof & derivation steps
  1. Enclose each isolated singularity $z_j$ inside $C$ by a small positively oriented circle $C_j$ of radius $\varepsilon_j$.
  2. By contour deformation for multiply-connected domains, $\oint_C f(z)\,dz = \sum_{j=1}^k \oint_{C_j} f(z)\,dz$.
  3. Integrate the Laurent series around $C_j$ term-by-term: since $\oint_{C_j}(z-z_j)^n\,dz = 0$ for all $n \neq -1$ and $2\pi i$ for $n = -1$, each circle yields $2\pi i\,b_{1,j} = 2\pi i\operatorname{Res}(f, z_j)$.
ML-Estimation Inequality (Darboux Bounding Theorem) THEOREM

If $f(z)$ is piecewise continuous along a contour $C$ of finite arc length $L$, and there exists a non-negative constant $M$ such that $|f(z)| \le M$ for all points $z$ on $C$, then the modulus of the contour integral is bounded by $ML$:

$$\left|\int_C f(z)\,dz\right| \le \int_C |f(z)|\,|dz| \le M L,\qquad L = \int_a^b |z'(t)|\,dt$$
Conditions: To bound denominators on a circle $|z| = R$, always use the reverse triangle inequality: $|z^n + a z^k + b| \ge R^n - |a|R^k - |b|$ (provided $R$ is large enough that the RHS is positive).
Exam Application: Tested in BUET Math 159 2011–12 Q.3b, 2014–15 Q.2a, 2017–18 Q.3a/3b, 2022–23 Q.2b, 2023–24 Q.3b, and 2024–25 Q.3b. For a quarter-circle of radius $R$, arc length is $L = \frac{\pi R}{2}$; for a semicircle, $L = \pi R$.
Liouville's Theorem, Maximum Modulus Principle & Morera's Theorem THEOREM

Liouville's Theorem: Every bounded entire function ($f(z)$ analytic for all $z \in \mathbb{C}$ with $|f(z)| \le M$) is constant.
Maximum Modulus Principle: If $f(z)$ is analytic and non-constant in a bounded domain $D$ and continuous on $\overline{D}$, then $|f(z)|$ attains its maximum strictly on the boundary $\partial D$, never at an interior point.
Morera's Theorem (Converse of Cauchy's Theorem): If $f(z)$ is continuous in a domain $D$ and $\oint_C f(z)\,dz = 0$ for every simple closed contour $C \subset D$, then $f(z)$ is analytic throughout $D$.

$$|f'(z_0)| \le \frac{M}{R} \xrightarrow{R\to\infty} 0 \implies f(z) = \text{const};\qquad \oint_C f(z)\,dz = 0\ (\forall\,C\subset D) \implies F'(z) = f(z)\text{ is analytic}$$
Conditions: Liouville's theorem requires $f(z)$ to be analytic on the ENTIRE complex plane $\mathbb{C}$ and bounded everywhere.
Exam Application: Yields a 3-line proof of the Fundamental Theorem of Algebra: if a non-constant polynomial $P(z)$ had no roots in $\mathbb{C}$, then $f(z) = 1/P(z)$ would be a bounded entire function and hence constant, a contradiction!
Complex Form of Green's Theorem THEOREM

If $F(z, \bar{z})$ is continuous and has continuous partial derivatives with respect to $x$ and $y$ (or equivalently $z$ and $\bar{z}$) in a region $R$ bounded by a positively oriented simple closed curve $C$, then:

$$\oint_C F(z, \bar{z})\,dz = 2i \iint_R \frac{\partial F}{\partial \bar{z}}\,dx\,dy = 2i \iint_R \frac{\partial F}{\partial \bar{z}}\,dA,\qquad \frac{\partial}{\partial \bar{z}} = \frac12\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial x} + i\frac{\partial}{\partial y}\right)$$
Conditions: Since $x = \frac{z+\bar{z}}{2}$ and $y = \frac{z-\bar{z}}{2i}$, $z$ and $\bar{z}$ act as independent variables when differentiating $F(z,\bar{z})$.
Exam Application: Directly tested in BUET Math 159 2018–19 Q.3b and provides an instant 1-line evaluation of $\oint_C \bar{z}\,dz = 2i \iint_R 1\,dA = 2i \cdot \text{Area}(R)$ (e.g. around an ellipse $\frac{x^2}{a^2}+\frac{y^2}{b^2}=1$, $\oint_C \bar{z}\,dz = 2\pi i ab$, tested in 2023–24 Q.3a & 2024–25 Q.3a)!
Path Dependence of Non-Analytic Contour Integrals vs. Path Independence PRINCIPLE

Path Independence Criterion: A contour integral $\int_{z_1}^{z_2} f(z)\,dz$ is independent of the path joining $z_1$ and $z_2$ in a simply connected domain $D$ if and only if $f(z)$ is analytic throughout $D$ (so an antiderivative $F'(z)=f(z)$ exists and $\oint_C f(z)\,dz = 0$).
Classic Path-Dependence Proof ($\int \bar{z}\,dz$ from $z=0$ to $z=4+2i$): Since $f(z)=\bar{z}$ is nowhere analytic ($\frac{\partial \bar{z}}{\partial \bar{z}}=1\neq 0$), the integral depends on the contour:

$$I_{\text{parabola}} = \int_{0\to 4+2i} \bar{z}\,dz = 10 - \frac{8}{3}i \;\neq\; I_{\text{broken line}} = 10 - 8i;\qquad f(z)\text{ analytic} \implies \int_{z_1}^{z_2} f(z)\,dz = F(z_2)-F(z_1)$$
Conditions: On any closed loop $C$, $\oint_C \bar{z}\,dz = 2i\,\text{Area}(R) \neq 0$, whereas $\oint_C z^n\,dz = 0$ for all $n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\ge 0}$.
Exam Application: Directly tested in BUET Math 159 class notes & past papers to contrast non-analytic integrals ($\bar{z}, \operatorname{Re}z, \operatorname{Im}z, |z|^2$) with path-independent analytic integrals.
Proof & derivation steps
  1. Path 1 (Parabola $z = t^2 + it$, $0\le t\le 2$): Here $\bar{z} = t^2 - it$ and $dz = (2t + i)\,dt$. Thus $I_1 = \int_0^2 (t^2-it)(2t+i)\,dt = \int_0^2 \big[(2t^3+t) - it^2\big]\,dt = \left[\frac{t^4}{2} + \frac{t^2}{2} - i\frac{t^3}{3}\right]_0^2 = (8+2) - \frac{8}{3}i = 10 - \frac{8}{3}i$.
  2. Path 2 (Broken line $0 \to 2i \to 4+2i$): Along $0\to 2i$ ($z=iy,\ dz=i\,dy,\ \bar{z}=-iy$): $\int_0^2 (-iy)(i\,dy) = \int_0^2 y\,dy = 2$. Along $2i\to 4+2i$ ($z=x+2i,\ dz=dx,\ \bar{z}=x-2i$): $\int_0^4 (x-2i)\,dx = \left[\frac{x^2}{2} - 2ix\right]_0^4 = 8 - 8i$. Summing gives $I_2 = 10 - 8i \neq I_1$.
Standard Exam Proofs Using the ML-Inequality & Reverse Triangle Inequality THEOREM

Three Classic BUET Exam $ML$-Inequality Proofs: To bound $\left|\int_C \frac{P(z)}{Q(z)}\,dz\right| \le M L$, bound the numerator from above using the Triangle Inequality $|z \pm a| \le |z| + |a|$ and bound the denominator from below using the Reverse Triangle Inequality $|z^n \pm a| \ge \big||z|^n - |a|\big| > 0$ on $C$:

$$\left|\int_C \frac{z+4}{z^3-1}\,dz\right| \le \frac{6\pi}{7},\qquad \left|\int_C \frac{dz}{z^2-1}\right| \le \frac{\pi}{3},\qquad \left|\int_{C_R} \frac{2z^2-1}{z^4+5z^2+4}\,dz\right| \le \frac{\pi R(2R^2+1)}{(R^2-1)(R^2-4)} \xrightarrow{R\to\infty} 0$$
Conditions: Always check that $R$ is strictly larger than all roots of the denominator so the lower bound $\big||z|^n - |a|\big|$ is strictly positive.
Exam Application: Directly tested in BUET Math 159 exams and used in Step 2 of every improper real integral contour proof to show $\lim_{R\to\infty} \int_{C_R} f(z)\,dz = 0$.
Proof & derivation steps
  1. Proof 1 ($|z|=2$ from $2$ to $2i$): Quarter-circle arc length is $L = \frac{1}{4}(2\pi \cdot 2) = \pi$. On $|z|=2$: $|z+4| \le |z|+4 = 6$ and $|z^3-1| \ge \big||z|^3 - 1\big| = 2^3 - 1 = 7$. Hence $|f(z)| \le \frac{6}{7} = M$ and $\left|\int_C \frac{z+4}{z^3-1}\,dz\right| \le M L = \frac{6\pi}{7}$.
  2. Proof 2 ($|z|=2$ in first quadrant): Arc length $L = \pi$. On $|z|=2$: $|z^2-1| \ge |z|^2 - 1 = 4 - 1 = 3 \implies \left|\frac{1}{z^2-1}\right| \le \frac{1}{3} = M \implies \left|\int_C \frac{dz}{z^2-1}\right| \le \frac{\pi}{3}$.
  3. Proof 3 (Semicircle $C_R: |z|=R>2$): Arc length $L = \pi R$. On $|z|=R$: $|2z^2-1| \le 2|z|^2+1 = 2R^2+1$, and $|z^4+5z^2+4| = |(z^2+1)(z^2+4)| \ge (R^2-1)(R^2-4)$. Thus $\left|\int_{C_R} f(z)\,dz\right| \le \frac{\pi R(2R^2+1)}{(R^2-1)(R^2-4)} = O(R^{-1}) \to 0$ as $R\to\infty$.
Complex Del Operator (∇), Complex Grad/Div/Curl & General Complex Green's Theorem THEOREM

Complex Differential Operators: Writing $x = \frac{z+\bar{z}}{2}$ and $y = \frac{z-\bar{z}}{2i}$, the chain rule yields $\nabla = \frac{\partial}{\partial x} + i\frac{\partial}{\partial y} = 2\frac{\partial}{\partial \bar{z}}$ and $\bar{\nabla} = \frac{\partial}{\partial x} - i\frac{\partial}{\partial y} = 2\frac{\partial}{\partial z}$. For a complex function $A(x,y) = P(x,y) + iQ(x,y)$:
• Analyticity: $\nabla A = 2\frac{\partial A}{\partial \bar{z}} = (P_x - Q_y) + i(Q_x + P_y) = 0 \iff A(z)$ satisfies Cauchy–Riemann.
• Divergence & Curl: $\bar{\nabla} A = 2\frac{\partial A}{\partial z} = (P_x + Q_y) + i(Q_x - P_y) = \operatorname{div}\mathbf{A} + i(\operatorname{curl}\mathbf{A})_z$.
• Laplacian: $\nabla\bar{\nabla} U = 4\frac{\partial^2 U}{\partial z\,\partial \bar{z}} = U_{xx} + U_{yy} = \nabla^2 U$.

$$\nabla \equiv \frac{\partial}{\partial x} + i\frac{\partial}{\partial y} = 2\frac{\partial}{\partial \bar{z}},\quad \bar{\nabla} \equiv \frac{\partial}{\partial x} - i\frac{\partial}{\partial y} = 2\frac{\partial}{\partial z};\qquad \oint_C \big(P\,dz + Q\,d\bar{z}\big) = 2i\iint_R \left(\frac{\partial P}{\partial \bar{z}} - \frac{\partial Q}{\partial z}\right)dx\,dy$$
Conditions: $P(z,\bar{z})$ and $Q(z,\bar{z})$ have continuous partial derivatives in a simply or multiply connected region $R$ bounded by positively oriented contour $C$.
Exam Application: Directly covered in BUET Math 159 Complex Variables Class Notes (pp. 7–9) and past exam questions on complex Green's theorem and Wirtinger derivatives.
Proof & derivation steps
  1. Substitute $dz = dx + i\,dy$ and $d\bar{z} = dx - i\,dy$: $\oint_C (P\,dz + Q\,d\bar{z}) = \oint_C \big[(P+Q)\,dx + i(P-Q)\,dy\big]$.
  2. Apply real Green's Theorem $\oint_C (M\,dx + N\,dy) = \iint_R (N_x - M_y)\,dx\,dy$ with $M = P+Q$ and $N = i(P-Q)$:
  3. $N_x - M_y = i(P_x - Q_x) - (P_y + Q_y) = i\big[(P_x + iP_y) - (Q_x - iQ_y)\big] = 2i\left(\frac{\partial P}{\partial \bar{z}} - \frac{\partial Q}{\partial z}\right)$, completing the proof.
Isolated Singularities Classification (Removable, Pole, Essential) & Residue at Infinity DEFINITION

Classification via Principal Part of Laurent Series $f(z) = \sum_{n=0}^\infty a_n(z-z_0)^n + \sum_{n=1}^\infty a_{-n}(z-z_0)^{-n}$:
• Removable Singularity: No negative powers ($a_{-n}=0\ \forall n\ge 1$, $\lim_{z\to z_0} f(z)$ is finite), e.g. $\frac{\sin z}{z}, \frac{1-\cos z}{z^2}$ at $z=0$; $\operatorname{Res}=0$.
• Pole of Order $m$: Highest negative power is $(z-z_0)^{-m}$ with $a_{-m}\neq 0$ ($m=1$: simple pole; $m=2$: double pole).
• Essential Singularity: Infinitely many $a_{-n}\neq 0$, e.g. $e^{1/z}, \sin(1/z), z^5 e^{1/z^2}$ at $z=0$. For $z^5 e^{1/z^2} = z^5\sum_{k=0}^\infty \frac{z^{-2k}}{k!} = z^5 + z^3 + \frac{z}{2!} + \frac{1}{3!z} + \cdots$, the $z^{-1}$ term ($2k-5=1\implies k=3$) gives $\operatorname{Res}_{z=0} = \frac{1}{3!} = \frac{1}{6}$.
Residue at Infinity: If $f(z)$ has finitely many singularities, the sum of all residues in $\mathbb{C}\cup\{\infty\}$ is zero.

$$\operatorname{Res}_{z=z_0} f(z) = a_{-1};\qquad \operatorname{Res}_{z=\infty} f(z) = -a_{-1} = -\operatorname{Res}_{w=0}\left[\frac{1}{w^2}f\!\left(\frac{1}{w}\right)\right];\qquad \sum_{k=1}^{n}\operatorname{Res}_{z=z_k} f(z) + \operatorname{Res}_{z=\infty} f(z) = 0$$
Conditions: Caution: Even if $\operatorname{Res}_{z=\infty} f(z) \neq 0$, $z=\infty$ does not have to be a singularity of $f(1/w)$ at $w=0$ (for example $f(z) = 1/z$ is analytic at $\infty$ since $f(1/w)=w$, yet $\operatorname{Res}_{z=\infty}(1/z) = -\operatorname{Res}_{w=0}(1/w) = -1$).
Exam Application: When a contour $C$ encloses many finite poles (e.g. $\oint_{|z|=10} \frac{z^7}{(z^8-2)(z+3)}\,dz$), use $\oint_C f(z)\,dz = -2\pi i\,\operatorname{Res}_{z=\infty} f(z) = 2\pi i\,\operatorname{Res}_{w=0}\left[\frac{1}{w^2}f(1/w)\right]$ to evaluate in 1 line!
Evaluation of Real Trigonometric & Improper Rational Integrals by Residues THEOREM

Three Canonical Real Integral Types Solved via Cauchy's Residue Theorem:
• Type 1 (Unit Circle $|z|=1$): For $\int_0^{2\pi} F(\cos\theta,\sin\theta)\,d\theta$, substitute $z=e^{i\theta}$, $d\theta = \frac{dz}{iz}$, $\cos\theta = \frac{z^2+1}{2z}$, $\sin\theta = \frac{z^2-1}{2iz}$. Only include poles with $|z_k| < 1$.
• Type 2 (Upper Half-Plane Semicircle): For $\int_{-\infty}^\infty \frac{P(x)}{Q(x)}\,dx$ with $\deg Q \ge \deg P + 2$ and $Q(x)\neq 0$ on $\mathbb{R}$, sum residues at poles in the upper half-plane ($\operatorname{Im}z_k > 0$).
• Type 3 (Fourier & Indented Contours): For $\int_{-\infty}^\infty \frac{P(x)}{Q(x)}e^{imx}\,dx$ ($m>0$, $\deg Q \ge \deg P + 1$), Jordan's Lemma gives $\lim_{R\to\infty}\int_{C_R} \frac{P(z)}{Q(z)}e^{imz}\,dz = 0$; each simple pole $x_j \in \mathbb{R}$ indented by a small upper semicircle contributes $+\pi i\,\operatorname{Res}_{z=x_j}$.

$$\int_0^{2\pi} F(\cos\theta,\sin\theta)\,d\theta = 2\pi i \sum_{|z_k|<1} \operatorname{Res}_{z=z_k}\!\left[\frac{1}{iz}F\!\left(\frac{z+z^{-1}}{2},\frac{z-z^{-1}}{2i}\right)\right];\qquad \text{P.V.}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{P(x)}{Q(x)}\,dx = 2\pi i\sum_{\operatorname{Im}z_k>0}\operatorname{Res}_{z=z_k}\frac{P(z)}{Q(z)}$$
Conditions: For $\int_0^\pi F(\cos\theta)\,d\theta$, use evenness to write $\frac{1}{2}\int_0^{2\pi} F(\cos\theta)\,d\theta$. For $\frac{1}{z^n+a^n}$ at a simple root $z_k$, always use the shortcut $\operatorname{Res}_{z=z_k}\frac{P(z)}{Q(z)} = \frac{P(z_k)}{Q'(z_k)} = \frac{P(z_k)}{n z_k^{n-1}} = \frac{z_k P(z_k)}{-n a^n}$.
Exam Application: Appears in every single BUET Math 159 Term Final (2010–2025 Q.3c / Q.4b / Q.4c).
Worked exam prototypes (Type 1 & Type 2)
  1. Prototype 1: $I = \int_0^{2\pi} \frac{d\theta}{5 - 3\cos\theta} = \oint_{|z|=1} \frac{dz/(iz)}{5 - \frac{3}{2}(z + z^{-1})} = \frac{2}{i}\oint_{|z|=1} \frac{dz}{-3z^2 + 10z - 3} = \frac{-2}{3i}\oint_{|z|=1} \frac{dz}{(z-3)(z - 1/3)}$. Only $z_1 = \frac{1}{3}$ lies inside $|z|=1$, so $I = 2\pi i \left(\frac{-2}{3i}\right)\frac{1}{1/3 - 3} = \frac{\pi}{2}$. (General formula for $a > |b|$: $\int_0^{2\pi} \frac{d\theta}{a + b\cos\theta} = \frac{2\pi}{\sqrt{a^2 - b^2}}$.)
  2. Prototype 2: $I = \int_{-\infty}^{\infty} \frac{dx}{x^4 + 1}$. Upper-half-plane simple poles of $z^4 = -1 = e^{i(\pi + 2k\pi)}$ are $z_1 = e^{i\pi/4}$ and $z_2 = e^{i3\pi/4}$. Using $\operatorname{Res}_{z_k}\frac{1}{z^4+1} = \frac{1}{4z_k^3} = -\frac{z_k}{4}$: $I = 2\pi i\left(-\frac{e^{i\pi/4} + e^{i3\pi/4}}{4}\right) = -\frac{\pi i}{2}\left(\frac{1+i}{\sqrt{2}} + \frac{-1+i}{\sqrt{2}}\right) = -\frac{\pi i}{2}\left(\frac{2i}{\sqrt{2}}\right) = \frac{\pi}{\sqrt{2}}$.
Vector algebra, vector calculus & integral theoremsFreq: 5/50/13
Conservative / Irrotational Vector Field Equivalence Theorem THEOREM

For a continuously differentiable vector field $\mathbf{F} = P\hat{\mathbf{i}} + Q\hat{\mathbf{j}} + R\hat{\mathbf{k}}$ on a simply connected domain $D \subset \mathbb{R}^3$, the following four conditions are mutually equivalent:
(1) $\mathbf{F}$ is irrotational ($\nabla\times\mathbf{F} = \mathbf{0}$ everywhere in $D$),
(2) $\mathbf{F}$ is the gradient of a single-valued scalar potential ($\mathbf{F} = \nabla\phi$, or $P\,dx + Q\,dy + R\,dz = d\phi$ is an exact differential),
(3) The circulation around every closed contour $C \subset D$ vanishes ($\oint_C \mathbf{F}\cdot d\mathbf{r} = 0$), and
(4) The line integral $\int_A^B \mathbf{F}\cdot d\mathbf{r} = \phi(B) - \phi(A)$ is independent of the path joining $A$ to $B$ in $D$.

$$(1)\ \nabla\times\mathbf{F}=\mathbf{0}\iff (2)\ \mathbf{F}=\nabla\phi\iff (3)\ \oint_C\mathbf{F}\cdot d\mathbf{r}=0\ (\forall\text{ closed }C)\iff (4)\ \int_A^B\mathbf{F}\cdot d\mathbf{r}=\phi(B)-\phi(A)$$
Conditions: $D$ must be simply connected (no holes threaded by the contour). In a multiply-connected domain (e.g. $\mathbf{v} = \frac{-y\hat{\mathbf{i}} + x\hat{\mathbf{j}}}{x^2+y^2}$ on $\mathbb{R}^2\setminus\{(0,0)\}$, BUET 2013–14 Q.6c), $\nabla\times\mathbf{v} = \mathbf{0}$ for $r\neq 0$ yet $\oint_{x^2+y^2=1}\mathbf{v}\cdot d\mathbf{r} = 2\pi \neq 0$!
Exam Application: When asked to evaluate $\int_A^B \mathbf{F}\cdot d\mathbf{r}$ along a complicated twisted curve, always check $\nabla\times\mathbf{F} = \mathbf{0}$ first: if zero, find $\phi(x,y,z) = \int P\,dx + \int (Q\text{ terms free of }x)\,dy + \int (R\text{ terms free of }x,y)\,dz$ and compute $\phi(B) - \phi(A)$!
Green's, Stokes' & Gauss' Integral Theorems (Unified Summary) THEOREM

Every fundamental theorem of vector calculus equates an integral of a derivative over a region $\Omega$ to an integral of the field itself over the oriented boundary $\partial\Omega$:
(1) Green's Theorem in the Plane: If $C = \partial R$ is a positively oriented (counterclockwise), piecewise-smooth, simple closed curve in the $xy$-plane bounding region $R$, and $P, Q$ have continuous first partials on $R$, then $\oint_C (P\,dx + Q\,dy) = \iint_R (Q_x - P_y)\,dx\,dy$ (with $\text{Area}(R) = \frac12\oint_C (x\,dy - y\,dx)$).
(2) Stokes' Curl Theorem: If $S$ is an oriented, piecewise-smooth open surface bounded by a simple closed curve $C = \partial S$ oriented by the right-hand rule relative to unit normal $\hat{\mathbf{n}}$, then $\oint_C \mathbf{F}\cdot d\mathbf{r} = \iint_S (\nabla\times\mathbf{F})\cdot\hat{\mathbf{n}}\,dS$.
(3) Gauss' Divergence Theorem: If $V$ is a solid 3D region bounded by a closed surface $S = \partial V$ with outward unit normal $\hat{\mathbf{n}}$, then $\oiint_S \mathbf{F}\cdot\hat{\mathbf{n}}\,dS = \iiint_V (\nabla\cdot\mathbf{F})\,dV$.

$$\oint_{\partial R}(P\,dx+Q\,dy)=\iint_R(Q_x-P_y)\,dx\,dy,\qquad \oint_{\partial S}\mathbf{F}\cdot d\mathbf{r}=\iint_S(\nabla\times\mathbf{F})\cdot\hat{\mathbf{n}}\,dS,\qquad \oiint_{\partial V}\mathbf{F}\cdot\hat{\mathbf{n}}\,dS=\iiint_V(\nabla\cdot\mathbf{F})\,dV$$
Conditions: Boundary orientation: counterclockwise for Green, right-hand thumb rule with $\hat{\mathbf{n}}$ for Stokes, outward normal $\hat{\mathbf{n}}$ for Gauss.
Exam Application: Notice that $\oiint_S (\nabla\times\mathbf{F})\cdot\hat{\mathbf{n}}\,dS$ over any CLOSED surface $S$ is zero because $\nabla\cdot(\nabla\times\mathbf{F}) = 0$ by Gauss. For an open hemisphere $S$ resting on $z=0$, Stokes' theorem lets you replace $\iint_S (\nabla\times\mathbf{F})\cdot\hat{\mathbf{n}}\,dS$ by the flat disk integral $\iint_{x^2+y^2\le a^2}(\nabla\times\mathbf{F})\cdot\hat{\mathbf{k}}\,dx\,dy$!
Proof & derivation steps
  1. Deduction of Green's Theorem from Stokes' Theorem (BUET 2011–12 Q.8b, 2013–14 Q.7b): Let $S = R$ be a flat planar region in the $xy$-plane ($z=0$) with upward unit normal $\hat{\mathbf{n}} = \hat{\mathbf{k}}$, and set $\mathbf{F} = P(x,y)\hat{\mathbf{i}} + Q(x,y)\hat{\mathbf{j}} + 0\hat{\mathbf{k}}$.
  2. Then $\mathbf{F}\cdot d\mathbf{r} = P\,dx + Q\,dy$ and $(\nabla\times\mathbf{F})\cdot\hat{\mathbf{k}} = \dfrac{\partial Q}{\partial x} - \dfrac{\partial P}{\partial y}$ with $dS = dx\,dy$, reducing Stokes' theorem directly to $\oint_C (P\,dx + Q\,dy) = \iint_R (Q_x - P_y)\,dx\,dy$.
Linear Dependence, Coplanarity of Four Points, Reciprocal Vectors & Vector Equations THEOREM

Linear Dependence & Independence: Vectors $\mathbf{v}_1, \dots, \mathbf{v}_k$ are linearly independent if $c_1\mathbf{v}_1 + \cdots + c_k\mathbf{v}_k = \mathbf{0} \implies c_1 = \cdots = c_k = 0$; otherwise they are linearly dependent. In $\mathbb{R}^3$, three vectors $\mathbf{a}, \mathbf{b}, \mathbf{c}$ are linearly independent (non-coplanar) iff $[\mathbf{a}\ \mathbf{b}\ \mathbf{c}] \neq 0$, and any vector $\mathbf{r} \in \mathbb{R}^3$ is uniquely expressible as $\mathbf{r} = (\mathbf{r}\cdot\mathbf{a}')\mathbf{a} + (\mathbf{r}\cdot\mathbf{b}')\mathbf{b} + (\mathbf{r}\cdot\mathbf{c}')\mathbf{c}$ using the reciprocal set $\mathbf{a}', \mathbf{b}', \mathbf{c}'$.

$$\mathbf{a}' = \frac{\mathbf{b}\times\mathbf{c}}{[\mathbf{a}\ \mathbf{b}\ \mathbf{c}]};\qquad \text{Solution of }k\mathbf{r} + \mathbf{a}\times\mathbf{r} = \mathbf{b}:\quad \mathbf{r} = \frac{k^2\mathbf{b} + (\mathbf{a}\cdot\mathbf{b})\mathbf{a} - k(\mathbf{a}\times\mathbf{b})}{k(k^2 + |\mathbf{a}|^2)}$$
Conditions: Coplanarity of 4 points $A, B, C, D$: Position vectors $\mathbf{r}_A, \mathbf{r}_B, \mathbf{r}_C, \mathbf{r}_D$ are coplanar iff $[\vec{AB}\ \vec{AC}\ \vec{AD}] = 0$ (i.e. $V_{\text{tetra}} = \frac16|[\vec{AB}\ \vec{AC}\ \vec{AD}]| = 0$), or iff $\alpha\mathbf{r}_A + \beta\mathbf{r}_B + \gamma\mathbf{r}_C + \delta\mathbf{r}_D = \mathbf{0}$ with $\alpha+\beta+\gamma+\delta = 0$.
Exam Application: To solve $k\mathbf{r} + \mathbf{a}\times\mathbf{r} = \mathbf{b}$ (tested in 2013–14 Q.5c, 2014–15 Q.5c, 2018–19 Q.6c, 2020–21 Q.6b): (1) dot with $\mathbf{a}$ to get $\mathbf{a}\cdot\mathbf{r} = \frac{\mathbf{a}\cdot\mathbf{b}}{k}$, (2) cross with $\mathbf{a}$ to get $k(\mathbf{a}\times\mathbf{r}) + (\mathbf{a}\cdot\mathbf{r})\mathbf{a} - |\mathbf{a}|^2\mathbf{r} = \mathbf{a}\times\mathbf{b}$, and substitute $\mathbf{a}\times\mathbf{r} = \mathbf{b} - k\mathbf{r}$.
Space Curves: Frenet–Serret Formulas, Curvature κ, Torsion τ & Rigid-Body Rotation THEOREM

Space Curve & Moving Trihedron: A space curve is the locus of a terminal point of a single-parameter position vector $\mathbf{r}(t) = x(t)\hat{\mathbf{i}} + y(t)\hat{\mathbf{j}} + z(t)\hat{\mathbf{k}}$. The unit tangent $\mathbf{T}$, principal normal $\mathbf{N}$, and binormal $\mathbf{B} = \mathbf{T}\times\mathbf{N}$ form a right-handed orthonormal frame satisfying the Frenet–Serret formulas with respect to arc length $s$:

$$\frac{d\mathbf{T}}{ds} = \kappa\mathbf{N},\quad \frac{d\mathbf{N}}{ds} = -\kappa\mathbf{T} + \tau\mathbf{B},\quad \frac{d\mathbf{B}}{ds} = -\tau\mathbf{N};\qquad \kappa = \frac{|\mathbf{r}'\times\mathbf{r}''|}{|\mathbf{r}'|^3},\qquad \tau = \frac{[\mathbf{r}'\ \mathbf{r}''\ \mathbf{r}''']}{|\mathbf{r}'\times\mathbf{r}''|^2}$$
Conditions: Rigid-Body Rotation (2023–24 Q.5c, 2024–25 Q.5b): If a rigid body rotates with angular velocity $\boldsymbol{\omega} = \omega\hat{\mathbf{u}}$ about an axis passing through $\mathbf{r}_0$, the linear velocity at position $\mathbf{r}$ is $\mathbf{v} = \boldsymbol{\omega}\times(\mathbf{r}-\mathbf{r}_0)$, and $\nabla\times\mathbf{v} = 2\boldsymbol{\omega}$.
Exam Application: To derive the Frenet–Serret formulas (2014–15 Q.6a, 2016–17 Q.6a, 2023–24 Q.6a): (1) $\mathbf{T}\cdot\mathbf{T}=1 \implies \frac{d\mathbf{T}}{ds} = \kappa\mathbf{N}$; (2) $\mathbf{B}=\mathbf{T}\times\mathbf{N} \implies \frac{d\mathbf{B}}{ds} = \mathbf{T}\times\frac{d\mathbf{N}}{ds}$ is parallel to $\mathbf{N}$, so $\frac{d\mathbf{B}}{ds} = -\tau\mathbf{N}$; (3) differentiate $\mathbf{N} = \mathbf{B}\times\mathbf{T}$ to get $\frac{d\mathbf{N}}{ds} = -\kappa\mathbf{T} + \tau\mathbf{B}$.
Directional & Normal Derivatives, Physical Meaning of Div/Curl & Radial Vector Identities THEOREM

Directional & Normal Derivatives: The rate of change of a scalar field $\phi(x,y,z)$ in the direction of a unit vector $\hat{\mathbf{u}} = \frac{\mathbf{a}}{|\mathbf{a}|}$ is $D_{\hat{\mathbf{u}}}\phi = \nabla\phi\cdot\hat{\mathbf{u}}$. Its maximum value occurs in the direction of the surface normal $\nabla\phi$ and equals the normal derivative $\frac{d\phi}{dn} = |\nabla\phi|$.
Physical Interpretation of Divergence & Curl: $\nabla\cdot\mathbf{F} = \lim_{\Delta V\to 0}\frac{1}{\Delta V}\oiint_{\partial(\Delta V)}\mathbf{F}\cdot d\mathbf{S}$ measures the net outward flux (source/expansion rate) per unit volume; $\mathbf{F}$ is solenoidal (incompressible) iff $\nabla\cdot\mathbf{F} = 0$. $\nabla\times\mathbf{F}$ measures twice the local angular velocity (circulation per unit area) of the field; $\mathbf{F}$ is irrotational (conservative) iff $\nabla\times\mathbf{F} = \mathbf{0}$.

$$\nabla\cdot\big(f(r)\mathbf{r}\big) = 3f(r) + r f'(r) = \frac{1}{r^2}\frac{d}{dr}\big(r^3 f(r)\big),\qquad \nabla^2 f(r) = f''(r) + \frac{2}{r}f'(r),\qquad \nabla^2(r^n\mathbf{r}) = n(n+3)r^{n-2}\mathbf{r}$$
Conditions: Thus $f(r)\mathbf{r}$ is solenoidal ($\nabla\cdot(f(r)\mathbf{r})=0$) if and only if $r^3 f(r) = C \iff f(r) = C r^{-3}$ (i.e. $\mathbf{F} = C\frac{\mathbf{r}}{r^3}$, tested in 2017–18 Q.5c & 2021–22 Q.6b). Moreover, $\nabla\times(f(r)\mathbf{r}) = \mathbf{0}$ for ANY differentiable $f(r)$ with potential $\phi(r) = -\int f(r)\,dr$.
Exam Application: Tested in 2010–11 Q.6c, 2013–14 Q.6b, 2016–17 Q.6c, 2018–19 Q.7a, 2020–21 Q.7a, and 2021–22 Q.6b/6c.
3D Basis Representation Proof, Reciprocal Vector Expansion & Planes Through Points THEOREM

Unique Linear Combination of 3 Non-Coplanar Vectors (2012–13 Q.5b, 2015–16 Q.5a, 2019–20 Q.5a, 2022–23 Q.5c, 2024–25 Q.5a): If $\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b},\mathbf{c}$ are three non-coplanar vectors ($[\mathbf{a}\ \mathbf{b}\ \mathbf{c}]\ne 0$), any vector $\mathbf{r}\in\mathbb{R}^3$ can be uniquely written as $\mathbf{r}=x\mathbf{a}+y\mathbf{b}+z\mathbf{c}$. Taking dot products successively with $\mathbf{b}\times\mathbf{c}$, $\mathbf{c}\times\mathbf{a}$, and $\mathbf{a}\times\mathbf{b}$ yields $x=\frac{[\mathbf{r}\ \mathbf{b}\ \mathbf{c}]}{[\mathbf{a}\ \mathbf{b}\ \mathbf{c}]}=\mathbf{r}\cdot\mathbf{a}'$, $y=\frac{[\mathbf{a}\ \mathbf{r}\ \mathbf{c}]}{[\mathbf{a}\ \mathbf{b}\ \mathbf{c}]}=\mathbf{r}\cdot\mathbf{b}'$, $z=\frac{[\mathbf{a}\ \mathbf{b}\ \mathbf{r}]}{[\mathbf{a}\ \mathbf{b}\ \mathbf{c}]}=\mathbf{r}\cdot\mathbf{c}'$. Moreover, any 4 vectors in $\mathbb{R}^3$ are linearly dependent.
Short Question — How Many Planes Can Be Constructed Using Two Points? (2022–23 Q.5b): Infinitely many planes! Two distinct points $P, Q$ define a single straight line $\mathbf{r}=\mathbf{r}_P+t(\mathbf{r}_Q-\mathbf{r}_P)$, and an infinite pencil of planes can rotate $360^\circ$ around axis $PQ$. A unique plane $(\mathbf{r}-\mathbf{r}_1)\cdot\big[(\mathbf{r}_2-\mathbf{r}_1)\times(\mathbf{r}_3-\mathbf{r}_1)\big]=0$ requires three non-collinear points.

$$\mathbf{r}=\frac{[\mathbf{r}\ \mathbf{b}\ \mathbf{c}]}{[\mathbf{a}\ \mathbf{b}\ \mathbf{c}]}\mathbf{a}+\frac{[\mathbf{a}\ \mathbf{r}\ \mathbf{c}]}{[\mathbf{a}\ \mathbf{b}\ \mathbf{c}]}\mathbf{b}+\frac{[\mathbf{a}\ \mathbf{b}\ \mathbf{r}]}{[\mathbf{a}\ \mathbf{b}\ \mathbf{c}]}\mathbf{c}=(\mathbf{r}\cdot\mathbf{a}')\mathbf{a}+(\mathbf{r}\cdot\mathbf{b}')\mathbf{b}+(\mathbf{r}\cdot\mathbf{c}')\mathbf{c}=(\mathbf{r}\cdot\mathbf{a})\mathbf{a}'+(\mathbf{r}\cdot\mathbf{b})\mathbf{b}'+(\mathbf{r}\cdot\mathbf{c})\mathbf{c}'$$
Conditions: Collinearity of 3 Points $A,B,C$: $(\mathbf{r}_2-\mathbf{r}_1)\times(\mathbf{r}_3-\mathbf{r}_1)=\mathbf{0} \iff \alpha\mathbf{r}_1+\beta\mathbf{r}_2+\gamma\mathbf{r}_3=\mathbf{0}$ with $\alpha+\beta+\gamma=0$.
Exam Application: Dual basis expansion $\mathbf{r} = (\mathbf{r}\cdot\mathbf{a})\mathbf{a}' + (\mathbf{r}\cdot\mathbf{b})\mathbf{b}' + (\mathbf{r}\cdot\mathbf{c})\mathbf{c}'$ was tested in 2016–17 Q.5b, 2019–20 Q.5b, and 2023–24 Q.5b.
Proof & derivation steps
  1. Write $\mathbf{r}=x\mathbf{a}+y\mathbf{b}+z\mathbf{c}$ and dot with $\mathbf{b}\times\mathbf{c}$: since $\mathbf{b}\cdot(\mathbf{b}\times\mathbf{c})=\mathbf{c}\cdot(\mathbf{b}\times\mathbf{c})=0$, we get $\mathbf{r}\cdot(\mathbf{b}\times\mathbf{c})=x[\mathbf{a}\ \mathbf{b}\ \mathbf{c}] \implies x=\frac{[\mathbf{r}\ \mathbf{b}\ \mathbf{c}]}{[\mathbf{a}\ \mathbf{b}\ \mathbf{c}]}$.
  2. For the reciprocal expansion $\mathbf{r}=c_1\mathbf{a}'+c_2\mathbf{b}'+c_3\mathbf{c}'$, dot both sides with $\mathbf{a}$: since $\mathbf{a}\cdot\mathbf{a}'=1$ and $\mathbf{a}\cdot\mathbf{b}'=\mathbf{a}\cdot\mathbf{c}'=0$, we obtain $c_1=\mathbf{r}\cdot\mathbf{a}$ (and similarly $c_2=\mathbf{r}\cdot\mathbf{b},\ c_3=\mathbf{r}\cdot\mathbf{c}$).
Classic Vector Triple/Quadruple Product Proofs & Transversal Cutting Two Forces THEOREM

Classic BUET Vector Algebra Proofs:
(1) Cyclic Box-Product Sum (2010–11 Q.5b): $[\mathbf{a}\times\mathbf{p},\ \mathbf{b}\times\mathbf{q},\ \mathbf{c}\times\mathbf{r}] + [\mathbf{a}\times\mathbf{q},\ \mathbf{b}\times\mathbf{r},\ \mathbf{c}\times\mathbf{p}] + [\mathbf{a}\times\mathbf{r},\ \mathbf{b}\times\mathbf{p},\ \mathbf{c}\times\mathbf{q}] = 0$.
(2) Quadruple Cross Product & Dual Volume (2012–13 Q.5c, 2014–15 Q.5b): $(\mathbf{A}\times\mathbf{B})\times(\mathbf{C}\times\mathbf{D}) = [\mathbf{A}\ \mathbf{C}\ \mathbf{D}]\mathbf{B} - [\mathbf{B}\ \mathbf{C}\ \mathbf{D}]\mathbf{A}$, and the volume of the parallelepiped with edges $\mathbf{B}\times\mathbf{C},\ \mathbf{C}\times\mathbf{A},\ \mathbf{A}\times\mathbf{B}$ equals $[\mathbf{A}\ \mathbf{B}\ \mathbf{C}]^2$.
(3) Transversal Cutting Two Concurrent Forces (2011–12 Q.5b): If two forces $\mathbf{P},\mathbf{Q}$ acting at $O$ have resultant $\mathbf{R}=\mathbf{P}+\mathbf{Q}$ and any transversal line cuts their lines of action at collinear points $A,B,C$, then $\frac{|\mathbf{P}|}{OA}+\frac{|\mathbf{Q}|}{OB}=\frac{|\mathbf{R}|}{OC}$.

$$(\mathbf{A}\times\mathbf{B})\times(\mathbf{C}\times\mathbf{D})=[\mathbf{A}\ \mathbf{C}\ \mathbf{D}]\mathbf{B}-[\mathbf{B}\ \mathbf{C}\ \mathbf{D}]\mathbf{A},\qquad [\mathbf{B}\times\mathbf{C},\ \mathbf{C}\times\mathbf{A},\ \mathbf{A}\times\mathbf{B}]=[\mathbf{A}\ \mathbf{B}\ \mathbf{C}]^2,\qquad \frac{|\mathbf{P}|}{OA}+\frac{|\mathbf{Q}|}{OB}=\frac{|\mathbf{R}|}{OC}$$
Conditions: In Proof (1), expand $(\mathbf{a}\times\mathbf{p})\cdot\big((\mathbf{b}\times\mathbf{q})\times(\mathbf{c}\times\mathbf{r})\big) = [\mathbf{b}\ \mathbf{q}\ \mathbf{r}][\mathbf{a}\ \mathbf{p}\ \mathbf{c}] - [\mathbf{b}\ \mathbf{q}\ \mathbf{c}][\mathbf{a}\ \mathbf{p}\ \mathbf{r}]$; cycling $(\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q},\mathbf{r})$ cancels all 6 terms in pairs.
Exam Application: For the transversal proof: write $\mathbf{P}=\frac{|\mathbf{P}|}{OA}\vec{OA}$, $\mathbf{Q}=\frac{|\mathbf{Q}|}{OB}\vec{OB}$, $\mathbf{R}=\frac{|\mathbf{R}|}{OC}\vec{OC}$ into $\mathbf{P}+\mathbf{Q}=\mathbf{R}$, then use collinearity $\vec{OC}-\vec{OA}=t(\vec{OB}-\vec{OA}) \implies (1-t)\vec{OA}+t\vec{OB}=\vec{OC}$ and equate coefficients of non-collinear $\vec{OA},\vec{OB}$ so that $\frac{|\mathbf{P}|/OA}{|\mathbf{R}|/OC} + \frac{|\mathbf{Q}|/OB}{|\mathbf{R}|/OC} = (1-t) + t = 1$.
Standard Vector Equations Solved for Unknown Vector r or x THEOREM

Three Tested Vector Equations for Unknown $\mathbf{r}$ or $\mathbf{x}$:
(1) Type 1 ($\mathbf{r}\times\mathbf{b}=\mathbf{a}\times\mathbf{b}$ with $\mathbf{r}\cdot\mathbf{c}=0$, 2017–18 Q.5b, 2022–23 Q.5a): $(\mathbf{r}-\mathbf{a})\times\mathbf{b}=\mathbf{0}\implies \mathbf{r}=\mathbf{a}+t\mathbf{b}$ ($t\in\mathbb{R}$); dotting with $\mathbf{c}$ gives $t=-\frac{\mathbf{a}\cdot\mathbf{c}}{\mathbf{b}\cdot\mathbf{c}}$.
(2) Type 2 ($k\mathbf{r}+\mathbf{a}\times\mathbf{r}=\mathbf{b}$ with $k\ne 0$, 2013–14 Q.5c, 2014–15 Q.5c, 2018–19 Q.6c, 2020–21 Q.6b): Dot with $\mathbf{a}\implies \mathbf{a}\cdot\mathbf{r}=\frac{\mathbf{a}\cdot\mathbf{b}}{k}$; cross with $\mathbf{a}\implies k(\mathbf{a}\times\mathbf{r})+(\mathbf{a}\cdot\mathbf{r})\mathbf{a}-|\mathbf{a}|^2\mathbf{r}=\mathbf{a}\times\mathbf{b}$, yielding $\mathbf{r}=\frac{k^2\mathbf{b}+(\mathbf{a}\cdot\mathbf{b})\mathbf{a}-k(\mathbf{a}\times\mathbf{b})}{k(k^2+|\mathbf{a}|^2)}$.
(3) Type 3 ($\mathbf{a}\times\mathbf{x}+(\mathbf{a}\cdot\mathbf{x})\mathbf{a}+\mathbf{b}=\mathbf{0}$ with $\mathbf{a}\ne\mathbf{0}$, 2021–22 Q.5b): Dot with $\mathbf{a}\implies (\mathbf{a}\cdot\mathbf{x})|\mathbf{a}|^2+\mathbf{a}\cdot\mathbf{b}=0\implies \mathbf{a}\cdot\mathbf{x}=-\frac{\mathbf{a}\cdot\mathbf{b}}{|\mathbf{a}|^2}$; cross from the left with $\mathbf{a}\implies (\mathbf{a}\cdot\mathbf{x})\mathbf{a}-|\mathbf{a}|^2\mathbf{x}+\mathbf{a}\times\mathbf{b}=\mathbf{0}\implies \mathbf{x}=\frac{\mathbf{a}\times\mathbf{b}}{|\mathbf{a}|^2}-\frac{(\mathbf{a}\cdot\mathbf{b})\mathbf{a}}{|\mathbf{a}|^4}$.

$$\mathbf{r}\times\mathbf{b}=\mathbf{a}\times\mathbf{b},\ \mathbf{r}\cdot\mathbf{c}=0\implies\mathbf{r}=\mathbf{a}-\frac{\mathbf{a}\cdot\mathbf{c}}{\mathbf{b}\cdot\mathbf{c}}\mathbf{b};\qquad \mathbf{a}\times\mathbf{x}+(\mathbf{a}\cdot\mathbf{x})\mathbf{a}+\mathbf{b}=\mathbf{0}\implies\mathbf{x}=\frac{\mathbf{a}\times\mathbf{b}}{|\mathbf{a}|^2}-\frac{(\mathbf{a}\cdot\mathbf{b})\mathbf{a}}{|\mathbf{a}|^4}$$
Conditions: In Type 1, $\mathbf{b}\cdot\mathbf{c}\ne 0$; in Type 2, $k\ne 0$; in Type 3, $\mathbf{a}\ne\mathbf{0}$.
Exam Application: Never divide by a vector! Always isolate $\mathbf{r}$ or $\mathbf{x}$ by taking both the dot product ($\mathbf{a}\cdot$) and the cross product ($\mathbf{a}\times$) with the coefficient vector $\mathbf{a}$, then apply $\mathbf{a}\times(\mathbf{a}\times\mathbf{x}) = (\mathbf{a}\cdot\mathbf{x})\mathbf{a} - |\mathbf{a}|^2\mathbf{x}$.
Space Curve Definitions (T, N, B, Curvature, Torsion), Fundamental Planes & Circular Motion DEFINITION

Fundamental Definitions of Space Curve Geometry:
(1) Space Curve & Arc Length: Locus of $\mathbf{r}(t) = x(t)\hat{\mathbf{i}} + y(t)\hat{\mathbf{j}} + z(t)\hat{\mathbf{k}}$ with $ds = |\mathbf{r}'(t)|\,dt$.
(2) Unit Tangent ($\mathbf{T}$): $\mathbf{T} = \frac{d\mathbf{r}}{ds} = \frac{\mathbf{r}'(t)}{|\mathbf{r}'(t)|}$, pointing in the direction of motion.
(3) Curvature ($\kappa$) & Radius of Curvature ($\rho$): $\kappa = \left|\frac{d\mathbf{T}}{ds}\right| = \frac{|\mathbf{r}'\times\mathbf{r}''|}{|\mathbf{r}'|^3}$ measures how rapidly the curve bends away from its tangent line ($\kappa=0 \iff$ straight line); $\rho = \frac{1}{\kappa}$.
(4) Principal Normal ($\mathbf{N}$) & Binormal ($\mathbf{B}$): $\mathbf{N} = \frac{1}{\kappa}\frac{d\mathbf{T}}{ds} = \mathbf{B}\times\mathbf{T}$ points toward the centre of curvature; $\mathbf{B} = \mathbf{T}\times\mathbf{N} = \frac{\mathbf{r}'\times\mathbf{r}''}{|\mathbf{r}'\times\mathbf{r}''|}$ is perpendicular to the osculating plane.
(5) Torsion ($\tau$) & Radius of Torsion ($\sigma$): $\tau = -\mathbf{N}\cdot\frac{d\mathbf{B}}{ds} = \frac{[\mathbf{r}'\ \mathbf{r}''\ \mathbf{r}''']}{|\mathbf{r}'\times\mathbf{r}''|^2}$ measures the rate at which the curve twists out of its osculating plane ($\tau=0 \iff$ planar curve); $\sigma = \frac{1}{\tau}$.

$$\text{Osculating }(\perp\mathbf{B}):\ (\mathbf{R}-\mathbf{r}_0)\cdot\mathbf{B}=0;\qquad \text{Normal }(\perp\mathbf{T}):\ (\mathbf{R}-\mathbf{r}_0)\cdot\mathbf{T}=0;\qquad \text{Rectifying }(\perp\mathbf{N}):\ (\mathbf{R}-\mathbf{r}_0)\cdot\mathbf{N}=0$$
Conditions: Fundamental lines through $\mathbf{r}_0$: Tangent $\mathbf{R}=\mathbf{r}_0+\lambda\mathbf{T}$, Principal Normal $\mathbf{R}=\mathbf{r}_0+\lambda\mathbf{N}$, Binormal $\mathbf{R}=\mathbf{r}_0+\lambda\mathbf{B}$.
Exam Application: Circular Motion Kinematics (2015–16 Q.6a): For $\mathbf{r}(t) = \cos\omega t\,\hat{\mathbf{i}} + \sin\omega t\,\hat{\mathbf{j}}$, $\mathbf{v}\cdot\mathbf{r}=0$ ($\mathbf{v}\perp\mathbf{r}$), $\mathbf{a} = -\omega^2\mathbf{r}$ (centripetal), and $\mathbf{r}\times\mathbf{v} = \omega\hat{\mathbf{k}} = \text{const}$.
Directional, Normal & Tangential Derivatives, Solenoidal & Irrotational Vector Fields DEFINITION

Directional, Normal & Tangential Derivatives:
(1) Directional Derivative ($D_{\hat{\mathbf{a}}}\phi = \nabla\phi\cdot\frac{\mathbf{a}}{|\mathbf{a}|}$): Rate of change of scalar field $\phi(x,y,z)$ per unit distance in direction $\hat{\mathbf{a}}$. Maximum increase is $|\nabla\phi|$ along $+\nabla\phi$; most rapid decrease is $-|\nabla\phi|$ along $-\nabla\phi$ (2018–19 Q.6a, 2022–23 Q.6a).
(2) Normal Derivative ($\frac{d\phi}{dn} = \nabla\phi\cdot\hat{\mathbf{n}} = |\nabla\phi|$): Rate of change of $\phi$ along the unit normal $\hat{\mathbf{n}} = \frac{\nabla\phi}{|\nabla\phi|}$ to the level surface $\phi(x,y,z)=c$ (2012–13 Q.6b, 2016–17 Q.5c).
(3) Tangential Derivative ($\frac{d\phi}{ds} = \nabla\phi\cdot\mathbf{T} = \nabla\phi\cdot\frac{d\mathbf{r}}{ds}$): Rate of change of $\phi$ along a curve $C$ with unit tangent $\mathbf{T}$ (equals $0$ if $C$ lies on $\phi=c$).
Solenoidal vs. Irrotational Fields: $\mathbf{F}$ is solenoidal iff $\nabla\cdot\mathbf{F}=0$ (zero net flux per unit volume, incompressible flow, $\mathbf{F}=\nabla\times\mathbf{V}$); $\mathbf{F}$ is irrotational (conservative) iff $\nabla\times\mathbf{F}=\mathbf{0}$ (zero local circulation/vorticity, $\mathbf{F}=\nabla\phi$).

$$D_{\hat{\mathbf{a}}}\phi=\nabla\phi\cdot\hat{\mathbf{a}}=|\nabla\phi|\cos\theta,\qquad \frac{d\phi}{dn}=\nabla\phi\cdot\hat{\mathbf{n}}=|\nabla\phi|,\qquad \frac{d\phi}{ds}=\nabla\phi\cdot\mathbf{T};\qquad \nabla\cdot\mathbf{F}=0\ (\text{solenoidal}),\quad \nabla\times\mathbf{F}=\mathbf{0}\ (\text{irrotational})$$
Conditions: Unit normal to surface $g(x,y,z)=c$ is $\hat{\mathbf{n}} = \pm\frac{\nabla g}{|\nabla g|}$.
Exam Application: Tested in virtually every BUET Math 159 paper from 2010 to 2025.
Complete Proofs of All 7 Tested Radial & Vector Differential Identities THEOREM

All 7 Tested BUET Vector Differential Identity Proofs:
(1) $\operatorname{div}\!\left\{\frac{f(r)\mathbf{r}}{r}\right\} = \nabla\!\left(\frac{f(r)}{r}\right)\cdot\mathbf{r} + \frac{f(r)}{r}\nabla\cdot\mathbf{r} = \frac{rf'(r)-f(r)}{r^2}\frac{\mathbf{r}\cdot\mathbf{r}}{r} + \frac{3f(r)}{r} = f'(r) + \frac{2}{r}f(r) = \frac{1}{r^2}\frac{d}{dr}\{r^2 f(r)\}$ (2013–14 Q.6b).
(2) $\nabla\cdot\big(f(r)\mathbf{r}\big) = 3f(r) + rf'(r) = 0 \iff \frac{f'(r)}{f(r)} = -\frac{3}{r} \iff f(r) = \frac{C}{r^3}$, so $\nabla\cdot(\mathbf{r}/r^3)=0$ (2017–18 Q.5c, 2021–22 Q.6b).
(3) $\nabla^2 f(r) = \nabla\cdot\nabla f(r) = \nabla\cdot\!\left(\frac{f'(r)}{r}\mathbf{r}\right) = f''(r) + \frac{2}{r}f'(r)$ (2016–17 Q.6c, 2020–21 Q.7a, 2021–22 Q.6c).
(4) $\nabla^2(r^n\mathbf{r}) = n(n+3)r^{n-2}\mathbf{r}$ (2010–11 Q.6c, 2018–19 Q.7a).
(5) $\nabla\times(\nabla\times\mathbf{A}) = \nabla(\nabla\cdot\mathbf{A}) - \nabla^2\mathbf{A}$ (2012–13 Q.6a, 2015–16 Q.6b).
(6) $\nabla\cdot(\mathbf{A}\times\mathbf{B}) = \mathbf{B}\cdot(\nabla\times\mathbf{A}) - \mathbf{A}\cdot(\nabla\times\mathbf{B})$; hence if $\nabla\times\mathbf{A}=\mathbf{0}$, $\nabla\cdot(\mathbf{A}\times\mathbf{r}) = \mathbf{r}\cdot(\nabla\times\mathbf{A}) - \mathbf{A}\cdot(\nabla\times\mathbf{r}) = 0$ (2011–12 Q.6c, 2023–24 Q.6c).
(7) Quotient Derivative (2019–20 Q.5c): Using $\dot{r} = \frac{\mathbf{r}\cdot\dot{\mathbf{r}}}{r}$, $\frac{d}{dt}\!\left(\frac{\mathbf{r}}{\mathbf{r}\cdot\mathbf{a}} - \frac{\mathbf{r}\times\mathbf{a}}{r}\right) = \frac{(\mathbf{r}\cdot\mathbf{a})\dot{\mathbf{r}} - (\dot{\mathbf{r}}\cdot\mathbf{a})\mathbf{r}}{(\mathbf{r}\cdot\mathbf{a})^2} - \frac{r^2(\dot{\mathbf{r}}\times\mathbf{a}) - (\mathbf{r}\cdot\dot{\mathbf{r}})(\mathbf{r}\times\mathbf{a})}{r^3}$.
