Conditions: Temperatures $T_H, T_C$ on the absolute thermodynamic (Kelvin) scale. Lowering sink $T_C$ by $\Delta T$ increases $\eta$ by $\Delta T/T_H$, which is ALWAYS greater than raising source $T_H$ by $\Delta T$ (2014–15 Q.5b)!
Exam Application: To test whether a proposed cyclic engine is reversible, irreversible, or impossible: compute $\sum Q_i/T_i$ (with $Q > 0$ absorbed, $Q < 0$ rejected); $= 0$ is reversible, $< 0$ is possible/irreversible, $> 0$ violates the Second Law!
Theorem of Equipartition of Energy & Degrees of Freedom (f) THEOREM

Degrees of Freedom ($f$): The number of independent coordinates or velocity components required to specify the configuration and dynamical energy of a molecule ($f = 3N - k$: Monatomic $f=3$; Linear/diatomic $f=5$; Non-linear polyatomic $f=6$).
Law of Equipartition of Energy: In thermal equilibrium at absolute temperature $T$, each independent quadratic term (degree of freedom) in the classical Hamiltonian of a molecule contributes an average kinetic/potential energy of $\frac12 k_B T$ per molecule (or $\frac12 RT$ per mole).

$$\langle \varepsilon_i\rangle = \frac12 k_B T,\qquad U = \frac{f}{2}nRT,\qquad C_v = \frac{f}{2}R,\qquad C_p = \left(\frac{f}{2}+1\right)R,\qquad \gamma = 1 + \frac{2}{f},\qquad \gamma_{\text{mix}} = \frac{\lambda_1 C_{p1} + \lambda_2 C_{p2}}{\lambda_1 C_{v1} + \lambda_2 C_{v2}}$$
Conditions: Monatomic: $f=3$ ($\gamma=5/3=1.67$); Rigid diatomic: $f=5$ ($\gamma=7/5=1.40$); Vibrating diatomic (high $T$): $f=7$ ($\gamma=9/7=1.29$); Non-linear polyatomic: $f=6$ ($\gamma=4/3=1.33$).
Exam Application: Each vibrational mode counts as 2 degrees of freedom (1 kinetic $\frac12 m\dot{x}^2$ + 1 potential $\frac12 kx^2$), contributing $2 \times \frac12 k_B T = k_B T$. For a mixture of $\lambda_1$ moles of monatomic and $\lambda_2$ moles of diatomic gas (2022–23 Q.5c), $\gamma_{\text{mix}} = \frac{5\lambda_1 + 7\lambda_2}{3\lambda_1 + 5\lambda_2}$.
Thermodynamic Systems, First Law, Specific Heats, Mayer's Relation & Steady-Flow Nozzle LAW

Thermodynamic Systems: An open system exchanges both matter and energy with its surroundings; a closed system exchanges energy (heat/work) but not matter; an isolated system exchanges neither matter nor energy.
First Law of Thermodynamics: Heat $\delta Q$ supplied to a system equals the increase in its internal energy $dU$ plus the external work $\delta W = P\,dV$ done by the system.
Mayer's Relation ($C_p - C_v = R$): At constant volume ($dV=0$), $\delta Q_V = nC_v\,dT = dU$. At constant pressure, $\delta Q_P = nC_p\,dT = dU + P\,dV = nC_v\,dT + nR\,dT \implies C_p - C_v = R$.

$$dQ = dU + P\,dV,\qquad C_p - C_v = R;\qquad \text{Steady-Flow Nozzle (Adiabatic Horizontal): }h_1 + \frac12 v_1^2 = h_2 + \frac12 v_2^2 \implies v_2 = \sqrt{v_1^2 + 2(h_1 - h_2)}$$
Conditions: For specific heats per unit mass ($c_p, c_v$ in $\text{J}\cdot\text{kg}^{-1}\cdot\text{K}^{-1}$): $c_p - c_v = r = \frac{R}{M}$. In the steady-flow nozzle equation (2020–21 Q.7b/7c), convert enthalpy $h$ from $\text{kJ/kg}$ to $\text{J/kg}$ ($\times 1000$) before taking the square root!
Exam Application: Tested in BUET PHY 121 2009–10 Q.1a/1b/1c, 2020–21 Q.7b/7c, 2021–22 Q.7c, 2022–23 Q.4a/6a, 2023–24 Q.7a, and 2024–25 Q.5b.
Reversible vs Irreversible Processes, Adiabatic Law (PV^γ = K) & Slope Theorem THEOREM

Reversible vs Irreversible Processes: A reversible process is carried out quasi-statically (infinitely slowly through equilibrium states) with zero dissipative friction/viscosity so both system and surroundings can be restored to their exact initial states. Any natural rapid or frictional process (free expansion, bursting tyre, heat flow across finite $\Delta T$) is irreversible.
Adiabatic Equation & Slope Theorem: In a reversible adiabatic process ($\delta Q = 0$), an ideal gas obeys $PV^\gamma = \text{const}$, $TV^{\gamma-1} = \text{const}$, and $T^\gamma P^{1-\gamma} = \text{const}$. At any point $(P, V)$, the adiabatic $P$–$V$ curve is steeper than the isothermal curve by a factor of $\gamma = C_p/C_v > 1$:

$$PV^\gamma = \text{const},\quad TV^{\gamma-1} = \text{const},\quad T^\gamma P^{1-\gamma} = \text{const};\qquad \left(\frac{dP}{dV}\right)_{\text{adiabatic}} = -\gamma\frac{P}{V} = \gamma\left(\frac{dP}{dV}\right)_{\text{isothermal}}$$
Conditions: Work done in isothermal process: $W_{\text{iso}} = nRT\ln\frac{V_2}{V_1} = nRT\ln\frac{P_1}{P_2}$. Work done in adiabatic process: $W_{\text{adia}} = \frac{P_1 V_1 - P_2 V_2}{\gamma - 1} = \frac{nR(T_1 - T_2)}{\gamma - 1}$.
Exam Application: Tested in 2009–10 Q.1a/2a, 2011–12 Q.1b/1c, 2012–13 Q.2a, 2013–14 Q.1a/1b, 2017–18 Q.7a/7c, and 2022–23 Q.4c.
Entropy as a State Function, Principle of Increase of Entropy & Variable-C / Real Gas Entropy THEOREM

Definition of Entropy ($S$): A thermodynamic state function measuring the unavailability of a system's thermal energy for conversion into mechanical work (or molecular disorder), defined for any reversible differential path by $dS = \frac{\delta Q_{\text{rev}}}{T}$.
Path Independence Proof: For any reversible cycle, Carnot's theorem generalized to a cascade of Carnot cycles yields $\oint \frac{\delta Q_{\text{rev}}}{T} = 0$, proving $\int_A^B \frac{\delta Q_{\text{rev}}}{T} = S(B) - S(A)$ is independent of the path!
Principle of Increase of Entropy: In an isolated system, $\Delta S = 0$ for reversible processes and $\Delta S > 0$ for all irreversible (natural) processes, so the entropy of the Universe is continually increasing.

$$\Delta S_{\text{ideal}} = nC_v\ln\frac{T_2}{T_1} + nR\ln\frac{V_2}{V_1} = nC_p\ln\frac{T_2}{T_1} - nR\ln\frac{P_2}{P_1};\qquad \Delta S_{\text{VdW}} = nC_v\ln\frac{T_2}{T_1} + nR\ln\!\left(\frac{V_2 - nb}{V_1 - nb}\right)$$
Conditions: For a gas with temperature-dependent specific heats $c_p = a + kT$ and $c_v = b + kT$ (tested in 2020–21 Q.8b/8c, where $r = c_p - c_v = a - b$): $\Delta s = \int_{T_1}^{T_2}\frac{a+kT}{T}dT - r\ln\frac{P_2}{P_1} = a\ln\frac{T_2}{T_1} + k(T_2 - T_1) - (a-b)\ln\frac{P_2}{P_1}$.
Exam Application: Tested in 2011–12 Q.3a/3c, 2012–13 Q.3b, 2015–16 Q.7a/7b/7c, 2017–18 Q.8a/8b/8c, 2020–21 Q.8a/8b/8c, 2022–23 Q.4b, 2023–24 Q.8a, and 2024–25 Q.6c.
The Four Thermodynamic Potentials (U, H, F, G) & Derivation of Maxwell's Four Relations THEOREM

The Four Thermodynamic Potentials:
(1) Internal Energy: $U(S,V)$ with $dU = T\,dS - P\,dV$
(2) Enthalpy: $H(S,P) = U + PV$ with $dH = T\,dS + V\,dP$
(3) Helmholtz Free Energy: $F(T,V) = U - TS$ with $dF = -S\,dT - P\,dV$
(4) Gibbs Free Energy: $G(T,P) = H - TS = U + PV - TS$ with $dG = -S\,dT + V\,dP$
Because $dU, dH, dF, dG$ are exact differentials ($\frac{\partial^2\Phi}{\partial x\partial y} = \frac{\partial^2\Phi}{\partial y\partial x}$), equating mixed second partial derivatives yields Maxwell's Four Fundamental Thermodynamic Relations:

$$(1)\ \left(\frac{\partial T}{\partial V}\right)_S = -\left(\frac{\partial P}{\partial S}\right)_V,\quad (2)\ \left(\frac{\partial T}{\partial P}\right)_S = \left(\frac{\partial V}{\partial S}\right)_P,\quad (3)\ \left(\frac{\partial S}{\partial V}\right)_T = \left(\frac{\partial P}{\partial T}\right)_V,\quad (4)\ \left(\frac{\partial S}{\partial P}\right)_T = -\left(\frac{\partial V}{\partial T}\right)_P$$
Conditions: Also from $dG = -S\,dT + V\,dP$: $S = -\left(\frac{\partial G}{\partial T}\right)_P$ and $V = \left(\frac{\partial G}{\partial P}\right)_T$ (asked in 2018–19 Q.3c). And Jacobian identity $\left(\frac{\partial P}{\partial T}\right)_S\left(\frac{\partial V}{\partial S}\right)_T - \left(\frac{\partial P}{\partial S}\right)_T\left(\frac{\partial V}{\partial T}\right)_S = 1$ (asked in 2016–17 Q.8a).
Exam Application: Tested in 2011–12 Q.3b, 2013–14 Q.3a/3b, 2015–16 Q.8a, 2016–17 Q.8a, 2018–19 Q.3c, 2021–22 Q.8a, 2022–23 Q.6b, and 2024–25 Q.6a/6b.
First & Second TdS Equations and General Cp − Cv Relation (Ideal & Van der Waals Gases) THEOREM

Expanding $S = S(T,V)$ and $S = S(T,P)$ and substituting Maxwell's 3rd and 4th relations yields the First and Second $TdS$ Equations, from which subtracting the two expressions gives the universal thermodynamic identity for $C_p - C_v$:

$$\begin{aligned}&T\,dS = C_v\,dT + T\left(\frac{\partial P}{\partial T}\right)_V dV = C_p\,dT - T\left(\frac{\partial V}{\partial T}\right)_P dP\\&C_p - C_v = T\left(\frac{\partial P}{\partial T}\right)_V\left(\frac{\partial V}{\partial T}\right)_P = -T\left(\frac{\partial V}{\partial T}\right)_P^2\left(\frac{\partial P}{\partial V}\right)_T\\&\text{Ideal gas: }C_p - C_v = R;\qquad \text{Van der Waals gas: }C_p - C_v = R\left(1 - \frac{2a(V-b)^2}{RTV^3}\right)^{-1} \approx R\left(1 + \frac{2a}{RTV}\right)\end{aligned}$$
Conditions: For 1 mole of Van der Waals gas $\left(P + \frac{a}{V^2}\right)(V-b) = RT$: $\left(\frac{\partial P}{\partial T}\right)_V = \frac{R}{V-b}$ and differentiating with respect to $T$ at constant $P$ gives $\left(\frac{\partial V}{\partial T}\right)_P = \frac{R}{P - a/V^2 + 2ab/V^3} \approx \frac{V-b}{T}\left(1 + \frac{2a}{RTV}\right)$.
Exam Application: Tested in 2014–15 Q.6b, 2016–17 Q.8b/8c, 2018–19 Q.3b, 2020–21 Q.7a, 2021–22 Q.8b, and 2022–23 Q.6b.
Clausius–Clapeyron Latent Heat Equation & Effect of Pressure on Phase Transitions THEOREM

Applying Maxwell's 3rd relation $\left(\frac{\partial P}{\partial T}\right)_V = \left(\frac{\partial S}{\partial V}\right)_T$ to a reversible isothermal-isobaric phase transition where $\Delta S = \frac{L}{T}$ and $\Delta V = v_2 - v_1$ gives the Clausius–Clapeyron Equation:

$$\frac{dP}{dT} = \frac{L}{T(v_2 - v_1)};\qquad \text{Integrated (vaporization, }v_2 \gg v_1\text{): }\ln P = -\frac{L_v}{R T} + C$$
Conditions: Physical Significance: (1) Boiling (liquid $\to$ vapour): $v_{\text{vap}} > v_{\text{liq}} \implies \frac{dP}{dT} > 0$, so increasing pressure raises the boiling point of water (explaining why a pressure cooker cooks food faster at higher $T$, 2019–20 Q.4a). (2) Melting of ice (solid $\to$ liquid): $v_{\text{water}} = 1.000\text{ cm}^3/\text{g} < v_{\text{ice}} = 1.091\text{ cm}^3/\text{g} \implies \frac{dP}{dT} < 0$, so increasing pressure lowers the melting point of ice!
Exam Application: In CGS numerical problems: convert $L = 80\text{ cal/g} \times 4.186\times 10^7\text{ erg/cal}$ and $1\text{ atm} = 1.013\times 10^6\text{ dynes/cm}^2$!
Kinetic Theory Postulates, Kinetic Pressure Equation & Brownian Motion THEOREM

Fundamental Postulates of Kinetic Theory of Gases: (1) A gas consists of a huge number of identical, tiny spherical molecules in random motion; (2) the volume of the molecules themselves is negligible compared to the container volume; (3) intermolecular forces are negligible except during brief collisions; (4) collisions are perfectly elastic and of negligible duration compared to free flight time.
Brownian Motion: The continuous, erratic, zig-zag motion of microscopic suspended particles in a fluid due to unbalanced random impacts from surrounding fluid molecules (increases with higher $T$, smaller particle size, and lower fluid viscosity).

$$P = \frac{1}{3}\frac{N m}{V}\overline{v^2} = \frac{1}{3}\rho v_{\text{rms}}^2 = \frac{2}{3}\frac{E_{\text{tr}}}{V},\qquad E_{\text{tr, mole}} = \frac{3}{2}RT,\qquad E_{\text{tr, molecule}} = \frac{3}{2}k_B T$$
Conditions: where $\rho = Nm/V$ is mass density and $v_{\text{rms}} = \sqrt{\overline{v^2}} = \sqrt{\frac{3P}{\rho}} = \sqrt{\frac{3RT}{M}}$.
Exam Application: Tested in 2011–12 Q.1a, 2014–15 Q.4c, 2018–19 Q.1c, 2021–22 Q.7a, 2022–23 Q.5a, and 2024–25 Q.4a.
Maxwell–Boltzmann Speed Distribution & Most Probable, Mean, and RMS Speeds THEOREM

In a gas of molecular mass $m$ in thermal equilibrium at temperature $T$, the fraction of molecules $\frac{dN}{N} = f(v)\,dv$ having speeds in the range $[v, v+dv]$ obeys the Maxwell–Boltzmann Speed Distribution Law:

$$f(v) = 4\pi\left(\frac{m}{2\pi k_B T}\right)^{3/2} v^2 e^{-\frac{mv^2}{2k_B T}};\qquad v_p = \sqrt{\frac{2k_B T}{m}},\quad \bar{v} = \sqrt{\frac{8k_B T}{\pi m}} = \frac{2}{\sqrt{\pi}}v_p,\quad v_{\text{rms}} = \sqrt{\frac{3k_B T}{m}} = \sqrt{\frac{3}{2}}v_p$$
Conditions: Key Properties of the $f(v)$ Curve (2019–20 Q.5a): (1) The total area under the curve $\int_0^\infty f(v)\,dv = 1$ (total probability = 1) at all temperatures; (2) the peak of the curve occurs at the most probable speed $v_p$ ($\frac{df}{dv}=0$); (3) as $T$ increases, the peak shifts to higher speeds ($v_p \propto \sqrt{T}$) and its height decreases so the area remains 1; (4) $v_p : \bar{v} : v_{\text{rms}} = 1 : 1.128 : 1.224$.
Exam Application: Tested in 2014–15 Q.4b, 2019–20 Q.5a/5b, and 2023–24 Q.7b.
Clausius Mean Free Path (λ) & Collision Frequency Derivation THEOREM

Mean Free Path ($\lambda$): The average distance travelled by a gas molecule between two successive collisions. A molecule of diameter $d$ moving at average speed $\bar{v}$ sweeps out a collision cylinder of cross-sectional area $\sigma = \pi d^2$. Accounting for the relative motion of target molecules ($\overline{v_{\text{rel}}} = \sqrt{2}\,\bar{v}$):

$$\lambda = \frac{\text{distance travelled in }t}{\text{number of collisions in }t} = \frac{1}{\sqrt{2}\,\pi n d^2} = \frac{k_B T}{\sqrt{2}\,\pi d^2 P},\qquad Z = \frac{\bar{v}}{\lambda} = \sqrt{2}\,\pi n d^2 \bar{v}$$
Conditions: where $n = \frac{N}{V} = \frac{P}{k_B T}$ is the number of molecules per unit volume and $d$ is molecular diameter. Notice $\lambda \propto \frac{T}{P}$ (at constant density $n$, $\lambda$ is independent of temperature).
Exam Application: Tested in BUET PHY 121 2012–13 Q.1c and 2024–25 Q.4b/4c.
Van der Waals Equation of State: Corrections, Critical Constants & Limitations THEOREM

Two Corrections to the Ideal Gas Law:
(1) Finite Molecular Volume Correction ($b$): Molecules are rigid spheres of diameter $d$ with excluded volume $b = 4 N_A \left(\frac43\pi r^3\right) = 4V_{\text{molecules}}$ per mole, reducing the free volume to $(V - nb)$.
(2) Intermolecular Attraction Correction ($a/V^2$): A molecule near the wall experiences a net inward cohesive pull proportional to $n/V$, and the number of striking molecules is also proportional to $n/V$, reducing the measured wall pressure by $P_{\text{int}} = \frac{n^2 a}{V^2}$.

$$\left(P + \frac{n^2 a}{V^2}\right)(V - nb) = nRT;\qquad V_c = 3b,\qquad P_c = \frac{a}{27b^2},\qquad T_c = \frac{8a}{27Rb},\qquad Z_c = \frac{P_c V_c}{R T_c} = \frac{3}{8} = 0.375$$
Conditions: At the critical point $(P_c, V_c, T_c)$, the horizontal inflection point on the $P$–$V$ isotherm satisfies $\left(\frac{\partial P}{\partial V}\right)_{T_c} = 0$ and $\left(\frac{\partial^2 P}{\partial V^2}\right)_{T_c} = 0$. Also $b = \frac{R T_c}{8 P_c}$ and $a = \frac{27 R^2 T_c^2}{64 P_c}$.
Exam Application: Main Defects of Van der Waals Equation (2016–17 Q.7b): (1) $a$ and $b$ are not strictly constant over wide ranges of $T$ and $P$; (2) experimental critical compressibility $Z_c = \frac{P_c V_c}{RT_c} \approx 0.23\text{–}0.29$ is smaller than the theoretical $\frac38 = 0.375$; (3) below $T_c$ it yields unphysical S-shaped loops requiring Maxwell's equal-area construction.
Gibbs–Helmholtz Equations, Parent Jacobian Identity & Law of Corresponding States THEOREM

Gibbs–Helmholtz Equations: Relate enthalpy $H$ to Gibbs free energy $G(T,P)$ and internal energy $U$ to Helmholtz free energy $F(T,V)$ without requiring direct entropy measurement.
Parent Jacobian Identity: Because $dU = T\,dS - P\,dV$ is an exact differential, the Jacobian of $(T,S)$ equals the Jacobian of $(P,V)$ with respect to any pair of state variables $(x,y)$.
Law of Corresponding States: Expressed in reduced variables $P_r = P/P_c$, $v_r = v_m/v_c$, $T_r = T/T_c$, the van der Waals equation becomes a universal material-independent law $\left(P_r + \frac{3}{v_r^2}\right)(3v_r - 1) = 8T_r$ with Boyle temperature $T_B = \frac{a}{Rb} = \frac{27}{8}T_c$.

$$\left[\frac{\partial(G/T)}{\partial T}\right]_P = -\frac{H}{T^2},\qquad \left[\frac{\partial(F/T)}{\partial T}\right]_V = -\frac{U}{T^2},\qquad \left(P_r + \frac{3}{v_r^2}\right)(3v_r - 1) = 8T_r,\qquad T_B = \frac{a}{Rb} = \frac{27}{8}T_c$$
Conditions: Closed simple compressible system in thermodynamic equilibrium; reduced equation of state holds for all fluids obeying a two-parameter van der Waals equation.
Exam Application: Covers PHY 121 Formula Atlas Topics 23 and 29 derivations: proving the Gibbs–Helmholtz identities and eliminating $(a,b,R)$ at the critical point $(v_c = 3b, P_c = a/27b^2, T_c = 8a/27Rb)$.
Proof & derivation steps
  1. From $dG = -S\,dT + V\,dP$, identify $S = -\left(\dfrac{\partial G}{\partial T}\right)_P$. Substitute into $G = H - TS$ to get $H = G - T\left(\dfrac{\partial G}{\partial T}\right)_P$, or equivalently $\left[\dfrac{\partial(G/T)}{\partial T}\right]_P = \dfrac{T(\partial G/\partial T)_P - G}{T^2} = -\dfrac{H}{T^2}$.
  2. Substitute $P = P_r P_c = P_r\dfrac{a}{27b^2}$, $v_m = v_r v_c = 3b v_r$, and $T = T_r T_c = T_r\dfrac{8a}{27Rb}$ into $\left(P + \dfrac{a}{v_m^2}\right)(v_m - b) = RT$, cancel $\dfrac{a}{9b}$ on both sides, and obtain $\left(P_r + \dfrac{3}{v_r^2}\right)(3v_r - 1) = 8T_r$.
Thermodynamic Systems (Open, Closed, Isolated) & Four Fundamental Processes DEFINITION

Definition of a Thermodynamic System & 3 Types: A definite macroscopic quantity of matter or region in space bounded by real or imaginary walls, separated from its surroundings:
(1) Open System: Exchanges both energy (heat/work) and matter (mass) with its surroundings (e.g., steam turbine, jet nozzle, boiling water in an open vessel, living organism).
(2) Closed System: Exchanges energy (heat and work) only, while its mass remains strictly constant ($\Delta m = 0$, e.g., gas sealed inside a conducting cylinder with a movable piston).
(3) Isolated System: Exchanges neither energy nor matter with its surroundings ($\Delta Q = 0, \Delta W = 0, \Delta m = 0$, e.g., gas enclosed in a rigid, perfectly thermally insulated thermos vessel, or the Universe as a whole).

ProcessDefining ConstraintEquation of State (Ideal Gas)First Law & Work Done
Isothermal$T = \text{const}\ (dT = 0)$$PV = nRT = \text{const}$$\Delta U = 0;\quad \Delta Q = W = nRT\ln\frac{V_2}{V_1}$
Adiabatic$\delta Q = 0$ (thermally insulated)$PV^\gamma = \text{const},\ TV^{\gamma-1} = \text{const}$$\Delta U = -W;\quad W = \frac{P_1 V_1 - P_2 V_2}{\gamma - 1}$
Isochoric (Isometric)$V = \text{const}\ (dV = 0)$$\frac{P}{T} = \frac{nR}{V} = \text{const}$$W = \int P\,dV = 0;\quad \Delta Q_V = \Delta U = nC_v\Delta T$
Isobaric$P = \text{const}\ (dP = 0)$$\frac{V}{T} = \frac{nR}{P} = \text{const}$$W = P(V_2 - V_1) = nR\Delta T;\quad \Delta Q_P = \Delta H = nC_p\Delta T$
$$\text{Isothermal }(dT=0):\ PV = \text{const};\qquad \text{Adiabatic }(\delta Q=0):\ PV^\gamma = \text{const};\qquad \text{Isochoric }(dV=0):\ \frac{P}{T} = \text{const};\qquad \text{Isobaric }(dP=0):\ \frac{V}{T} = \text{const}$$
Conditions: A slow process in a vessel with diathermal (conducting) walls immersed in a thermostat is isothermal; a very rapid process (e.g. sound wave compressions or bursting of a tyre) or one inside adiabatic walls is adiabatic.
Exam Application: Directly asked in BUET PHY 121 2009–10 Q.1a, 2012–13 Q.2a, 2013–14 Q.1a, 2016–17 Q.7a, 2017–18 Q.7a, 2022–23 Q.4a, and 2023–24 Q.7a.
Internal Energy (U), First Law of Thermodynamics & Molar Specific Heats (Cv and Cp) DEFINITION

Definition of Internal Energy ($U$): The total intrinsic microscopic energy possessed by a system due to molecular translational, rotational, and vibrational kinetic energies plus intermolecular potential energies. $U$ is a state (point) function whose change $\Delta U = U_f - U_i$ depends solely on the initial and final equilibrium states and is independent of the path ($\oint dU = 0$); for an ideal gas, intermolecular forces vanish so $U = \frac{f}{2}nRT$ depends on temperature $T$ alone.
Definition of the First Law of Thermodynamics: A general statement of the Law of Conservation of Energy including thermal energy: when an amount of heat $\delta Q$ is supplied to a system capable of doing external work, the heat absorbed is equal to the sum of the increase in internal energy $dU$ of the system and the external work $\delta W = P\,dV$ done by the system on its surroundings ($\delta Q = dU + \delta W$).
Definitions of Molar Specific Heats ($C_v$ and $C_p$):
(1) Molar Specific Heat at Constant Volume ($C_v$): The quantity of heat required to raise the temperature of $1\text{ mole}$ of a gas through $1\text{ K}$ (or $1^\circ\text{C}$) while keeping its volume constant ($dV = 0 \implies \delta W = 0$, so $\delta Q_V = nC_v\,dT = dU$).
(2) Molar Specific Heat at Constant Pressure ($C_p$): The quantity of heat required to raise the temperature of $1\text{ mole}$ of a gas through $1\text{ K}$ while maintaining its pressure constant ($dP = 0$, so $\delta Q_P = nC_p\,dT = dU + P\,dV = dH$).

$$\delta Q = dU + \delta W = dU + P\,dV,\qquad C_v = \frac{1}{n}\left(\frac{\delta Q}{\partial T}\right)_V = \left(\frac{\partial U_m}{\partial T}\right)_V,\qquad C_p = \frac{1}{n}\left(\frac{\delta Q}{\partial T}\right)_P = \left(\frac{\partial H_m}{\partial T}\right)_P,\qquad C_p - C_v = R$$
Conditions: Why $C_p$ Is Always Greater Than $C_v$ ($C_p > C_v$): At constant volume, all supplied heat goes into raising internal energy ($dU$); at constant pressure, the gas expands upon heating and must absorb an additional amount of heat $P\,dV = R\,dT$ per mole to perform external expansion work against the surrounding pressure, yielding Mayer's relation $C_p - C_v = R$.
Exam Application: Directly asked in BUET PHY 121 2009–10 Q.1b, 2014–15 Q.6a, 2020–21 Q.7a, 2022–23 Q.6a, and 2024–25 Q.5a.
Six Fundamental Assumptions of Kinetic Theory & 5 Characteristics of Brownian Motion DEFINITION

Six Fundamental Assumptions (Postulates) of the Kinetic Theory of Gases:
(1) Particulate Nature & Random Motion: Every gas consists of an enormous number ($N \sim 10^{23}$) of identical, tiny, rigid spherical particles called molecules moving continuously in all directions with all possible velocities.
(2) Negligible Molecular Volume: The actual volume occupied by the gas molecules themselves is negligible compared to the total volume $V$ of the container.
(3) No Intermolecular Forces: Molecules exert no attractive or repulsive forces on one another except during collisions, moving in straight lines with constant velocity (free paths) between impacts.
(4) Perfectly Elastic Collisions: Collisions between molecules and with the container walls are perfectly elastic (conserving both total momentum and total kinetic energy).
(5) Negligible Collision Duration: The time spent during a molecular collision is negligible compared to the time of free flight between collisions.
(6) Kinetic Interpretation of Temperature: The average translational kinetic energy of a gas molecule is directly proportional to the absolute temperature $T$ of the gas ($\frac12 m\overline{v^2} = \frac32 k_B T$).
Definition & Five Characteristics of Brownian Motion: The continuous, rapid, irregular zig-zag motion of microscopic particles (e.g., pollen grains, smoke particles, gamboge) suspended in a liquid or gas, caused by unbalanced random impacts from surrounding fluid molecules. Five Characteristics: (1) Continuous, random, and eternal (never ceases); (2) Independent of external mechanical shaking or convection; (3) Smaller suspended particles move more rapidly because fewer simultaneous impacts hit them, creating larger statistical force imbalances; (4) Increases with higher temperature $T$ and lower fluid viscosity $\eta$ ($\overline{x^2} = \frac{RT}{3\pi N_A \eta r}t$); (5) Provides direct visual proof of the thermal agitation of molecules postulated by kinetic theory.

$$P = \frac{1}{3}\rho\overline{v^2},\qquad \langle\varepsilon_{\text{tr}}\rangle = \frac{1}{2}m\overline{v^2} = \frac{3}{2}k_B T,\qquad \overline{x^2} = \frac{RT}{3\pi N_A \eta r}\,t = 2Dt$$
Conditions: By measuring the mean-square Brownian displacement $\overline{x^2}$ of suspended spheres of known radius $r$ in a liquid of viscosity $\eta$ over time $t$, Jean Perrin experimentally determined Avogadro's number $N_A \approx 6.022\times 10^{23}\text{ mol}^{-1}$.
Exam Application: Directly asked in BUET PHY 121 2011–12 Q.1a, 2014–15 Q.4c, 2018–19 Q.1a/1c, 2021–22 Q.7a, 2022–23 Q.5a, and 2024–25 Q.4a.
Degrees of Freedom, Equipartition of Energy & Maxwell Speed Distribution Curve Features DEFINITION

Definition of Degrees of Freedom ($f$): The total number of independent coordinates (or independent quadratic energy modes) required to specify the configuration and dynamical state of a molecule ($f = 3N - k$ for a rigid molecule of $N$ atoms with $k$ bond constraints):
(1) Monatomic gas ($\text{He, Ne, Ar}$, $N=1, k=0$): $f = 3$ (3 translational only; $U = \frac32 RT, C_v = \frac32 R, \gamma = \frac53 = 1.67$).
(2) Diatomic / Linear polyatomic gas ($\text{H}_2, \text{O}_2, \text{N}_2, \text{CO}_2$, $k=1$ for $N=2$): $f = 3_{\text{tr}} + 2_{\text{rot}} = 5$ at room temperature ($C_v = \frac52 R, \gamma = \frac75 = 1.40$); at high $T$, $(3N-5)$ vibrational modes activate, each adding $2$ degrees of freedom (1 kinetic + 1 potential, giving $f=7$ and $\gamma = \frac97 = 1.29$ for vibrating diatomic).
(3) Non-linear polyatomic gas ($\text{H}_2\text{O}, \text{NH}_3, \text{CH}_4$, $k=3$): $f = 3_{\text{tr}} + 3_{\text{rot}} = 6$ rigid ($C_v = 3R, \gamma = \frac43 = 1.33$), plus $2(3N-6)$ vibrational terms at high $T$.
Law of Equipartition of Energy: For a classical system in thermal equilibrium at absolute temperature $T$, the total energy is distributed equally among all degrees of freedom, with each independent quadratic term in the Hamiltonian contributing an average energy of $\frac12 k_B T$ per molecule (or $\frac12 RT$ per mole).
Three Key Features of the Maxwell Speed Distribution Curve $f(v)$:
(1) Peak of the Curve: Occurs at the most probable speed $v_p = \sqrt{\frac{2k_B T}{m}} = \sqrt{\frac{2RT}{M}}$ (the speed possessed by the maximum fraction of molecules, where $\frac{df(v)}{dv} = 0$).
(2) Area Under the Curve: Equals $\int_0^\infty f(v)\,dv = \int_0^\infty \frac{1}{N}\frac{dN}{dv}\,dv = 1$ (total probability = $100\%$ of molecules, or $N$ if $dN/dv$ is plotted), which remains invariant at all temperatures.
(3) Why the Curve Flattens & Shifts Right as $T$ Increases: Raising $T$ increases molecular speeds ($v_p \propto \sqrt{T}$), shifting the peak to the right and broadening the speed range; since the total area under the curve must remain constant at $1$, the peak height $f(v_p) \propto T^{-1/2}$ must decrease (flatten).

$$f = 3N - k,\qquad \langle\varepsilon_i\rangle = \frac{1}{2}k_B T,\qquad \gamma = 1 + \frac{2}{f},\qquad v_p = \sqrt{\frac{2k_B T}{m}},\qquad \int_0^\infty f(v)\,dv = 1$$
Conditions: Notice the strict ordering of the three characteristic speeds at any temperature $T$: $v_p : \bar{v} : v_{\text{rms}} = \sqrt{2} : \sqrt{\frac{8}{\pi}} : \sqrt{3} = 1 : 1.128 : 1.224$ ($v_{\text{rms}} > \bar{v} > v_p$).
Exam Application: Directly asked in BUET PHY 121 2012–13 Q.1a, 2014–15 Q.4a/4b, 2015–16 Q.8b, 2019–20 Q.5a, 2022–23 Q.5b, and 2023–24 Q.7b.
Van der Waals Two Corrections, Reversible vs Irreversible Processes & Second Law Statements DEFINITION

Two Physical Considerations Behind Van der Waals Corrections: Real gases deviate from $PV = nRT$ at high pressures and low temperatures because two assumptions of ideal kinetic theory break down:
(1) Finite Volume of Molecules (Co-volume Correction $b$): Gas molecules are not point masses; each spherical molecule of radius $r$ (diameter $d = 2r$) prevents the center of any other molecule from approaching closer than $d$, excluding a sphere of volume $\frac43\pi d^3 = 8V_{\text{mol}}$ per collision pair, or $4V_{\text{mol}}$ per molecule. Thus 1 mole excludes volume $b = 4N_A(\frac43\pi r^3) = 4\times(\text{actual molecular volume})$, leaving compressible free volume $V_{\text{ideal}} = V - nb$.
(2) Intermolecular Attractive Forces (Internal Pressure Correction $a/V^2$): While a molecule in the interior is pulled equally in all directions, a molecule about to strike the wall experiences a net inward cohesive pull proportional to molecular density $(n/V)$, and the number of molecules striking unit wall area per second is also proportional to $(n/V)$. Thus the measured wall pressure $P$ is reduced by $\Delta P = \frac{n^2 a}{V^2}$, so $P_{\text{ideal}} = P + \frac{n^2 a}{V^2}$.
Definitions of Reversible & Irreversible Processes: A reversible process is an idealized quasi-static process carried out infinitely slowly through continuous equilibrium states in the complete absence of friction, viscosity, or finite temperature gradients, so both system and surroundings can be restored to their exact initial states without leaving any net change in the universe. An irreversible process is any real natural process proceeding at a finite rate or involving dissipative effects (free expansion, friction, heat conduction across finite $\Delta T$), generating entropy ($\Delta S_{\text{univ}} > 0$).
Kelvin–Planck & Clausius Statements of the Second Law:
(1) Kelvin–Planck Statement: It is impossible to construct a cyclic heat engine whose sole effect is to extract heat from a single thermal reservoir and convert it completely into an equivalent amount of mechanical work ($Q_2 > 0$ is mandatory, so $\eta < 100\%$).
(2) Clausius Statement: It is impossible for any self-acting cyclic machine to transfer heat from a body at a lower temperature to a body at a higher temperature without the aid of an external agency (external work input $W > 0$).

$$V_{\text{ideal}} = V - nb,\quad b = 4N_A\!\left(\frac{4}{3}\pi r^3\right) = 4V_{\text{mol}};\qquad P_{\text{ideal}} = P + \frac{n^2 a}{V^2} \implies \left(P + \frac{n^2 a}{V^2}\right)(V - nb) = nRT$$
Conditions: The Kelvin–Planck and Clausius statements are logically equivalent: if a device violated the Clausius statement, coupling it with a normal heat engine would violate the Kelvin–Planck statement, and vice versa.
Exam Application: Directly asked in BUET PHY 121 2009–10 Q.2a, 2011–12 Q.2a, 2013–14 Q.2a, 2016–17 Q.7b, 2017–18 Q.7a, 2019–20 Q.6a, and 2023–24 Q.8a.
Carnot Efficiency: Source vs Sink Temperature Modification & Cascade of Carnot Cycles DEFINITION

Source vs Sink Temperature Modification in a Carnot Engine: To increase the efficiency $\eta = 1 - \frac{T_2}{T_1}$ of a Carnot engine operating between source $T_1$ and sink $T_2$ ($T_1 > T_2 > 0$) by a given temperature change $\Delta T > 0$:
(1) Raising the source temperature by $\Delta T$ gives $\eta_1 = 1 - \frac{T_2}{T_1 + \Delta T} = \frac{T_1 - T_2 + \Delta T}{T_1 + \Delta T}$.
(2) Lowering the sink temperature by $\Delta T$ gives $\eta_2 = 1 - \frac{T_2 - \Delta T}{T_1} = \frac{T_1 - T_2 + \Delta T}{T_1}$.
Since both fractions have the same positive numerator $(T_1 - T_2 + \Delta T)$ while $\eta_2$ has the smaller denominator $T_1 < T_1 + \Delta T$, we have $\eta_2 > \eta_1$ (with $\eta_2 - \eta_1 = \frac{(T_1 + \Delta T - T_2)\Delta T}{T_1(T_1 + \Delta T)} > 0$). Thus decreasing the sink temperature $T_2$ is always more effective than increasing the source temperature $T_1$ by the same amount!
Representing Any Reversible Cycle as a Cascade of Carnot Cycles: Any closed reversible cycle $ABA$ on a $P$–$V$ diagram can be divided by a closely spaced family of reversible adiabats into a large number of narrow strips, and the top and bottom boundary arcs of each strip approximated by infinitesimal isothermal steps at $T_{1,i}$ and $T_{2,i}$. Every elementary strip forms a miniature Carnot cycle satisfying $\frac{\delta Q_{1,i}}{T_{1,i}} + \frac{\delta Q_{2,i}}{T_{2,i}} = 0$, while adjacent interior adiabatic segments are traversed twice in opposite directions and cancel out completely.

$$\eta_1 = 1 - \frac{T_2}{T_1 + \Delta T},\qquad \eta_2 = 1 - \frac{T_2 - \Delta T}{T_1},\qquad \eta_2 - \eta_1 = \frac{(T_1 + \Delta T - T_2)\Delta T}{T_1(T_1 + \Delta T)} > 0 \implies \eta_2 > \eta_1;\qquad \sum_{i} \frac{\delta Q_i}{T_i} = \oint_{\text{rev}}\frac{\delta Q}{T} = 0$$
Conditions: In the limit of infinitely many strips, summing over all elementary cycles yields Clausius's Theorem $\oint_{\text{rev}} \frac{\delta Q}{T} = 0$, establishing that $dS = \frac{\delta Q_{\text{rev}}}{T}$ is an exact differential!
Exam Application: Directly asked in BUET PHY 121 2011–12 Q.3a, 2012–13 Q.2b, 2014–15 Q.5b, 2015–16 Q.7a, 2018–19 Q.2b, and 2021–22 Q.8a.
Physical Significance of Entropy, T–S Diagram & Clausius–Clapeyron Pressure Cooker / Regelation Analysis DEFINITION

Physical Significance of Entropy ($S$):
(1) Measure of Unavailable Energy: Entropy quantifies the degradation of thermal energy into random microscopic motion unavailable for doing useful mechanical work ($W_{\text{unavailable}} = T_0\Delta S_{\text{univ}}$).
(2) Measure of Molecular Disorder (Boltzmann's Principle): $S = k_B\ln W$, where $W$ is the thermodynamic probability (number of accessible microstates of a macrostate); melting, vaporization, and gas expansion all increase spatial/velocity disorder ($W$ increases $\implies \Delta S > 0$).
(3) Why the Entropy of the Universe Always Increases: Because all spontaneous natural processes (heat flow across finite $\Delta T$, free expansion, friction, diffusion) are irreversible, Clausius's inequality $\oint \frac{\delta Q}{T} < 0$ implies $\Delta S_{\text{univ}} = \Delta S_{\text{sys}} + \Delta S_{\text{surr}} > 0$ for every natural process.
Graphical Notation ($T$–$S$ Temperature–Entropy Diagram): On a $T$–$S$ diagram ($T$ on vertical axis, $S$ on horizontal axis), the area under any reversible path equals the heat transferred ($Q_{\text{rev}} = \int T\,dS$). A reversible isotherm is a horizontal segment ($dT=0$), a reversible adiabat (isentropic process) is a vertical segment ($dS=0$), and a Carnot cycle is a simple rectangle of area $W = Q_1 - Q_2 = (T_1 - T_2)(S_2 - S_1)$ and efficiency $\eta = \frac{T_1 - T_2}{T_1}$.
Physical Significance of Clausius–Clapeyron Equation ($\frac{dP}{dT} = \frac{L}{T(v_2 - v_1)}$):
(1) Why Food Cooks Faster in a Pressure Cooker (Boiling Point): For vaporization ($v_2 = v_{\text{steam}} \gg v_1 = v_{\text{water}}$), $v_2 - v_1 > 0 \implies \frac{dP}{dT} > 0$. Sealing steam inside a pressure cooker raises pressure to $\sim 2\text{ atm}$, elevating water's boiling point from $100^\circ\text{C}$ to $\approx 120^\circ\text{C}$. Food cooks rapidly because chemical cooking reaction rates roughly double per $10^\circ\text{C}$ rise and condensing steam transfers a huge latent heat of $L_v \approx 540\text{ cal/g}$ ($2.26\times 10^6\text{ J/kg}$).
(2) Melting Point & Regelation of Ice: Whereas most solids expand on melting ($v_2 > v_1 \implies dP/dT > 0$), ice contracts when melting into liquid water ($v_2 = 1.000\text{ cm}^3/\text{g} < v_1 = 1.091\text{ cm}^3/\text{g} \implies v_2 - v_1 < 0 \implies \frac{dP}{dT} < 0$, $\approx -0.0072^\circ\text{C/atm}$). Thus high pressure lowers the melting point of ice, causing ice under a weighted wire or skate blade to melt and refreeze (regelation) once pressure is removed.

$$dS = \frac{\delta Q_{\text{rev}}}{T},\qquad S = k_B\ln W,\qquad \Delta S_{\text{univ}} = \Delta S_{\text{sys}} + \Delta S_{\text{surr}} \ge 0,\qquad W_{\text{Carnot}} = \oint T\,dS = (T_1 - T_2)(S_2 - S_1),\qquad \frac{dP}{dT} = \frac{L}{T(v_2 - v_1)}$$
Conditions: At high altitudes (mountains), atmospheric pressure is lower than $1\text{ atm}$, so water boils below $100^\circ\text{C}$ ($dP/dT > 0$) and cooking in an open pot takes much longer unless a pressure cooker is used.
Exam Application: Directly asked in BUET PHY 121 2011–12 Q.3a, 2012–13 Q.3a, 2014–15 Q.6c, 2015–16 Q.7a, 2017–18 Q.8a, 2018–19 Q.3a, 2019–20 Q.4a, 2020–21 Q.8a, 2022–23 Q.4b, and 2024–25 Q.6c.
C programming: definitionsFreq: 5/50/5
Storage Classes in C: auto, register, static, extern DEFINITION

Conditions: Applying static to a global variable or function restricts its visibility to the current translation unit (.c file).
Exam Application: Classic output-tracing question: a static local variable inside a function is initialised ONLY ONCE and remembers its value across recursive or repeated calls.
Structure vs Union, Call by Value vs Call by Address, malloc vs calloc DEFINITION
  • struct vs union: A struct allocates separate memory for every member (size $\ge \sum$ member sizes with padding), whereas a union shares a single memory block among all members (size $= \max$ member size); only one union member holds a valid value at a time.
  • malloc vs calloc: malloc(size) allocates a single uninitialised block (contains garbage); calloc(n, elem_size) allocates $n$ elements and zero-initialises all bits to 0.
  • Dangling Pointer vs Memory Leak: A dangling pointer points to memory that has already been freed or gone out of scope; a memory leak occurs when heap memory allocated via malloc/new loses all pointers to it without being freed.
Conditions: Standard short-note / comparison questions in CSE 109 Section A.
Exam Application: After calling free(p), set p = NULL immediately to prevent dangling-pointer dereference or double-free bugs.
Bitwise Logical vs Arithmetic Shifts, Preprocessor Macro Rule & Pointer/File Traps DEFINITION
  • Logical vs Arithmetic Right Shift (K&R Ch. 2, Sec. 2.9; Schildt Ch. 11): Casting to unsigned int forces >> to pad vacated MSBs with 0s (logical shift, essential for rightrot, leftRotate, and ((unsigned int)x >> 31) & 1). On signed int, int mask = x >> 31; replicates the sign bit (0 if $xge 0$, -1 if $x<0$), enabling branchless absoluteVal(x) = (x + mask) ^ mask.
  • Preprocessor Macro Golden Rule (Sec. 1.6): Always parenthesize every parameter (x) AND the entire macro expansion ((...)). Unparenthesized cube(x+1) expands to x + 1 * x + 1 * x + 1, and unparenthesized i >= min in RANGE(x || y, 0, 1) parses as x || (y >= 0) because >= binds tighter than ||.
  • Array & File I/O Edge Cases (Sec. 3.2.9 & 7.1): In adjacent-difference loops (max_dist, 2020–21 Q3b), initialize max_d = 0 and start at i = 1 to avoid out-of-bounds arr[-1]. In byte-offset file reversal (fseek / ftell, 2010–11 Q8a), always use binary modes "rb" and "wb".
Conditions: Never pass expressions with side effects like i++ into a macro such as square(i++) or abs(i++) because the argument is evaluated multiple times.
Exam Application: Directly tested in BUET CSE 109 2010–11 Q1c/Q8a, 2013–14 Q5a,b, 2014–15 Q5c, 2015–16 Q7b, 2017–18 Q1a, 2018–19 Q1a, 2019–20 Q2b, 2020–21 Q3b/Q7c, 2023–24 Q5b, and 2024–25 Q8a,b.
C Theory One-Liners, Variable Scope/Lifetime Table, Struct vs Union & Macro vs Function (Rev. Guide Sec. 5.3, 5.6, 6.2 & 8.3) DEFINITION

8.3 C Programming Theory One-Liners (Quick Definitions):

  • Compiler vs Interpreter: Compiler translates the whole program, then runs. Interpreter translates and executes line by line.
  • Von Neumann Architecture: Input, Output, Memory (stores program + data together), CPU = CU (Control Unit) + ALU (Arithmetic Logic Unit) + registers.
  • Symbolic Constant: #define NAME value (preprocessed text replacement, no ; at the end).
  • Short Circuit Evaluation: && / || skip evaluating the right-hand operand as soon as the overall truth value is known.
  • Call by Value vs Call by Reference: Passing a copy of the value vs passing the address/alias (via C pointers int *p or C++ references int &r).
  • Function Prototype: Declaration before main() that lets the compiler type-check function calls before the full function definition appears.
  • Static Local Variable: Initialised to 0 only once and retains its value between function calls; lifetime is the whole program, scope is the local block.
  • Recursion vs Loop: Recursion is shorter/cleaner for tree/divide-and-conquer problems, but uses call-stack frames and is slower than iteration.
  • Mutual Recursion: Function A calls B and B calls A (requires a forward function prototype before the first function).
  • Memory Leak vs Dangling Pointer: Memory leak = heap block allocated with malloc/new but never freed and its address is lost. Dangling pointer = pointer that still points to already-freed or out-of-scope dead memory.
  • Null Character ('\0'): ASCII 0 byte that marks the end of a C string (a string of length $L$ requires $L + 1$ bytes).
  • Array of Pointers vs 2D Array: char *list[100] allocates exact row sizes on the heap and swaps strings in $O(1)$ by swapping pointers; char names[100][31] uses fixed row width and requires strcpy to swap.
  • Bit-Field: Struct member with an explicit bit width (e.g. unsigned day : 5;); cannot take its address (&), cannot form arrays of bit-fields, and cannot pass directly to scanf.
  • Text vs Binary File: Text file stores human-readable characters with newline translation; binary file stores raw memory bytes (exact copy via fread/fwrite).
  • Binary Search Tree (BST): Every node satisfies $\text{left} < \text{node} < \text{right}$; average search time is $O(\log_2 n)$.
  • Can We Run C Without an Editor? Yes — only a compiler is required (e.g. gcc a.c -o a).
KindScopeLifetimeDefaultKeyword
localblockblockgarbage(auto)
static localblockwhole program0static
globalfile (below it)whole program0–
externrefers to global elsewhere––extern

Scope Types: block, function (goto labels), file, function-prototype. Declaration vs Definition: Declaration allocates no memory (extern int x;); definition allocates memory (int x;).

Macro vs Function (Sec. 5.6): Macro = preprocessor text replacement, faster (no call overhead), no type checking, code size grows, arguments may be evaluated multiple times (e.g. SQ(i++)). Function = real call, type-checked, arguments evaluated once, single copy in memory.

Conditions: CSE 109 Complete Revision Guide, Section 5.3 (Functions & Scope), Section 5.6 (Macro vs function), Section 6.2 (Struct/Union/Bit-field) & Section 8.3 (Theory one-liners), Pages 12–15, 19–20.
Exam Application: Directly answers 1-to-2 mark short theory questions across all 14 BUET CSE 109 past papers.
Output-Tracing Rules (1–28), Find-the-Error Diagnostic Rules & Two's Complement Theory (Rev. Guide Sec. 1.1, 1.2, 3.2, 8.1 & 8.2) RULE

Why Two's Complement Is Used in Modern Computers (Sec. 3.2):

  1. Single Zero: Only one representation of zero ($0000\,0000_2$), unlike sign-magnitude and one's complement which have $+0$ and $-0$.
  2. Unified Addition & Subtraction: Subtraction $A - B$ is computed as $A + (\text{two's complement of } B)$ dropping the carry out, so the ALU needs no separate subtractor circuit.
  3. No End-Around Carry: Unlike one's complement, carry out of the MSB is simply discarded. Shortcut: $\sim x = -x - 1$.

Key Output-Tracing Laws (Sec. 8.1):

  • Integer Division & Promotion: float f = 1/2; evaluates 1/2 = 0 first, then casts to 0.000000. Unsigned overflow wraps modulo $2^{32}$ before any cast.
  • Short-Circuit & Ternary Loops: In a && b++, if a == 0, b is untouched. In while(i+1 ? --i : j++), evaluate the ternary condition every iteration.
  • Shadowing & Global Fallback: An inner-block declaration int i = 2; inside a loop hides the loop counter i; an undeclared local identifier resolves to the global variable.
  • Pointers & 3D Indexing: Printing a pointer with %d outputs $\text{base} + \text{index} \times \text{sizeof(type)}$. For a[X][Y][Z], flat index $= i \cdot (YZ) + j \cdot Z + k$.
  • C++ Object Lifecycle Tracing: new A[5] + delete[] prints 5 constructor and 5 destructor lines. Derived object runs Base constructor first, Derived destructor first. Passing an object by value prints a Copy Constructor line on entry and a Destructor line at function return. In cout << f(), prints inside f() appear before its returned value. Default argument b = x binds to the current value of global x.
Conditions: CSE 109 Complete Revision Guide, Section 3.2 (Two's complement), Section 8.1 (Output-tracing rules) & Section 8.2 (Find-the-error checklist), Pages 8, 19.
Exam Application: Apply directly when tracing C/C++ output tables and explaining why two's complement is preferred over one's complement.
C++ and object-oriented programming: definitionsFreq: 5/50/4
Four Pillars of OOP: Encapsulation, Abstraction, Inheritance & Polymorphism DEFINITION
  • Encapsulation & Data Hiding: Bundling data members and member functions inside a class while restricting direct external access via private/protected specifiers.
  • Compile-Time vs Run-Time Polymorphism: Compile-time (early/static binding) is achieved via function overloading and operator overloading. Run-time (late/dynamic binding) is achieved via inheritance and virtual functions invoked through a base-class pointer or reference using the vtable / vptr mechanism.
Conditions: Operators that CANNOT be overloaded in C++: ::, ., .*, ?:, and sizeof.
Exam Application: Operators that MUST be overloaded as non-static member functions (cannot be friend): =, (), [], and ->.
Shallow vs Deep Copy, Virtual Destructor, Abstract Class & Diamond Problem DEFINITION
  • Shallow vs Deep Copy: Default compiler copy copies member values bit-by-bit (sharing the same heap pointer $\Rightarrow$ double-free crash on destruction). Deep copy allocates a fresh heap buffer and copies the pointed-to contents.
  • Pure Virtual Function & Abstract Class: A class with at least one pure virtual function (virtual void draw() = 0;) cannot be instantiated directly, though base pointers Shape *p can point to derived objects.
  • Diamond Problem & Virtual Base Class: When D inherits from both B and C which share base A, D gets two copies of A unless B and C inherit virtually (class B : virtual public A).
Conditions: Rule of Three: if a class manages raw heap memory, it needs a custom Destructor, Copy Constructor, and Copy Assignment Operator.
Exam Application: When asked "Why is a virtual destructor needed?", explain that deleting a derived object via Base *p = new Derived(); delete p; leaks Derived resources unless ~Base() is declared virtual.
The Rule of Three, Multi-Class Friend Forward Declarations & Operator Overloading (Schildt Ch. 4–10) DEFINITION
  • Why the Rule of Three Is Mandatory (Schildt Ch. 4–10, Sec. 8.1): When a class contains a raw pointer (char *pStr or int *x), default bitwise (shallow) copy causes both objects to point to the same heap block. When one object goes out of scope, its destructor frees that memory, leaving the other object with a dangling pointer and causing a double-free crash. Defining a Deep Copy Constructor, Destructor, and Overloaded operator= (with if (this != &rhs) guard) solves this completely.
  • Forward Declaration for Two-Class Friend Functions (Sec. 8.3.6): For a friend function shared across two classes (friend int compare(limousine a, corolla b);, 2010–11 Q7b), declare class corolla; above class limousine so the compiler knows corolla is a class type.
  • Postfix Dummy int Parameter & 2D Object Arrays (Sec. 8.3.4, 8.3.6): Postfix Person operator++(int) takes a dummy int argument, saves Person temp = *this;, increments age++, and returns temp. A 2D object array box boxes[4][2] initializes each element via { box(1,2,3), box(4,5,6) }.
Conditions: Copy constructor parameter MUST be a reference (const String &other); passing by value would invoke the copy constructor itself in infinite recursion.
Exam Application: Tested in BUET CSE 109 2010–11 Q5a/Q6a/Q7b, 2012–13 Q7a/Q8a, 2013–14 Q8, 2014–15 Q4, 2015–16 Q7c/Q8a, 2016–17 Q4a,b, 2017–18 Q8a, 2018–19 Q7a,b, 2019–20 Q1a/Q4b, 2020–21 Q8a, 2021–22 Q12b,c/Q13c, 2023–24 Q7b/Q8a, and 2024–25 Q4b.
Pointer vs Reference, new vs malloc, Overloading Ambiguity, Inline & C++ Theory One-Liners (Rev. Guide Sec. 7.1–7.6 & 8.3) DEFINITION
  • Pointer vs Reference (Sec. 7.1): A reference (int &r = x;) is an alias that must be initialised when declared, can never be NULL, cannot be re-seated to another variable, and needs no * to dereference. p2 = p1 copies an address; r2 = r1 copies the object's contents.
  • new vs malloc (Sec. 7.1): new requires no sizeof or type cast, automatically calls the constructor (and delete calls the destructor), can initialise values (new int(5)), can be overloaded per class, and is type-safe.
  • Function Overloading Ambiguity Traps (Sec. 7.1):
    1. f(float) and f(double) called with f(10) (int promotes equally to float or double).
    2. g(int) and g(int, int = 0) called with g(1) (default argument creates two 1-arg matches).
    3. h(int) and h(int &) called with h(x) (lvalue matches both value and reference).
    Note: Return type alone can never distinguish overloaded functions.
  • inline Functions (Sec. 7.1 & 8.3): Pastes the function body at the call site to eliminate call/return stack overhead; unsuitable for large, recursive, or loop-containing functions. Member functions defined inside a class declaration are automatically inline.
  • Object Destruction Order & Private Constructors (Sec. 7.3): Objects are destroyed in reverse order of creation (Base constructs first, Derived constructs second $\to$ Derived destructs first, Base destructs last). A private constructor restricts instantiation to inside the class or its friends (used in Factory / Singleton patterns).
  • Operator Overloading Restrictions (Sec. 7.4): Cannot invent new operator symbols; cannot change precedence, associativity, or arity; cannot overload ., ::, .*, ?:, or sizeof; at least one operand must be a user-defined type; =, (), [], and -> must be non-static member functions; overloaded operators cannot have default arguments (except operator()).
  • Overloading vs Overriding & Virtual Dispatch (Sec. 7.6): Overloading = same function name with different parameter lists in the same scope (compile-time binding). Overriding = derived class redefines a virtual base function with the exact same signature (run-time binding via vtable). Calling through a base pointer/reference runs the object's version if virtual and the pointer type's version if non-virtual; passing a derived object by value to a base parameter slices off derived members and runs the base version.
  • C++ Theory One-Liners (Sec. 8.3):
    • OOP Pillars (A PIE): Abstraction, Polymorphism, Inheritance, Encapsulation (private data + public functions).
    • Namespace: Avoids identifier name clashes; the C++ standard library lives in std.
    • Abstract Class: Contains at least one pure virtual function (virtual double area() = 0;); cannot instantiate objects directly.
    • Virtual Destructor: Required in a polymorphic base class so delete basePtr; invokes the derived destructor before the base destructor (preventing memory leaks).
    • Static Member & Friend: Static data member has one shared copy per class (defined outside via int Str::count = 0;); a static member function has no this pointer and cannot access non-static members. A friend function/class is a non-member granted access to private and protected members.
Conditions: CSE 109 Complete Revision Guide, Sections 7.1–7.6 (C++ and Object Oriented Programming) & Section 8.3 (Theory one-liners), Pages 16–20.
Exam Application: Covers all C++ short-note and theory questions: pointer vs reference, new vs malloc, overloading ambiguity, inline, Rule of Three, virtual dispatch, slicing, and OOP pillars.
